CHK-001 — Suitability completeness against client information
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 11 requirement(s). 8 raised a concern (G1.2, G1.3, G1.4, G1.6, G1.7, G1.8, G1.10, G1.11). The fact find records highest rate of income tax paid as 0%, which would imply the client pays no income tax, yet the report's cashflow section (P22) describes income from state pension (~£230/week), employment (~£300/week net) and Sea Cadets (£20/week) plus spouse income — this combination likely places the client above the personal allowance, creating a tension with the 0% tax rate recorded in the fact find. However, the 0% field may reflect gross basic annual income being blank rather than a confirmed zero-rate taxpayer. The report does not state the client's income tax band explicitly, so no direct band mismatch can be identified between the report and the evidence, but the tax band is absent from both the canonical model and the fact find in a usable form. No MPAA trigger event is evidenced in any provider document or fact find, consistent with the report's assertion of mpaaTriggered = false. 4 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G1.2] Tax status stated in the report is consistent with the evidence
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report asserts (page 20) that the client has not triggered the MPAA and does not have a lower annual allowance. No income tax band, annual allowance figure or territorial profile is stated in the canonical model for this client. The report also references the standard Annual Allowance of £60,000 for 2024/2025 and lump sum allowance of £268,275 in generic legislative passages.
    File:     The fact find (P17) records the client as employed (HGV Driver, CDH Recruitment) with a highest rate of income tax paid of 0% and a net basic monthly income of £1,200 (P24). The provider documents (P1 Standard Life, P4 Aviva, P8 Scottish Widows) confirm no lifetime allowance protection is held. No document in the pack records that the MPAA has been triggered. No fact find field or provider document records a Money Purchase Annual Allowance trigger event. The Aviva pension input period table (P4) shows £0 contributions in recent tax years (2021/22 through 2025/26) apart from £756.14 in 2020/21, with no drawdown income payments recorded.
    Discrepancies:
      - The fact find records highest rate of income tax paid as 0%, which would imply the client pays no income tax, yet the report's cashflow section (P22) describes income from state pension (~£230/week), employment (~£300/week net) and Sea Cadets (£20/week) plus spouse income — this combination likely places the client above the personal allowance, creating a tension with the 0% tax rate recorded in the fact find. However, the 0% field may reflect gross basic annual income being blank rather than a confirmed zero-rate taxpayer.
      - The report does not state the client's income tax band explicitly, so no direct band mismatch can be identified between the report and the evidence, but the tax band is absent from both the canonical model and the fact find in a usable form.
      - No MPAA trigger event is evidenced in any provider document or fact find, consistent with the report's assertion of mpaaTriggered = false.
      - The fact find field 'Highest rate of income tax paid (%)' shows 0, while the gross basic annual income field is blank (P17/P24), meaning the income tax position cannot be positively confirmed from the fact find alone.
    The report's core MPAA assertion (not triggered) is consistent with the available evidence: no provider document records a drawdown trigger, the Aviva pension input period shows no income withdrawals, and no fact find entry indicates a MPAA event. That aspect is corroborated.
    
    However, the report relies on drawdown and tax-free cash mechanics that depend on the client's income tax position (marginal rate applies to drawdown withdrawals per P19). The fact find records 0% as the highest rate of income tax paid (P17), yet the suitability report describes weekly income from state pension (£230), employment (£300 net) and Sea Cadets (£20), plus spouse income of £1,100/month — an income profile that would ordinarily exceed the personal allowance and attract basic-rate tax at minimum. This creates a material gap: the tax band is not confirmed in the report or reliably established in the fact find, yet drawdown income will be taxed at the client's marginal rate.
    
    The territorial profile is also absent, which matters because Scottish income tax bands differ from rUK bands. Without it, a band mismatch cannot be ruled out.
    
    The generic legislative passages about the £60,000 annual allowance and £268,275 lump sum allowance (P19) are not applied as client-specific figures and are covered by the legislative-statement guard — no finding arises from them.
    
    The 0% income tax rate in the fact find, set against the income described in the report, is a material gap in the recorded tax position that could affect the tax treatment of drawdown withdrawals, which are a core part of the recommendation. This meets the 'tax treatment asserted without a recorded tax position' threshold in the evidence-absent guard.

  [G1.3] Employment details are consistent with the evidence
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report states the client (JS) is employed full-time as an HGV driver through an agency, earning approximately £300 per week net. A secondary income of £20 per week comes from a volunteer role with the Sea Cadets. The client intends to retire within two years (inferred retirement date 2027-10-14). Kim (spouse) intends to work until age 66, with an inferred retirement date of 2026-10-14.
    File:     The fact find (P5) records the client as Employed, occupation HGV Driver, employer CDH Recruitment. The fact find income table (P13) records basic income as Employed, CDH Recruitment, Monthly, net £1,200.00. The cashflow model (P15) states the client receives £300 per week as an HGV driver and £20 per week helping with the sea cadets. The baseline scenario cashflow (P3) shows employment income of £16,640 for JS and £13,200 combined. The risk profile report (P19) notes an investment period of 2 years. No fact-find record of the Sea Cadets volunteer role as a formal employment entry is visible, though P15 corroborates the £20/week figure. No fact-find entry records Kim's intended retirement age of 66 or a retirement date.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states the employer is 'agency' but the fact find (P13/P5) names the employer as 'CDH Recruitment' — a specific named employer, not a generic agency description.
      - The fact find (P13) records net basic monthly income of £1,200.00, which annualises to £14,400 net per year. The report states £300 per week net, which annualises to approximately £15,600 net per year. These do not reconcile (difference of approximately £1,200 per year / £100 per month).
      - The cashflow baseline (P3) records JS employment income as £16,640, which is consistent with £320/week gross (52 × £320) rather than £300/week net, adding further ambiguity about whether £300 is gross or net and which figure underpins the model.
      - The intended retirement date of 2027-10-14 (inferred as 'within two years' from report date 14/10/2025) is not corroborated by any fact-find or supporting document field for JS.
      - Kim's intended retirement age of 66 and inferred retirement date of 2026-10-14 are not corroborated by any fact-find or supporting document entry in the pack.
      - The Sea Cadets volunteer role producing £20/week is corroborated in the cashflow assumptions (P15) but does not appear as a formal employment or income entry in the fact find income table (P13), which shows only the CDH Recruitment basic income and state pension.
    There are two material discrepancies. First, the report describes the employer as 'agency' whereas the fact find clearly names 'CDH Recruitment' — this is a factual mismatch, though it may be that CDH Recruitment is an agency; however, the report presents this as an unnamed agency rather than the named employer in the file. Second, and more material, the fact find records net basic monthly income of £1,200 (£14,400 p.a.) whereas the report asserts £300 per week net (approximately £15,600 p.a.), a discrepancy of approximately £100/month or £1,200/year. The cashflow model uses £16,640 employment income for JS, which does not match either the fact-find figure or the report's stated £300/week net figure, suggesting a third inconsistent value is embedded in the model. Employment income underpins the affordability assessment and cashflow projections; the guard ('Evidence absent: Potential Concern where employment income underpins affordability') therefore applies, and the mismatch between fact-find income and report-stated income is a material concern. The £20/week Sea Cadets figure is corroborated in P15 (cashflow assumptions) but absent from the fact-find income table, so it is unsupported by a formal client data source. Kim's intended retirement age of 66 is not corroborated by any evidence in the pack. The retirement date discrepancy (2026 vs 2027 for Kim vs JS) is an extraction inference issue but the underlying age-66 retirement assertion for Kim has no evidential support. The employer name discrepancy ('agency' vs 'CDH Recruitment') is lower severity but still a factual inconsistency. The income figure mismatch (£1,200/month in fact find vs £300/week in report vs £16,640 in cashflow model) is the most material finding as employment income directly underpins affordability.

  [G1.4] Income and expenditure figures are consistent with the evidence
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   C1 (John): HGV driver £300/week net, Sea Cadets £20/week net, State Pension £230/week (current). C2 (Kim): Employment £1,100/month net, State Pension £230/week (future, from ~2026). Household expenditure £1,700/month. Derived monthly totals: C1 HGV £1,300/month, Sea Cadets £86.67/month, State Pension £1,000/month; C2 employment £1,100/month. Total monthly income £4,486.67, surplus £2,786.67. Assets £6,000 cash, no liabilities.
    File:     Fact find [P10] shows income: Basic Income (employed, CDH Recruitment) £1,200/month net and State Pension £230/month net, totalling £1,430/month. [P20] shows net basic monthly income £1,200.00. [P17] (suitability report narrative) states £300/week HGV, £20/week Sea Cadets, £1,100/month Kim, £230/week State Pension. [P9] (cashflow section of suitability report) states 'You receive state pension income of £11,973 per annum' and 'Kim receives £1,100 per month'. Fact find [P1] shows total monthly household expenditure £1,718 and total net monthly income £1,430. [P13] confirms cash savings £6,000. [P16] confirms household income need £1,700/month net at retirement.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states C1 HGV income is £300/week net (£1,300/month equivalent). Fact find [P10] records employed income as £1,200/month net — a difference of £100/month (7.7%), exceeding 5% tolerance.
      - Report derives C1 monthly HGV equivalent as £1,300/month (£300 x 52/12). Fact find total net monthly income is only £1,430, which is consistent with £1,200 + £230 (State Pension monthly as recorded in fact find) but not with the report's higher income figures.
      - Fact find [P10] records State Pension as £230/month net, whereas the report states £230/week (£1,000/month equivalent) — a very large discrepancy: £230/month vs £1,000/month.
      - Cashflow section [P9] states state pension income of £11,973 per annum (£230/week x 52 = £11,960, approximately consistent with report narrative), but fact find records £230/month (£2,760/year) — these cannot both be correct.
      - Fact find total monthly household expenditure is £1,718 [P1], while report states approximately £1,700/month — a £18 difference (1.1%), within rounding tolerance.
      - Fact find [P1] total net monthly income is £1,430, far below the report's derived total of £4,486.67/month — consistent with the fact find recording £1,200 employed + £230 State Pension only, omitting Kim's income, Sea Cadets income, and using a lower HGV figure.
      - Report states Kim's State Pension of £230/week future income; fact find does not appear to record Kim's state pension or her employment income separately, only the combined household income of £1,430 which does not include her.
      - Sea Cadets income of £20/week (£86.67/month) is stated in the report narrative [P17] but does not appear in the fact find income table [P10] or [P13].
      - Cashflow assumption in [P9] states Kim's state pension age as 67 ('from age 67'), but canonical model infers state pension age 66 and [P11] shows Kim's STATE PENSION AGE as 66 — internal inconsistency in cashflow section of the report itself.
    There are material discrepancies between the income figures in the suitability report and the fact find evidence. The most significant is the State Pension figure: the report asserts £230/week (annualising to approximately £11,973 p.a. as confirmed in the cashflow section [P9]), but the fact find [P10] records State Pension as £230/month net — a fourfold difference. This is a direct contradiction between the report and the Category B evidence. The HGV income also differs: the report states £300/week (£1,300/month equivalent per the 52-week annualisation guard), but the fact find records £1,200/month net [P10][P20]. The 7.7% gap exceeds the 5% tolerance. The Sea Cadets income of £20/week appears in the report narrative [P17] but is absent from the fact find income table. The total monthly income in the fact find (£1,430) is consistent with only the fact find's own lower figures (£1,200 employed + £230 State Pension monthly), not with the report's figures. The expenditure discrepancy (£1,700 vs £1,718) is within rounding tolerance and is not a concern. The cashflow section [P9] itself introduces a further inconsistency by stating Kim's state pension age as 67, while the modelling tool [P11] and the canonical model both indicate age 66. The State Pension frequency mismatch (weekly vs monthly in the fact find) is the most severe issue as it drives the income adequacy assessment and cashflow modelling used to support the recommendation.

  [G1.6] State pension entitlement is consistent with the evidence
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   JS is in receipt of his full State Pension of £230 per week (currently in payment). Kim will receive her full State Pension of around £230 per week when she reaches State Pension age of 67, payable from 2027-10-14.
    File:     The suitability report narrative (P9) states £230 per week for JS (in payment) and £230 per week for Kim at State Pension age. The cashflow assumptions in P5 state 'You receive state pension income of £11,973 per annum' and 'Kim will receive state pension income of £11,973 per annum from age 67.' The intelliflo planning baseline scenario (P1) shows Kim's STATE PENSION AGE as 66, not 67. The fact find (P15) contains a State Pension Entitlement section but no values are populated. No HMRC State Pension forecast or BR19 projection is present in the evidence pack.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts Kim's State Pension age is 67; intelliflo planning baseline scenario (P1) shows Kim's STATE PENSION AGE as 66.
      - Report states Kim's State Pension payable from 2027-10-14; intelliflo baseline (P1) shows Kim's 'Retire' event at age 71 in 2027, with STATE PENSION AGE listed as 66, which would place State Pension commencement around 2026, not 2027.
      - £230 per week annualises to £11,960 per year (£230 x 52); the cashflow model uses £11,973 per annum — a difference of £13 per annum (rounding/presentation difference).
      - Fact find State Pension Entitlement section (P15) is blank — no values recorded for either client.
      - No BR19 projection or HMRC State Pension forecast is present in the evidence pack to corroborate either client's entitlement amount or State Pension age.
    The primary discrepancy of substance is the conflict between the report's assertion that Kim's State Pension age is 67 and the intelliflo planning tool's baseline scenario (P1) which records her STATE PENSION AGE as 66. These are not the same thing as retirement age (guard noted), but this field is explicitly labelled STATE PENSION AGE in the planning tool, making the mismatch a direct contradiction on the same concept. If Kim's State Pension age is 66 rather than 67, this affects both the timing of income in the cashflow model and the payable-from date. The cashflow in P5 cites age 67 for Kim's State Pension, consistent with the report, but P1 says 66 — an internal conflict across the case file documents. The £230 per week vs £11,973 per annum difference is £13 (£11,960 vs £11,973), which is within typical rounding/inflation-uplift tolerance and does not represent a material concern. The absence of any BR19 or HMRC forecast means there is no external corroboration for the £230 per week entitlement figure for either client, or for the State Pension ages asserted. The fact find State Pension section is entirely blank. The conflict on Kim's State Pension age between the report/cashflow (67) and the intelliflo baseline planning tool (66) is a material discrepancy on a figure that affects the timing of income in the retirement plan.

  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   Five existing pension arrangements are listed on page 5 of the suitability report: (1) People's Pension 6197175/PP – current/transfer value £6,601.55, fund: Global Investments (up to 85% shares), contributions £1,193.33 PA client / £897.13 PA employer; (2) Scottish Widows ZU4225575 – £211.32, fund: SW Pension Portfolio Four CS1 & Three CS1; (3) Aviva SP50163762 – £3,744.36, fund: Aviva pension my future focus consolidation; (4) Zurich P10026-719-001/DL – £103,439.24, fund: Managed AP; (5) Standard Life D2301334000 – £3,002, fund: SL at retirement universal (PP 10 year) pension fund.

    Discrepancies:
      - Standard Life D2301334000: report states £3,002 (rounded); provider statement [P12] shows £3,002.02 and a separate Standard Life valuation [P18] dated 06 Oct 2025 shows £2,994.30 – minor rounding difference on [P12] but [P18] shows a different valuation of £2,994.30 on a later date.
      - People's Pension 6197175/PP: report states current/transfer value £6,601.55 and contributions of £1,193.33 PA client / £897.13 PA employer; no provider statement showing this fund value or these contribution amounts appears in the retrieved evidence – only contribution transaction history in small increments is present.
      - Scottish Widows ZU4225575: report states current/transfer value £211.32; no provider statement confirming this value appears in the retrieved passages.
      - Fact find [P1] records an Aviva Platform personal pension AV2936864-001 with a value of £116,555.45 (valuation date 23/07/2026) – this arrangement does not appear in the suitability report's list of five existing arrangements and is not explained.
      - The report's Standard Life value of £3,002 appears rounded from the provider's £3,002.02 – immaterial rounding.
      - People's Pension contributions (£1,193.33 PA client; £897.13 PA employer) are stated in the report but no supporting document in the pack confirms these annual contribution amounts.
    Three of the five arrangements can be at least partially corroborated by provider evidence in the pack. Zurich P10026-719-001/DL is confirmed at £103,439.24 by [P15], Aviva SP50163762 is confirmed at £3,744.36 by [P16], and Standard Life D2301334000 is confirmed at £3,002.02 by [P12] (the report rounds to £3,002 – this is immaterial rounding within the penny-tolerance guard). A second Standard Life valuation [P18] shows £2,994.30 on 06 Oct 2025, a later date than [P12]; the date-gap guard applies and this is not a mismatch. The People's Pension value of £6,601.55 and the Scottish Widows value of £211.32 have no provider statement in the pack confirming them; only transaction-level contribution history is present for the People's Pension. Per the evidence-absent rule, plan values quoted without provider documentation are a Potential Concern. More materially, the fact find [P1] records an Aviva Platform personal pension AV2936864-001 with a fund value of £116,555.45 (valuation date 23/07/2026) that does not appear anywhere in the suitability report's list of existing arrangements. This is an unsupported omission: an asset of material size recorded in the fact find is unaccounted for in the report. The annual contribution amounts for the People's Pension (£1,193.33 PA / £897.13 PA) are asserted by the report but no document in the pack corroborates them. These gaps and the unexplained Aviva Platform arrangement constitute material concerns driving a Potential Concern outcome at High severity.

  [G1.8] Data mismatches and unsupported figures within the report itself
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The report contains several internally inconsistent figures and statements: (1) People's Pension value £6,601.55 is consistent but the recommendation to 'Retain' contradicts consolidation objective OBJ3. (2) Risk rating described as both 4 (per questionnaire) and 5 (agreed/confirmed) on page 4 with ambiguous attribution. (3) Aviva existing charge 0.50%; new platform charge 0.44% (-0.06% impact), but page 6 text states 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution', which contradicts the charge comparison direction. (4) Fund allocation on page 2 lists 'CT Universal Map Balanced 50%, HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio 50%' while page 12 lists 'HSBC at 50% and CT Universal MAP at 50%' — names differ across pages. (5) Income described as 'typically earning around £300 per week net' on page 3, but page 14 cashflow assumes a fixed £300 per week without qualification. (6) Sum of five existing arrangement values = £116,998.47, but total transfer value stated on page 9 = £110,185, an unexplained shortfall of £6,813.47.
    File:     This requirement compares the report against itself. No external supporting documents were searched or required for this group.
    Discrepancies:
      - Transfer value total £110,185 does not reconcile with the sum of the five individual plan values (£6,601.55 + £211.32 + £3,744.36 + £103,439.24 + £3,002.00 = £116,998.47); unexplained shortfall of £6,813.47.
      - Page 6 text states the existing plan is '0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution', but the charges table on page 11 shows existing Aviva 0.50% vs new 0.44%, meaning the recommended solution is 0.06% cheaper — the text contradicts the arithmetic direction.
      - Risk rating described as 4 per questionnaire and then as 5 'agreed' on the same page 4, with two different framings (adviser override vs client's own wish to increase), creating an unresolved internal inconsistency about the basis for the final rating.
      - People's Pension (£6,601.55) is recommended to be 'Retained' on page 6, contradicting the stated consolidation objective OBJ3 which implies consolidating pensions.
      - Fund names differ across pages: page 2 uses 'CT Universal Map Balanced' and 'HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio', while page 12 uses 'CT Universal MAP' and 'HSBC', with no explicit cross-reference confirming they are the same funds.
      - Page 3 qualifies income as 'typically earning around £300 per week net' (variable), but page 14 cashflow treats £300 per week as a fixed deterministic assumption without acknowledging variability.
    All six discrepancies are drawn from the report's own internal text and figures. The most material is the £6,813.47 shortfall between the sum of the five individual plan values (£116,998.47) and the stated total transfer value (£110,185). This figure drives the recommendation (transfer value, charge calculations and required outperformance all reference £110,185), making it a highest-severity finding. The charge direction error on page 6 — stating the existing plan is 0.06% more expensive when the table shows the opposite — is also high severity as it directly contradicts the recommendation rationale. The risk rating inconsistency (4 vs 5, two different framings on the same page) is an internal self-contradiction requiring no external evidence, rated High. The fund name discrepancy (page 2 vs page 12) uses shortened vs full names but the allocations (50%/50%) match; this is lower severity but still a presentation inconsistency that should be resolved. The People's Pension 'Retain' vs consolidation objective OBJ3 tension is noted but relates partly to recommendation suitability (CHK-002/CHK-009 territory); however the factual inconsistency between the stated objective and the action is within scope of CHK-001 as a data/consistency matter. The income 'typically' vs fixed cashflow difference is a low-severity presentation issue. No guard (as-at date difference) explains the £6,813.47 transfer value shortfall or the charge direction error. Overall outcome: Potential Concern, severity High, driven primarily by the transfer value arithmetic discrepancy and the charge direction contradiction.

  [G1.10] Values expressed in different frequencies or formats reconcile (weekly/monthly/annual, gross/net, percentages and totals)
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report states the following incomes on page 3: (1) Client 1 (Alan) earns £300 per week net as HGV driver; (2) Client 1 receives £20 per week from Sea Cadets volunteer role; (3) Kim earns approximately £1,100 per month net; (4) Client 1 receives full State Pension of £230 per week; (5) Kim will receive full State Pension of around £230 per week. Monthly equivalents are derived as: £300/wk = £1,300/month; £20/wk = £86.67/month; £1,100/month stated; £230/wk State Pension = £1,000/month each. Total monthly net income stated as £4,486.67 (sum: 1300 + 86.67 + 1100 + 1000 = 4486.67, but note Kim's future State Pension appears included). Expenditure stated as £1,700/month. Cashflow model uses £300 per week for Alan.
    File:     The fact find (P2) shows: Client 1 (Alan) basic income from CDH Recruitment is £1,200 net per month (not £300/week or £1,300/month equivalent). State Pension shown as £230/month net (not £230/week). Kim's income from CDH Recruitment is £1,200 net per month. Total Net Monthly Income in fact find cashflow section is £1,430 (P1). Total Monthly Expenditure is £1,718 (P1), not £1,700. The fact find income table shows monthly figures only — no weekly figures. Sea Cadets income of £20/week is not evidenced in the fact find at all. The £230 State Pension in the fact find (P2) is listed as monthly, not weekly.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states Alan earns £300 per week net (monthly equivalent £1,300), but fact find (P2) records basic income as £1,200 net per month — a difference of £100/month (£1,300 vs £1,200).
      - Report states State Pension for Alan is £230 per week (monthly equivalent ~£1,000 using 52/12 conversion, or ~£997), but fact find (P2) records State Pension as £230 per month — a discrepancy of approximately £770/month on this single income line.
      - Report states Sea Cadets volunteer income of £20 per week (£86.67/month equivalent), but this income source does not appear in the fact find income records at all.
      - Report states Kim earns approximately £1,100 per month net, but fact find (P2) records Kim's income as £1,200 net per month — a difference of £100/month.
      - Report states total monthly expenditure of £1,700, but fact find (P1) records calculated total monthly household expenditure as £1,718 — a difference of £18/month.
      - Fact find (P1) records total net monthly income as £1,430, which is irreconcilable with the report's stated total of £4,486.67 — a very large discrepancy driven largely by the State Pension frequency mismatch and other income differences.
      - Report's total monthly net income of £4,486.67 includes Kim's future State Pension (£1,000/month), which is a future income not yet in payment; this may inflate the current income total but the basis is not clearly separated in the report.
      - The cashflow model (P2/fact find) shows a monthly deficit of -£288, whereas the report narrative implies a comfortable surplus given income of £4,486.67 vs expenditure of £1,700 — these are entirely inconsistent pictures of the household's financial position.
    Multiple material discrepancies exist between the report's income figures and the fact find evidence. The most significant is the State Pension frequency error: the report states £230 per week (annualising to ~£11,960/year, monthly ~£997-£1,000), while the fact find records it as £230 per month (£2,760/year) — a near four-fold difference on this line. This is not a rounding or formatting issue; it is a direct contradiction between the report and the fact find on a key retirement income figure. The weekly/monthly guard does not explain this away — it requires comparison of like with like, and when converted to a common frequency the values are fundamentally different. Alan's employment income also differs: £300/week net = £1,300/month in the report, versus £1,200/month net in the fact find — a £100/month gap that is not explained by rounding. Kim's income is stated as £1,100/month in the report versus £1,200/month in the fact find — another £100/month unexplained gap. The Sea Cadets income (£20/week) has no corroboration in the fact find at all. Expenditure differs by £18/month (£1,700 report vs £1,718 fact find) — this is minor. The cumulative effect is that the fact find shows total monthly income of £1,430 and a deficit of -£288/month, while the report presents a picture of income of £4,486.67 against expenditure of £1,700, implying a large surplus. These are materially contradictory portrayals of the household cashflow position. The State Pension discrepancy is particularly significant as it drives retirement income planning and the adequacy of the recommended solution. The income figures used in the cashflow model (£300/week per P2 assumption) appear to use only Alan's HGV income, not the full household picture. The gross/net guard does not apply here as all figures are stated as net. The frequency conversion guard (52 vs 4.33×12) creates only a 0.4% difference on weekly amounts and does not explain the State Pension discrepancy. These are genuine material mismatches that go to the core of the client's financial position and retirement planning suitability.

    Report:   Cashflow was modelled.     File:     Intelliflo proposed scenario [P7] shows asset tree with New Aviva pension £110,185 and existing pensions (Peoples pension £6,601, Scottish Widows £211) retained separately. The fund allocation (HSBC 50%, CT Universal MAP 50%) is confirmed in [P18] and [P24]. The mid-growth rate discrepancy (2.94% in suitability report vs 2.95% in pension switch report [P16]) is a minor rounding difference and is most likely explained by the provider using a slightly different FCA-prescribed rate in its own switch report compared to the cashflow tool; however it is a difference that should be noted. More materially, the -30% market crash stress test figure appears in the report narrative but is not present in any retrieved cashflow modelling passage — the stress test outputs are described in [P1] only at a high level ('no shortfall') without the -30% input parameter being visible in the modelling file. The monthly surplus of £2,786.67 is similarly unCorroborated in any passage. The 'typically' qualifier on the £300/week income is a presentational inconsistency rather than a material factual error, and is addressed by the guard that a deterministic model legitimately uses a point estimate. The -30% crash figure and £2,786.67 surplus are both asserted in the report but have no corroborating document in the file — these are moderate concerns as the surplus figure is a factual output that should be traceable and the stress test input parameter is not visible in any modelling output provided.

CHK-002 — Objectives, Needs and Time-Horizon Alignment
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 7 requirement(s). 7 raised a concern (G2.1, G2.2, G2.3, G2.4, G2.5, G2.6, G2.7). The fact find narrative (P12) mentions consolidation and retirement income as the primary stated needs, with lifestyle affordability not explicitly recorded as a separate standalone objective — it is implicit rather than explicitly stated as a distinct priority-ranked goal in the fact find. The fact find (P12) records the time horizon as 'Short Term', which could be read as inconsistent with the long-term lifestyle sustainability objective (OBJ1) that implies a lifetime planning horizon. The file note (P5) references the client wishing for 'best growth potential over a 10-year period', which introduces a 10-year investment horizon not explicitly referenced in the three stated objectives in the suitability report. 4 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G2.1] Stated goals and their priority order
  Potential Concern — severity Low — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report states three client-stated objectives in priority order: (1) Ensure continued ability to afford lifestyle through lifetime; (2) Retire within two years with household income of £1,700 net per month; (3) Consolidate and streamline pension arrangements. All three are marked as client-stated and ranked 1, 2, 3 respectively.
    File:     The fact find (P12) records under 'Retirement Planning' that the client 'Just needs advice helping him organise his pensions in the best place. Wants to consolidate to help him get his head around what he has. Thinks he'll probably retire in the next 2 years and needs a household income of £1,700 net per month.' The suitability report itself (P2) restates the three objectives verbatim. The file note (P5) references the client's income sources and confirms he is not reliant on pension funds and wishes for best growth over a 10-year period. The intelliflo planning tool (P8) shows a retirement event for John at age 71 in 2027 (approximately 2 years from the 2025 advice date). The fact find declaration (P1) confirms the client signed that his financial planning objectives are those identified and prioritised in the document.
    Discrepancies:
      - The fact find narrative (P12) mentions consolidation and retirement income as the primary stated needs, with lifestyle affordability not explicitly recorded as a separate standalone objective — it is implicit rather than explicitly stated as a distinct priority-ranked goal in the fact find.
      - The fact find (P12) records the time horizon as 'Short Term', which could be read as inconsistent with the long-term lifestyle sustainability objective (OBJ1) that implies a lifetime planning horizon.
      - The file note (P5) references the client wishing for 'best growth potential over a 10-year period', which introduces a 10-year investment horizon not explicitly referenced in the three stated objectives in the suitability report.
      - No explicit priority ranking (1, 2, 3) of the three objectives is recorded in the fact find or file notes — the priority order appears in the suitability report only.
      - The intelliflo proposed scenario (P8) shows John's retirement at age 71 in 2027, which is approximately 2 years from 2025, consistent with OBJ2, but the baseline scenario data does not explicitly record the £1,700 net per month income target as a separate documented client requirement.
    The three objectives in the suitability report are substantially corroborated by the evidence, though with some gaps in explicit documentation. OBJ2 (retire in 2 years, £1,700 net/month) is well evidenced in the fact find (P12) which records both the 2-year retirement horizon and the £1,700 income figure explicitly. OBJ3 (consolidate pensions) is also clearly recorded in the fact find (P12). OBJ1 (ensure lifestyle affordability through lifetime) is the least explicitly documented in the supporting file: the fact find does not record it as a distinct named objective, though it is arguably implicit in the retirement planning discussion. The 10-year growth horizon referenced in the file note (P5) is not directly linked to any of the three stated objectives in the report, creating a minor tension — the stated goals focus on a 2-year retirement horizon while the investment is positioned over 10 years, but this relates more to the investment strategy rationale than the objective itself. The 'Short Term' time horizon entry in the fact find (P12) appears to relate to the retirement goal (2 years to retirement) rather than the investment term, so is not a material contradiction. The priority ranking is only explicitly recorded in the suitability report and is not evidenced in the fact find or file notes, though the client declaration (P1) confirms they agreed to the objectives as identified and prioritised. The guard that advisers paraphrase applies here — the substance of OBJ2 and OBJ3 is clearly evidenced. OBJ1 is weakly evidenced as a distinct standalone objective with client authorship, being more of an adviser framing of a general retirement planning need. No vulnerability factors identified in the evidence appear to materially affect these objectives or their documentation. Overall, the objectives are substantially supported but OBJ1 lacks explicit client-authored evidence as a distinct priority-ranked goal, and the priority ordering is not corroborated in the fact find or file notes.

  [G2.2] Retirement target date and investment time horizon details
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts: target date 2027-10-14; term 2 years; time horizons of LongTerm, MediumTerm, MediumTerm across objectives; intended retirement age 66. The target age field is absent from the extracted model (extraction silent on /objectives[]/targetAge).
    File:     [P10] (intelliflo proposed scenario) shows JS retiring at age 71 in 2027 and KS retiring at age 66 in 2026. State pension age for both is 66. [P16] (intelliflo baseline scenario) corroborates the same retire events: KS age 66 in 2026, JS age 71 in 2027. [P11] (risk profile report) records an investment period of 2 years. [P1] (pension switch report) uses retirement age 75 for the Aviva plan illustration. [P15] (Zurich policy info) records selected retirement age 65 with planned retirement age 75. The pension switch report throughout uses 'projected value at age 75' as the maturity point. The cashflow model in [P10] and [P16] runs to 2060 (age 100/104).
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts intended retirement age 66 for the client (JS), but [P10] and [P16] show JS retiring at age 71 in 2027, not age 66. Age 66 is shown as the state pension age and as KS's retirement age, not JS's.
      - The report's target date of 2027-10-14 aligns with JS retiring in 2027 at age 71, yet the report simultaneously states retirement age 66 — these are internally inconsistent: age 66 from a 1956 date of birth would be approximately 2022, not 2027.
      - The pension switch report uses age 75 as the maturity/projection age throughout (e.g. [P1], [P2], [P3], [P5], [P7]), which is materially longer than the 2-year investment term or the 2027 retirement target used to justify the risk profile and recommendation.
      - The risk profile tool ([P11]) records investment period of 2 years and investment of £106,000, but the pension switch comparison projects to age 75 — creating a spread of horizons (2 years vs ~6 years to 2027 vs ~6 years to age 75) without reconciliation in the report.
      - Time horizons recorded in the canonical model include 'LongTerm' for at least one objective, which is not obviously consistent with a 2-year investment period or a 2027 target date.
    The canonical model asserts retirement age 66 for JS, but the intelliflo planning tool in [P10] and [P16] clearly records JS's retire event as age 71 in 2027, with age 66 being the state pension age applicable to both clients and also KS's retirement age. This is a material discrepancy: the report appears to have attributed KS's retirement age (66) to JS, or has conflated state pension age with JS's chosen retirement date. The 2027 target date is corroborated by [P10], but is consistent with age 71 for JS (born 1956), not age 66. The 2-year investment term recorded in the risk profile tool ([P11]) is corroborated in the canonical model, but the pension switch report consistently projects to age 75, a materially different and longer horizon. The guards require collecting every horizon before judging: the spread here is 2 years (risk profile/canonical term), approximately 2 years to 2027 (retirement target), and approximately 6 years to age 75 (switch report illustrations). The switch report horizon of age 75 is a contractual/illustration default and is flagged by the guard as routinely different from the client's intended date — so that alone is not a concern. However, the misattribution of retirement age 66 to JS (when evidence clearly shows JS retires at 71) is not explained by any guard and represents a material error in the report's stated objective. Additionally, the presence of a 'LongTerm' time horizon classification alongside a 2-year term and a 2027 target date lacks supporting explanation. No vulnerability factors in the evidence appear to materially alter this assessment. The primary concern is the retirement age 66 attributed to JS being contradicted by the planning evidence showing age 71.

  [G2.3] Access requirements — emergency, planned and ad-hoc
  Potential Concern — severity Low
    Report:   The report asserts: (1) An emergency fund of £6,000 is held and confirmed sufficient to cover unexpected emergencies, allowing retirement planning to proceed with reassurance. (2) The client is comfortable with the pension wrapper because liquid cash is available for instant access needs. (3) Access to pension funds in the future will take 10–15 working days for administration, disinvestment and banking processes. (4) The client understands pension freedoms and prefers flexi-access drawdown for flexible control over how and when benefits are taken. No specific planned withdrawal amounts or dates are stated; ad-hoc access is addressed by noting the 10–15 working day processing time and the availability of liquid cash.
    File:     The cashflow planning files confirm £6,000 savings held as a Cash Account by JS, shown as liquid assets throughout the plan. The proposed scenario shows liquid assets starting at £6,000 in 2025 and declining gradually (to ~£5,956 in 2026, ~£5,912 in 2027 etc.), confirming cash is present but slowly eroding. The suitability report itself (P20/P21) states the client is not reliant on pension funds, that state pension income covers bills in retirement so the pension can keep growing for large ad-hoc spends, and that flexi-access drawdown is preferred. P10 explains FAD mechanics including ad-hoc withdrawals. P24 states 'Your state pension income will cover your bills in retirement. Meaning your pension will be allowed to keep growing for large ad hoc spends.' The fact find (P19) confirms the £6,000 cash saving. No evidence of any specific planned withdrawal event, date-certain access requirement, or additional ad-hoc access need beyond what the report describes is present in the file.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report characterises the £6,000 as an 'emergency fund confirmed sufficient' but the cashflow model shows liquid assets declining year on year (from £6,000 in 2025 to ~£4,773 by age 100/96), implying the cash is being drawn upon to meet expenditure shortfalls in later years — this is not flagged as a concern in the report. However, no explicit contradiction arises for the current period under review.
      - The report records the access type for pension drawdown as 'Emergency' in the canonical model, which is a classification mismatch — the described need (future flexible drawdown with 10–15 working days processing) is an ad-hoc/flexible access need, not an emergency fund. The £6,000 cash account is the emergency reserve; the pension access description is a general liquidity notice.
      - No amount is specified for the pension access need (amount field is null/Absent), meaning the report does not quantify any planned or ad-hoc withdrawal requirement. No evidence in the file specifies a planned withdrawal amount either, so this is consistent silence rather than a contradiction.
      - The current monthly cashflow in the fact find (P11) shows a negative disposable income of -£288/month (income £1,430, expenditure £1,718), which is not reconciled in the report's emergency fund sufficiency assessment. The report states the emergency fund is confirmed sufficient, but the underlying cashflow deficit is not addressed.
    The core assertions are broadly supported. The £6,000 emergency fund is evidenced in both the cashflow planning files (P1, P2, P5, P13) and the fact find (P19). The report's statement that liquid cash covers instant access needs is corroborated by P24 and P20/P21 which confirm state pension income covers regular bills, leaving the pension to grow. The FAD flexibility and 10–15 working day access notice is a standard product disclosure consistent with P10 and P21. The most notable discrepancy is the misclassification in the canonical model of the pension drawdown notice as an 'Emergency' access type — the evidence and the report text both describe this as general flexible/ad-hoc access, not an emergency reserve. The actual emergency fund is the £6,000 cash. This is a labelling/categorisation inconsistency in the extraction rather than a substantive error in the report itself. The more material concern is the fact-find monthly cashflow deficit of -£288/month (P11), which is not acknowledged in the report's confirmation that emergency funds are sufficient. While the report notes income comfortably meets expenditure elsewhere (P20), the raw fact-find figures show a deficit, and the cashflow model shows liquid assets declining slowly over time. This raises a moderate concern that the emergency fund sufficiency assertion is not fully evidenced given the underlying cashflow position. No vulnerability factors are identified in the evidence that materially affect this access/liquidity assessment. Overall the access requirements are addressed, but the cashflow deficit visible in the fact find creates a gap in the evidential support for the emergency fund sufficiency claim.

  [G2.4] Foreseeable life changes
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report asserts four foreseeable life changes: (1) Client (JS) retirement expected within two years, dated 2027-10-14, with financial impact of -£1,386.67/month net (loss of HGV income £1,300/month and Sea Cadets income £86.67/month); (2) Kim's retirement at age 66, dated 2026-03-26, with financial impact of -£1,100/month net; (3) Kim's State Pension commencement at age 66, dated 2026-03-26, with financial impact of +£1,000/month net (£230/week); (4) IHT regime change for pensions from 6 April 2027, with no quantified financial impact.
    File:     The fact find (P6, P9) records JS expects to retire in the next 2 years and needs a household income of £1,700 net per month. Kim intends to continue working until she reaches 66 at which point she plans to retire fully. Kim will receive her full State Pension of around £230 per week when she reaches State Pension age. The cashflow tool (P8) shows JS retire event at age 71 in 2027 and Kim retire event at age 66 in 2026, consistent with the report. The suitability report itself (P6) states the IHT pension regime change from 6 April 2027. The fact find income section (P18) records Kim's basic income as £1,200/month net and State Pension as £230/month, not £230/week. The cashflow baseline (P15) records Kim's employment income as £13,200/year (£1,100/month). The report states Kim's State Pension will be £230/week (approx £996/month), which the canonical model rounds to £1,000/month impact; the fact find income table records Kim's State Pension as £230/month. The report states JS's Sea Cadets income is £20/week (£86.67/month); the fact find (P6) corroborates £20/week. JS's HGV income is stated as £300/week net (approx £1,300/month) in the report and corroborated in the file.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states Kim will receive her full State Pension of around £230 per week when she reaches State Pension age (approx £996-£1,000/month); the fact find income table [P18] records Kim's State Pension as £230 per month — a material discrepancy in the stated State Pension amount (weekly vs monthly).
      - The canonical model derives JS retirement financial impact as -£1,386.67/month, combining HGV income (£1,300/month from £300/week) and Sea Cadets income (£86.67/month from £20/week). The fact find [P18] records Kim's basic income as £1,200/month not £1,100/month (though the cashflow model [P15] uses £1,100/month / £13,200/year), creating a minor discrepancy in Kim's employment income figure between fact find and cashflow model.
      - The report asserts JS retirement date as 2027-10-14 (age 71); the cashflow tool [P8] shows JS retire at age 71 in 2027, consistent; the report text says 'within the next two years' from a 2025 report date, which aligns with 2027 broadly, though 2027-10-14 is a specific date not explicitly evidenced as a client-stated date.
      - The IHT regime change is noted in the report as a foreseeable change affecting the client; per the guard, legislative changes affecting everyone are not client-specific foreseeable changes — this is a difference in categorisation rather than a factual discrepancy.
      - The fact find [P18] records 'Do you expect any changes in your income in the foreseeable future? No' for both clients, yet the report records multiple foreseeable income changes (retirement, State Pension). This creates a tension between the fact find's recorded answer and the foreseeable changes documented in the report.
    The main foreseeable life changes — JS retirement within two years, Kim's retirement at 66, and Kim's State Pension commencement — are all corroborated in the suitability report narrative (P6), the fact find objectives section (P9), and the cashflow planning tool (P8). The retire events in the cashflow tool at age 71/2027 for JS and age 66/2026 for Kim are fully consistent with the report. The HGV income (£300/week = £1,300/month) and Sea Cadets income (£20/week = £86.67/month) are evidenced in P6 and the derived retirement impact figure of -£1,386.67/month is arithmetically coherent. Kim's employment income of £1,100/month used in the cashflow model (P15: £13,200/year) is consistent with the report's stated £1,100/month, though the fact find income table (P18) records £1,200/month as Kim's basic income — a minor discrepancy that slightly undermines the corroboration. The most material discrepancy is the Kim State Pension figure: the report and P6 state £230 per week (approx £996-£1,000/month), but the fact find income table (P18) records £230 per month for Kim's State Pension — a fourfold difference. This is material because it affects the income modelling at Kim's retirement. The fact find's 'No' answer to expected income changes (P18) is inconsistent with the multiple foreseeable income changes documented in the report; this suggests the fact find may not have been fully completed or updated, which is a gap in evidencing client-disclosed foreseeable changes. The IHT regime change is a legislative change affecting all DC pension holders, not a client-specific disclosure; the guard applies and this is not a finding in its own right. The specific JS retirement date of 2027-10-14 is not evidenced as a client-stated date — it appears derived from birth date and age — but the narrative 'within two years' is evidenced. The Kim State Pension weekly vs monthly discrepancy is the most significant concern as it would materially affect the income projection used to justify retirement adequacy. No vulnerability factors are identified in the evidence that would materially alter this assessment.

  [G2.5] Conflicting goals
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The report states three objectives ranked by priority (ranks 1, 2, 3). One target date is extracted: 2027-10-14 (approximately 2 years from the advice date, consistent with the report's statement 'You want to retire in 2 years time'). The objectives are: (1) ensure continued affordable lifestyle through lifetime; (2) retire in 2 years with household income of £1,700 net per month; (3) clearer understanding of pension arrangements and consolidation into a more streamlined structure ready for retirement. No target amount is recorded in the extracted model. The recommended solution invests in a long-term balanced portfolio (risk 5) within a pension wrapper on the Aviva Platform, with a critical yield illustration projected to age 75.
    File:     [P4] confirms the three objectives verbatim: lifestyle sustainability, retirement in 2 years with £1,700 net/month household income, and pension consolidation/streamlining. [P11] (cashflow proposed scenario) shows JS retiring at age 71 in 2027 and KS retiring at age 66 in 2026, with a timeline running to 2060 (age 104/100). The baseline and proposed cashflow models ([P9], [P15]) show the plan generating surpluses throughout, with liquid assets and accessible pensions remaining positive across the entire projection period. [P8] confirms the critical yield projection runs to age 75, used as the planning horizon for the switch analysis. [P11] records JS's date of birth as 07/06/1956 (age 69 at advice) and KS's as 07/05/1960 (age 65). Retirement for JS is modelled at age 71 (2027), which is consistent with '2 years time'. No competing capital demands are visible in the evidence that would create a structural conflict between the objectives.
    Discrepancies:
      - The extracted model records only one targetDate (2027-10-14) and no targetAmount for any objective. The absence of a targetAmount means the quantified income goal of £1,700 net/month cannot be traced to a formal recorded target in the model, though it is stated narratively in the report ([P4]) and supported by the cashflow modelling.
      - The critical yield analysis uses age 75 as the planning horizon ([P8]), whereas the stated retirement date is approximately age 71 (2027). The investment is structured for growth to age 75, which is 4 years beyond the stated retirement access point. This could represent a mismatch if the client intends to access funds at retirement (age 71) but the strategy is calibrated to age 75.
      - Priority rank order is stated (1, 2, 3) but the report does not explicitly explain how a potential conflict between objective 1 (sustain lifestyle) and objective 2 (retire in 2 years) is managed if pension income at retirement is insufficient — though the cashflow model ([P9], [P11]) appears to address this implicitly by showing surpluses post-retirement.
      - No targetAmount is present in the extracted model for any objective, meaning the £1,700/month income requirement is only evidenced narratively rather than as a formal planning parameter in the canonical model.
    The three objectives are clearly stated in the report and are directly corroborated by [P4]. The priority ranking (1 > 2 > 3) is present. No structural conflict exists between the objectives in the sense that two objectives claim the same capital simultaneously: objective 3 (consolidation) enables objective 2 (retirement income), which enables objective 1 (lifetime lifestyle). The cashflow model ([P9], [P11], [P15]) supports the viability of the plan across the full lifetime horizon with consistent surpluses. However, a moderate structural concern arises: the critical yield analysis and investment strategy are projected to age 75 ([P8]: 'The retirement age used is 75'), while the stated retirement target is age 71 in 2027 (approximately 2 years from advice, per [P4] and [P11]). If the client intends to begin drawing income from the consolidated pension at age 71, the growth horizon used to justify the risk profile and fund selection (age 75) extends 4 years beyond the actual access date. This is not a locked-capital scenario (the Aviva Platform pension is accessible from minimum pension age), but it does mean the investment term used to justify the strategy is longer than the stated goal horizon, which falls within the 'High' severity band per the decision logic. The discrepancy between a 4-year extension of the planning horizon and the stated 2-year retirement target is not explicitly acknowledged or resolved in the suitability report. The absence of a recorded targetAmount is a minor gap but does not itself constitute a material concern given narrative corroboration. No vulnerability factors are identified in the evidence that would materially alter this assessment. The IHT position is noted as £0 ([P4]) and does not create a competing capital claim.

  [G2.6] Recommendations are appropriate for the stated goals given access needs, term and foreseeable changes
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report links six recommendations to three objectives: OBJ1, OBJ2, and OBJ3. OBJ1 and OBJ2 appear to relate to income/retirement planning and OBJ3 to pension consolidation. The sole recommended plan is an Aviva Pension Portfolio (Personal Pension) with a transfer value of £110,185 and a selected retirement age of 71, with projections to age 75. The client is stated to need £1,700 net per month income in retirement, with state pension covering bills in retirement so the pension can grow for large ad hoc spends. The client understands pension freedoms and prefers Flexi Access Drawdown. Time horizons are classified as LongTerm (OBJ1) and MediumTerm (OBJ2 and OBJ3). The cashflow runs to age 100. No emergency fund, liquidity requirements, or foreseeable life changes are explicitly stated in the canonical model.
    File:     The fact find [P24] records monthly household expenditure of £1,718 against monthly net income of £1,430, giving a monthly deficit of -£288, with no changes in expenditure expected. The policy research [P1] records a retirement income need of £1,700 net per month. The cashflow model [P10] confirms: state pension income of £11,973 per annum for both client and partner Kim, retirement expenditure of approximately £1,700 per annum (per report text, though the cashflow shows annual not monthly), and notes 'Your state pension income will cover your bills in retirement. Meaning your pension will be allowed to keep growing for large ad hoc spends.' The intelliflo cashflow [P12] confirms figures in real terms. The Zurich policy info [P21, P23] uses retirement date of 7 June 2031 (age 75). The Aviva existing plan [P22] states minimum pension age 55 (57 from April 2028). The suitability report [P16] confirms the client 'understand[s] the pension freedoms and prefer[s] the flexibility of entering Flexi Access Drawdown.' The pension switch report [P15] uses retirement age 75. The fund recommendation [P13] confirms investment strategy aligned to retirement objectives. No emergency fund or specific liquidity requirement is documented in any passage. No foreseeable life changes are recorded in the fact find [P24].
    Discrepancies:
      - The selected retirement age in the canonical model is stated as 71 (from the recommendedPlans field), but projections are to age 75 and the retirement age used in all comparison tables is 75. This creates an internal inconsistency: the plan's selected retirement age of 71 conflicts with the planning horizon of age 75 used throughout the report and supporting documents.
      - The report classifies OBJ2 and OBJ3 as MediumTerm but the planning horizon runs to age 75 (approximately 6 years from a client born 1956, currently ~69) — a relatively short horizon that is consistent with medium-term but the report does not explicitly articulate why pension consolidation (OBJ3) is medium-term when the consolidation is into a long-term wrapper.
      - The cashflow narrative in [P10] states retirement expenditure of 'approximately £1,700' but the fact find [P24] shows current monthly expenditure of £1,718 — if £1,700 is monthly, the pension plus state pension must cover £20,400 per annum; if it is annual, it is implausibly low. The report does not clarify whether £1,700 is monthly or annual in retirement.
      - The current monthly cash flow shows a deficit of -£288 per month (income £1,430, expenditure £1,718) [P24], yet no emergency fund or liquidity buffer is mentioned in the report or evidenced in any passage, and the report makes no comment on how the client is managing this deficit or how it affects suitability.
      - The People's Pension (£6,601.55) is recommended to be retained rather than consolidated, yet OBJ3 is pension consolidation and four other plans are switched to Aviva. This is noted in the extraction inconsistencies but affects objective alignment: the report links recommendations 1 and 2 solely to OBJ3 yet one recommendation is to retain, which does not consolidate.
      - No foreseeable life changes are documented, which is consistent between report and file, but the fact find [P24] does not record any discussion of anticipated changes despite the client's age (69) and proximity to retirement being a natural trigger for such discussion.
    The principal concern is the discrepancy between the selected retirement age of 71 (as extracted from the canonical model's recommendedPlans) and the consistent use of age 75 as the planning and projection horizon across the suitability report, pension switch report, Zurich and Scottish Widows illustrations, and cashflow model. If the client's actual intended retirement age is 71, the investment term is approximately 2 years, not 6, which would materially affect the appropriateness of a balanced risk-rated (risk 5) portfolio and the critical yield analysis. However, the weight of evidence in the supporting documents (all projections to age 75, Zurich retirement date of 7 June 2031 at age 75) suggests 75 is the operative horizon and 71 may be a data extraction artefact or an intermediate review age. The guard on access mechanism is relevant: the client is already 69 and above the minimum pension age, so there is no access restriction concern. The more significant concern is whether £1,700 in retirement is monthly or annual — the policy research [P1] states 'Needs £1,700 net per month income in retirement' and the cashflow [P10] states 'In retirement, you expect your annual expenditure to be approximately £1,700', which is a material inconsistency between the two supporting documents. If £1,700 is monthly (£20,400 pa), the cashflow model and state pension coverage statement need to be reconciled; if £1,700 is annual, it is implausibly low. The current pre-retirement monthly deficit of -£288 [P24] is unaddressed in the report — no liquidity buffer or emergency fund is documented. This is a moderate concern at this client's age and circumstances. The objective linkages are broadly specific: OBJ3 (consolidation) is linked to the switch recommendations, OBJ2 (retirement income/growth) to the investment, and OBJ1 (income) to the plan itself. However, the retention of the People's Pension is linked to OBJ3 (consolidation) which is internally contradictory. No vulnerability factors are evidenced in the file that would materially alter this assessment. On balance, the retirement age ambiguity (71 vs 75) and the £1,700 monthly vs annual ambiguity are material concerns requiring clarification, as they directly affect whether the term and projected outcomes support the stated objective.

  [G2.7] Goals, assumptions or planning requirements stated in the report that are not supported by the evidence
  Potential Concern — severity Low
    Report:   The suitability report asserts three client-stated objectives: (1) ensure continued ability to afford lifestyle through lifetime; (2) retire within two years with household income of £1,700 net per month; (3) consolidate and streamline pension arrangements. Planning assumptions include: general expenditure inflation 2%, savings inflation 2%, state pensions inflation 2.5%, Aviva personal pensions inflation 5%, mid growth rate 2.94%, and 0% of surplus income saved.
    File:     The fact find (category B) records the client's goals as: organise/consolidate pensions, retire in approximately 2 years, and achieve a household income of £1,700 net per month. The file notes (category C) reference consolidation as the main priority and retirement readiness. The intelliflo planning scenario (category F) is present in the file. The fact find also records a £6,000 cash savings balance and a monthly expenditure of £1,718 with net monthly income of £1,430, showing a current deficit. No explicit record of the lifestyle-affordability objective as a separately articulated client goal appears in the retrieved passages beyond what is implied by the retirement income target.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report frames 'ensure continued ability to afford lifestyle through lifetime' as a distinct, explicitly client-stated objective. The fact find passages do not record this as a separately stated goal; it appears to be an adviser inference from the retirement income and consolidation objectives rather than a verbatim client statement.
      - The cashflow assumptions (2%, 2%, 2.5%, 5%, 2.94%, 0% surplus saved) are stated in the report but no corroborating passage from the intelliflo planning scenario or any provider illustration has been retrieved to confirm these specific values were drawn from a supporting document rather than inserted by the adviser without a documented source.
      - The fact find records total monthly household expenditure of £1,718 against net monthly income of £1,430, producing a deficit of £288/month. The report's objective of affording lifestyle through lifetime and the cashflow model assuming the client can fund retirement at £1,700/month is not explicitly reconciled against the pre-retirement cash deficit evident in the fact find.
    Objectives 2 and 3 (retire in 2 years on £1,700 net/month; consolidate pensions) are directly and clearly evidenced in the fact find at [P5], which records verbatim that the client 'Thinks he'll probably retire in the next 2 years and needs a household income of £1,700 net per month' and 'Just needs advice helping him organise his pensions in the best place. Wants to consolidate.' The file notes at [P1] also confirm consolidation as the main client priority. These two objectives are well-supported. Objective 1 ('ensure continued ability to afford lifestyle through lifetime') is a reasonable adviser-level synthesis of the retirement income objective, and the guard permits this: an objective the adviser inferred and the client confirmed is treated as stated. However, no passage in the retrieved evidence explicitly records the client articulating this as a standalone goal; it reads as boilerplate framing of the income objective. This is a low-level concern rather than a material one. On cashflow assumptions: the intelliflo planning scenario document (category F) is present in the file ([P20]) but the retrieved passages from it do not contain the specific assumption values. The report cites these assumptions at page 19 under 'Cashflow Assumptions'. Given the guard that a planning assumption drawn from a provider illustration is evidenced by that illustration, and given that the intelliflo document exists in the file even if its relevant pages were not retrieved, this is a borderline rather than clear evidential gap. The pre-retirement cash deficit (£288/month shortfall per [P8]) is not addressed in the retrieved passages as a planning consideration, which could affect the plausibility of the retirement income objective, but this is more relevant to CHK-003/CHK-004 than to this check. Overall, the objectives are substantially supported, with only the lifestyle-through-lifetime framing being potentially generic/adviser-constructed rather than client-authored. CHK-003 — Risk, Capacity for Loss and Investment Strategy Match
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 10 requirement(s). 9 raised a concern (G3.1, G3.2, G3.3, G3.4, G3.5, G3.6, G3.7, G3.9, G3.10). The risk profile tool's 'Agreed risk level' section records 'No additional notes were recorded', meaning no written justification for the upward override from 4 to 5 is captured within the tool itself, even though the suitability report provides narrative justification. The suitability report contains two slightly different framings of the override: (a) 'We felt that this didn't fully reflect your risk appetite. We agreed that you are a risk rating 5 rather than a 4' (adviser-led override) and (b) 'You confirmed that you wish to increase your Risk rating to 5' (client-initiated increase), creating ambiguity about who drove the change. The ATR questionnaire responses in the risk profile document (P17) show cautious answers (e.g. 'Cautious' investment approach, 'Caution' associated with investment risk, 'More cautious' self-description, anxious at falls of more than £4,000 on £20,000) which are consistent with a rating of 4, not 5, adding weight to the concern that the upward override lacks a recorded rationale in the tool. 2 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G3.1] Risk rating and summary
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   Assessed (questionnaire) code: 4. Agreed code: 5, label 'Balanced', on a 1-10 scale.
    File:     Risk profile report (category D) records a natural risk level of 4 'Cautious Balanced' from the ATR questionnaire completed 18/09/2025, and an agreed risk level of 5 'Balanced' following interactive discussion. The SIPP research report (category G) confirms agreed risk level 5. The suitability report itself (category I) states the questionnaire indicated rating 4 but that rating 5 'Balanced' was agreed and confirmed by the client. File note (category C) references the client's existing funds at various risk levels but does not contradict the agreed rating. The override from 4 to 5 is documented but the adviser rationale note in the risk profile tool shows 'No additional notes were recorded' for the agreed risk level section.
    Discrepancies:
      - The risk profile tool's 'Agreed risk level' section records 'No additional notes were recorded', meaning no written justification for the upward override from 4 to 5 is captured within the tool itself, even though the suitability report provides narrative justification.
      - The suitability report contains two slightly different framings of the override: (a) 'We felt that this didn't fully reflect your risk appetite. We agreed that you are a risk rating 5 rather than a 4' (adviser-led override) and (b) 'You confirmed that you wish to increase your Risk rating to 5' (client-initiated increase), creating ambiguity about who drove the change.
      - The ATR questionnaire responses in the risk profile document (P17) show cautious answers (e.g. 'Cautious' investment approach, 'Caution' associated with investment risk, 'More cautious' self-description, anxious at falls of more than £4,000 on £20,000) which are consistent with a rating of 4, not 5, adding weight to the concern that the upward override lacks a recorded rationale in the tool.
    The report asserts a questionnaire score of 4 and an agreed rating of 5 ('Balanced') on a 1-10 scale. Both the category D risk profile report and the category G SIPP research report corroborate the agreed rating of 5 'Balanced' on the same 1-10 scale, so the agreed code, label and scale are supported. The questionnaire code of 4 is also corroborated by [P1]. The scale guard does not apply — both ratings are on the same 1-10 scale. The override guard applies: a questionnaire result and an agreed rating legitimately differ; an upward override is not itself a mismatch for G3.1 purposes and is handled by G3.6. However, the check instructions require that any override be clearly explained and evidenced. The suitability report provides narrative explanation (client not reliant on pension, wishes best growth over 10-year period, aware of higher risk). The ATR questionnaire responses in [P17] are notably cautious, and the risk profile tool's own notes field for the agreed risk level records 'No additional notes were recorded' ([P13]), meaning no independent written justification exists within the formal risk profiling tool to support the upward adjustment. The dual framing of the override in the suitability report (adviser-assessed vs client-elected) is an unexplained inconsistency flagged in the extraction ambiguities. On balance, the agreed rating of 5 is corroborated by category D and G evidence, satisfying the minimum corroborating category requirement. The absence of notes in the risk tool and the ambiguity of who drove the override are concerns but do not prevent the comparison from being made; they are a moderate concern about the adequacy of the override justification rather than a conflict in the risk code itself. The vulnerability overlay: no vulnerability characteristics identified in the evidence appear to affect this assessment. Overall: the agreed rating is evidenced, but the override justification is thin and internally inconsistent, warranting a Potential Concern at moderate severity.

  [G3.2] Capacity for loss assessment
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts a 'Medium' capacity for loss rating, separately assessed from ATR. The assessment basis includes: (1) state pension of ~£230/week; (2) HGV driver earnings ~£300/week net plus £20/week Sea Cadets; (3) Kim contributing £1,100/month from employment plus future state pension ~£230/week; (4) combined income comfortably meets household expenditure of ~£1,700/month; (5) savings of ~£6,000 with no outstanding liabilities; (6) client confirmed any potential underperformance would not have a detrimental impact on day-to-day living. The report states CFL is separately assessed from ATR.
    File:     [P11] (suitability report) contains verbatim the CFL rationale matching the canonical model. [P20] (Risk profile report, category D) contains a 'Capacity for loss for the goal' section but records 'No additional notes were recorded' for that section. [P5] (Risk profile report) shows the 'Capacity for loss questionnaire' section also records 'No additional notes were recorded'. [P10] (Risk profile report) contains the Capacity For Loss questionnaire responses for John Sullivan on 18/09/2025 — these responses are in the file. [P1] corroborates savings of £6,000. [P19]/[P17] show the agreed risk level is 5 Balanced and natural risk level is 4 Cautious Balanced. No formal 'Medium' CFL classification label appears anywhere in the risk profile report or the CFL questionnaire output; no numerical CFL rating scale is referenced in any retrieved passage.
    Discrepancies:
      - The risk profile report (category D) records 'No additional notes were recorded' in both the 'Capacity for loss for the goal' section [P20] and the 'Capacity for loss questionnaire' notes section [P5], meaning the supporting financial reasoning for the 'Medium' CFL rating is absent from the contemporaneous risk assessment document.
      - No formal CFL rating scale (e.g. Low/Medium/High or numerical equivalent) is visible in any retrieved passage from the risk profile report or CFL questionnaire output; the 'Medium' label in the suitability report cannot be cross-referenced to a defined scale in the evidence.
      - The CFL questionnaire responses are present in [P10] but no scored outcome or classification deriving 'Medium' from those responses is shown in the retrieved passages — the link between the questionnaire answers and the 'Medium' rating is not evidenced.
      - The extraction metadata flags '/riskAssessment/capacityForLoss/provenance/quote' as PresentButUnparseable and notes ambiguity about whether 'Medium' is a formal classification or descriptive, adding further uncertainty about the evidential basis.
      - The report states CFL is separately assessed from ATR, but the risk profile report notes sections for CFL contain no recorded content, which raises the question of whether a genuine separate assessment was conducted and documented.
    The suitability report provides a detailed financial rationale for the 'Medium' CFL conclusion — income sources, surplus over expenditure, savings, no liabilities, and client confirmation of no detriment to day-to-day living. Each individual financial fact cited (£230/week state pension, £300/week HGV earnings, £20/week Sea Cadets, £1,100/month Kim, £1,700/month expenditure, £6,000 savings) is corroborated by [P11] (the report itself) and the figures are cross-confirmed by [P1] for savings. However, the check requires the CFL to be evidenced by the supporting case file, not merely by the report repeating its own assertions. The critical gap is in category D (Risk profile report): both the 'Capacity for loss for the goal' section [P20] and the 'Capacity for loss questionnaire' notes [P5] record 'No additional notes were recorded'. While the CFL questionnaire responses exist in [P10], no scored result, classification or outcome deriving 'Medium' from those responses appears in the retrieved passages. There is no defined CFL rating scale in any retrieved passage against which 'Medium' can be validated. The guard warns that a CFL rating with no supporting financial reasoning is not evidenced merely because the words appear — here the words 'Medium' appear only in the report itself, not in the independent file evidence. The guard also flags that CFL restated as a consequence of ATR should be treated as a weak assessment; although the report does provide financial reasoning separate from ATR, the absence of any corroborating documentation in the risk profile report for the 'Medium' classification means the assessment basis in the supporting file is thin. The ambiguity noted in the extraction metadata about whether 'Medium' is formal or descriptive compounds this concern. No vulnerability factors from the file appear to materially affect this specific CFL assessment. Overall: the financial facts underpinning the CFL rationale are individually corroborated, but the formal CFL rating of 'Medium' is not anchored to a defined scale or recorded outcome in the contemporaneous risk profile document, constituting a moderate concern about evidential completeness.
