  [G3.3] Tolerance for loss and need-level risk
  Potential Concern — severity Low
    Report:   The report contains no quantified maximum acceptable loss percentage or amount. It makes two qualitative tolerance-for-loss statements: (1) the client 'is aware that investment values can rise as well as fall'; (2) 'You are aware that your funds may go up as well as down, and the higher risk you take with your funds, potential there is for larger drops in value.' No need-level risk figure is extracted from the model (path /objectives[]/needLevelRisk is absent).
    File:     Passage P18 (suitability report verbatim) reproduces both statements word-for-word. Passage P23 (stress-test section of the suitability report) models a -30% immediate market crash combined with high inflation (6%) and concludes there would be no shortfall requiring pension or investment funds to be used — this is the closest the file comes to quantifying a loss scenario, but no maximum acceptable loss percentage is stated. The risk profile report (P14, P19) and SIPP research report (P1, P5) confirm the agreed risk level of 5 (Balanced) and describe the Balanced investor profile, but contain no quantified tolerance-for-loss figure. The capacity-for-loss questionnaire responses are referenced in P11 and P24 but no numerical loss threshold is recorded.
    Discrepancies:
      - No quantified maximum acceptable loss percentage or amount appears anywhere in the file — the report asserts none, and none is evidenced. This is a documentation gap: tolerance for loss was never quantified.
      - The path /objectives[]/needLevelRisk is absent from the canonical model and no need-level risk figure appears in any retrieved passage, so no comparison between need-level risk and agreed risk rating can be performed.
      - The stress test in P23 models a -30% immediate loss scenario and concludes no shortfall would arise, but this scenario result is not connected in the report to a stated tolerance-for-loss limit or client acknowledgement of that specific loss magnitude as acceptable.
    The two qualitative tolerance-for-loss statements in the report are directly corroborated verbatim by P18. There is therefore no contradiction between report and file on what is stated. However, tolerance for loss is never quantified: no maximum acceptable loss percentage or amount is given in the report or anywhere in the supporting documents. The guard acknowledges that many firms do not quantify this separately and that absence is a documentation gap rather than a contradiction. The stress test at P23 models a -30% loss combined with 6% inflation and finds no shortfall, which partially addresses the spirit of the requirement but does not constitute a client-specific quantified tolerance. The need-level risk path is absent both from the extracted model and from all retrieved passages, so no assessment of whether the agreed risk rating is the minimum needed to meet the client's goals can be performed. Given the -30% stress test result is not linked to a stated tolerance limit or client acknowledgement of that specific loss threshold, and tolerance for loss is wholly unquantified, this represents a documentation gap. Under the decision logic and severity guidance, this is a low-severity concern: tolerance for loss is not quantified where no large-loss scenario is modelled as a client-specific threshold (the stress test exists but is not framed as a tolerance boundary). No vulnerability factors in the evidence appear to alter this conclusion materially.

  [G3.6] Risk override justification where the agreed rating differs from the questionnaire
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The suitability report asserts that the ATR questionnaire produced a risk rating of 4, but this was overridden upward to risk rating 5 ('Balanced'). The justification given is that the questionnaire 'didn't fully reflect your risk appetite'. The client acknowledgement states the client confirmed they wish to increase their risk rating to 5, is aware this is higher than their questionnaire result, is happy to take on this risk with their pension, is not reliant on pension funds, and wishes for best growth potential over a 10-year period with a balanced approach.
    File:     The risk profile report (category D) confirms the natural risk level from the ATR questionnaire as rating 4 ('Cautious Balanced') and the agreed risk level as rating 5 ('Balanced'). The SIPP research report (category G) confirms the agreed risk level as 5 ('Balanced'). The ATR questionnaire responses in the risk profile report appendix show cautious answers (e.g. 'Cautious' investment approach, 'More than £4,000' fall causing anxiety, 'Caution' association with investment risk). The file notes (category C) contain a draft of the ATR wording that states 'You are happy to proceed with a Risk rating of 6' — differing from the rating of 5 stated elsewhere in the same draft. The risk profile report records 'No additional notes were recorded' for the 'Agreed risk level' section, meaning no narrative rationale for the override is documented there. The capacity for loss section also records 'No additional notes were recorded'.
    Discrepancies:
      - The file notes (P11) contain draft ATR wording stating 'You are happy to proceed with a Risk rating of 6', while the suitability report and all other documents state the agreed rating is 5. This is an internal file inconsistency, though the finalised suitability report and research report consistently state 5.
      - The risk profile report's 'Agreed risk level' section records 'No additional notes were recorded', meaning the override from 4 to 5 carries no supporting narrative rationale within the risk profile document itself — the justification exists only in the suitability report.
      - The ATR questionnaire responses in the risk profile report appendix reflect consistently cautious answers, yet the agreed rating was increased from 4 to 5 without any notes in the risk profile document explaining why those cautious responses were considered not to reflect the client's true risk appetite.
      - The report states both (a) 'We agreed that you are a risk rating 5 rather than a 4' (adviser-led assessment) and (b) 'You confirmed that you wish to increase your Risk rating to 5' (client-led increase), creating an ambiguity as to whether this was an adviser override or a client election — noted in the extraction ambiguities.
    The override from questionnaire rating 4 to agreed rating 5 is confirmed by both the risk profile report (P2, P6) and the SIPP research report (P15, P16, P20), so the fact of an upward override is established. The suitability report provides a justification ('didn't fully reflect your risk appetite') and records client acknowledgement including awareness that 5 is higher than the questionnaire result, non-reliance on pension funds, and a 10-year growth objective. These are present in the suitability report and corroborated verbatim in P4. However, the risk profile report — the document that would normally contain the adviser's contemporaneous notes supporting such an override — records 'No additional notes were recorded' in both the 'Agreed risk level' and 'Capacity for loss' sections (P1, P3, P14). This means the override justification exists only within the suitability report itself and is not independently evidenced in the supporting file. The file notes (P11) contain a draft with the anomalous 'Risk rating of 6' reference, which, while likely a drafting error superseded by the final report, adds a degree of uncertainty about the process. The ATR questionnaire responses (P14) are consistently cautious in character, which makes the absence of contemporaneous notes in the risk profile document more material — a reviewer cannot independently verify from the file why those responses were considered not to reflect the client's true appetite. The direction of override is upward (4 to 5), which attracts high severity under the guidance. The justification in the report is brief and generic ('didn't fully reflect your risk appetite') and is not corroborated by any independent file note or adviser record beyond the suitability report itself. The vulnerability overlay does not appear to raise additional concerns specific to this check based on available evidence. Overall, the override is asserted and client acknowledgement is recorded in the suitability report, but independent evidential support in the file is absent, constituting a material concern.

  [G3.7] Recommendation or strategy exceeds risk tolerance, taking account of capacity for loss
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report asserts: (1) The client's agreed risk rating is 5 (Balanced), upgraded from questionnaire result of 4 (Cautious Balanced) at the client's request/adviser's assessment. (2) The recommended investment strategy targets risk rating 5. (3) Capacity for loss is assessed as 'Medium', justified by income comfortably meeting expenditure with savings and pension funds available. (4) The recommended funds are CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund C Acc (50%) and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio Accumulation C (50%), both mapped to risk level 5 Balanced. (5) Investment term is 10 years. (6) The client confirmed awareness that risk rating 5 is higher than the questionnaire result and is happy to proceed.
    File:     [P3] (Risk profile report, category D) confirms the natural/questionnaire risk level is 4 (Cautious Balanced), based on ATR questionnaire completed 18/09/2025. [P3] and [P2] confirm the agreed risk level following interactive discussion is 5 (Balanced). [P5] (Suitability Report, category I) states: 'We felt that this didn't fully reflect your risk appetite. We agreed that you are a risk rating 5 rather than a 4 as per the questionnaire. You are happy to proceed with a Risk rating of 5.' and 'You confirmed that you wish to increase your Risk rating to 5- Balanced investor, you are aware that this is higher than your agreed risk rating and are happy to take on this risk with your pension.' [P5] also records capacity for loss rationale: income from State Pension (~£230/week), employment (~£300/week), Sea Cadets (£20/week), and partner's employment (~£1,100/month), confirming any underperformance would not have detrimental impact on day-to-day living. [P1] confirms the agreed risk level is 5 and the recommended funds are CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund C Acc (50%) and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio Accumulation C (50%). [P7] and [P18] confirm the client's agreed risk level is 5 for the pension switch research. [P24] records the ATR questionnaire responses and shows no notes recorded for capacity for loss discussion beyond the questionnaire responses. The ATR questionnaire responses in [P24] show cautious answers (e.g. 'Cautious' investment approach, discomfort at loss of more than £4,000 on £20,000, 'Caution' associated with risk). The override to rating 5 is recorded but the capacity for loss section in [P24] records 'No additional notes were recorded'.
    Discrepancies:
      - The questionnaire result is risk rating 4 (Cautious Balanced) but the agreed/recommended rating is 5 (Balanced) — an upward override of one level.
      - The suitability report contains ambiguous language about the override: page 4 states both 'We agreed that you are a risk rating 5 rather than a 4' (adviser-led assessment) and 'You confirmed that you wish to increase your Risk rating to 5' (client-initiated increase), making it unclear whether this is an adviser override or a client-elected increase.
      - The capacity for loss section in the risk profile document (category D, [P24]) records 'No additional notes were recorded', meaning no formal rationale is documented in that section of the risk profiling tool — the CFL justification exists only in the suitability report narrative.
      - Capacity for loss is described as 'Medium' but there is no formal quantification of tolerance for loss (e.g. maximum acceptable percentage or monetary fall) in any document.
      - The ATR questionnaire responses in [P24] are consistently cautious (cautious investment approach, caution associated with risk, discomfort at losses over £4,000 on £20,000 = 20%), which would support rating 4 rather than 5; the upward override to 5 is therefore materially significant.
      - The value '7' (existing arrangement risk rating on provider scale) is not matched in the passages — nearest is 7.12 (a volatility figure in [P10]), which is not a risk rating.
    The core comparison can be made. The strategy targets risk rating 5, matching the agreed rating of 5 — so on the face of it the strategy does not exceed the agreed profile. However, several concerns arise. First, the override from questionnaire rating 4 to agreed rating 5 is an upward adjustment. The suitability report provides a client-acknowledgement rationale in [P5] (10-year term, no reliance on pension funds, desire for growth, awareness of higher risk), and the risk profile document [P3] records the agreed level as 5 following interactive discussion. This satisfies the requirement for a justification and client acknowledgement of the override, so the guard for 'override without evidenced justification' is partially addressed. However, the language inconsistency between adviser-led assessment and client-elected increase ([P5]) remains unexplained and creates ambiguity about whether the override followed proper process. Second, the capacity for loss is assessed as 'Medium' in the report narrative with income-based justification ([P5]), but the risk profile tool ([P24]) records 'No additional notes were recorded' in the capacity for loss section. This means the CFL assessment is not formally documented in the dedicated risk profiling document — it exists only in the suitability report's own narrative, which is not independent corroboration. This raises a moderate concern: the CFL is asserted in the report but not supported by a formal assessment record in the file. Third, the ATR questionnaire responses in [P24] are uniformly cautious, consistent with rating 4 not 5. The override to 5 is documented but the weak formal CFL record means it is harder to demonstrate that the override was appropriate given the client's actual demonstrated risk appetite. Fourth, no vulnerability factors appear in the evidence that would affect this assessment. The strategy risk rating (5) equals the agreed rating (5) so strategy does not exceed the agreed profile, but the capacity for loss documentation gap and the override ambiguity constitute a moderate concern requiring clarification. The missing risk rating '7' for one existing arrangement on a provider scale is noted; existing arrangement risk ratings are stated as not comparable to the client scale, so this does not affect the strategy alignment assessment per the guard.

  [G3.9] Recommended funds and portfolio align with the client's attitude to risk, capacity for loss and investment term
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts that the client's questionnaire produced a risk rating of 4 (Cautious Balanced) but the agreed risk rating was uplifted to 5 (Balanced) on a 1-10 scale. The capacity for loss is assessed as 'medium', supported by a strong income position (State Pension ~£230/week, HGV employment ~£300/week net, Sea Cadets £20/week, spouse income £1,100/month) comfortably covering household expenditure of ~£1,700/month, with savings of ~£6,000 and no liabilities. The investment term is 10 years. The recommended strategy is the 'Aviva Risk 5 Portfolio' (CIP, Blend style), comprising HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc (50%, risk rating 5, OCF 0.18%) and CT Universal MAP Balanced C (50%, risk rating 5, OCF 0.29%), both rated 5 on the adviser's scale. The report states the funds reflect the client's risk profile individually and combined, are risk-profiled solutions, and align with the agreed risk level of 5.
    File:     The risk profile report (D) confirms a natural risk level of 4 (Cautious Balanced) from the ATR questionnaire and an agreed risk level of 5 (Balanced) following an interactive discussion considering ATR, capacity for loss and required return. The Defaqto SIPP research report (G) confirms the agreed risk level of 5 (Balanced) and lists CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund C Acc (50%) and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio Accumulation C (50%) as the selected investments at that risk level. The HSBC KIID (H) states the fund is rated 3 on its own internal HSBC five-point risk scale (where 1=lower, 5=higher) and is rated 4 on the SRRI/Risk and Reward Indicator. The KIID also states the fund has a recommended minimum holding period of 5 years. The Defaqto research report shortlist confirms both HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio Accumulation C and CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund C Acc as risk-level-5-rated investments within the Defaqto framework, with OCFs of 0.18% and 0.29% respectively. MiFID II cost disclosures in the research report confirm ongoing costs of £99.17 (0.18%) for HSBC and £159.77 (0.29%) for CT Universal MAP. Capacity for loss factors (income, expenditure, savings) are corroborated by the suitability report text itself; no separate standalone CFL assessment document is present in the retrieved passages, but the narrative in the report is consistent with the financial position data referenced.
    Discrepancies:
      - The suitability report assigns both funds a risk rating of 5 on the adviser's 1-10 scale. The HSBC KIID states the fund is rated 3 on HSBC's own internal five-point Global Strategy risk scale (1=lower, 5=higher) and 4 on the regulatory SRRI/Risk and Reward Indicator. These are different scales, but the report labels the fund risk rating 5 without clarifying which scale is being used.
      - The report states the agreed risk rating is 5 but the questionnaire result was 4. The uplift is noted but the specific justification provided in the report ('We felt that this didn't fully reflect your risk appetite') is brief and the wording in the report is ambiguous — one passage says the adviser felt it didn't reflect the client's risk appetite, another says the client wished to increase it. The risk profile document (D) confirms the agreed level is 5 via an interactive discussion process, which provides some corroboration.
      - The KIID for HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio recommends a minimum holding period of 5 years; the client's investment term is 10 years, which exceeds this — this is not a mismatch per the stated guard.
      - The capacity for loss is described as 'medium' and stated to be consistent with the agreed ATR of 5, but no separately scored or independently assessed CFL document is present in the retrieved passages; the assessment rests entirely on the narrative in the suitability report.
      - The report's CFL conclusion states 'medium capacity for loss, consistent with your agreed Attitude to Risk level of 5' — this phrasing links the CFL rating to the ATR rating rather than deriving it independently, raising a question as to whether CFL was genuinely separately assessed.
    The core alignment question is whether the recommended Risk 5 portfolio is supported by the ATR, CFL and investment term evidence. On ATR: the risk profile document (D) and Defaqto research report (G) both confirm an agreed risk level of 5 (Balanced) following an interactive process starting from a questionnaire score of 4. This satisfies the override requirement in principle — the uplift is documented and the client confirmed it. The ambiguity in the report's wording (adviser-led vs client-elected uplift) is noted but the D-category document corroborates the agreed level of 5. On fund risk ratings: the report assigns both funds a risk rating of 5 on the adviser's 1-10 scale. The HSBC KIID's internal rating of 3 (on HSBC's own 1-5 scale) and SRRI of 4 are on different scales and per the stated guard should not be value-matched against the profiler's scale — they serve as corroboration of moderate risk character only. The Defaqto research report (G) lists both funds under risk level 5 investments within its framework, which directly corroborates the adviser's assignment of risk rating 5 to both funds. On CFL: the report asserts medium CFL with a reasoned narrative (income surplus, savings, no liabilities, client confirmation of no detrimental impact). The financial position data is consistent across the report. However, the conclusion states CFL is 'consistent with your agreed Attitude to Risk level of 5' — this phrasing conflates CFL with ATR rather than presenting CFL as an independent constraint. This is a moderate concern under the severity guidance (CFL asserted as a restatement of ATR). No separate CFL assessment tool output is present in the file. On investment term: 10 years is stated; the HSBC minimum holding period of 5 years is shorter, so no mismatch per the guard. On strategy alignment: the 50/50 split between two Balanced risk-5 funds, both confirmed in the Defaqto research report, is consistent with the agreed risk level. The benchmark (IA Mixed Investment 20-60% Shares) is appropriate for a balanced multi-asset strategy. The vulnerability overlay: no vulnerability factors are identified in the retrieved evidence that would materially affect this assessment. Overall: the main substantive concern is the CFL assessment being presented as consistent with ATR rather than as an independent finding, which is a moderate concern. The fund-level evidence from Defaqto (G) supports the risk alignment claim.

  [G3.10] Unsupported, contradictory or unexplained statements about risk, loss capacity, term or strategy
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The suitability report asserts: (1) the ATR questionnaire produced risk rating 4, but the adviser felt this did not fully reflect the client's risk appetite and agreed a rating of 5 (Balanced) after discussion; (2) the client confirmed they wished to increase to risk rating 5, are aware this is higher than their questionnaire result, and are happy to take on this risk with their pension; (3) the client confirmed they are not reliant on their pension funds and wish for best growth potential over a 10-year period; (4) capacity for loss is assessed as medium, based on state pension income of ~£230/week, HGV earnings ~£300/week net, Sea Cadets income £20/week, spouse Kim's employment contribution of £1,100/month plus future state pension, combined income comfortably meeting household expenditure of ~£1,700/month, savings of ~£6,000, no outstanding liabilities, and the client's confirmation that underperformance would not detrimentally affect day-to-day living; (5) the medium CFL is described as consistent with agreed ATR of 5; (6) tolerance for loss is addressed only through general volatility-awareness statements with no quantified maximum acceptable loss; (7) the recommended investment strategy is an Aviva Risk 5 Portfolio comprising HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc (50%, risk rating 5, OCF 0.18%) and CT Universal MAP Balanced C (50%, risk rating 5, OCF 0.29%), both rated risk 5, designed to remain within the agreed risk level; (8) each fund individually and combined reflects the client's risk profile so portfolio drift does not change risk exposure.
    File:     [P1]/[P8] (Risk profile report, category D): The natural ATR questionnaire result is documented as risk level 4 'Cautious Balanced'. The agreed risk level recorded in the risk profile document is 5 'Balanced', described as agreed following interactive discussion considering natural ATR, capacity for loss and required return. [P3]/[P4] note that no additional notes were recorded at the agreed risk level or capacity for loss discussion stages. [P15] records capacity for loss questionnaire responses. [P2]/[P5] (SIPP research report, category G): Agreed risk level confirmed as 5 Balanced; fund selection shows CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund C Acc (50%) and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio Accumulation C (50%). [P17] (Suitability report, category I): Confirms ATR section and capacity for loss section wording consistent with canonical model. [P18] (Suitability report, category I): Fund recommendation table confirms HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc 50% risk 5 / 0.18% and CT Universal MAP Balanced C 50% risk 5 / 0.29%. [P8] shows investment period of 2 years in the risk profile tool projection, whereas the suitability report states a 10-year investment term. [P14] shows the SIPP research summary uses an investment of £110,185 with a date of 13 October 2025.
    Discrepancies:
      - The suitability report states the client wishes to invest over a 10-year period ('best growth potential over a 10-year period'), but the risk profile report [P8] shows the investment period used in the risk profiling tool projections as 2 years, not 10 years.
      - The risk profile document [P3]/[P4] records 'No additional notes were recorded' at both the agreed risk level and capacity for loss discussion stages, providing no narrative support for why the questionnaire result of 4 was overridden to 5 beyond the bare agreed outcome.
      - The justification for the upward override from 4 to 5 is described in the suitability report as 'We felt that this didn't fully reflect your risk appetite' — this is an adviser judgement assertion with no supporting rationale or evidence in the risk profile document or any other category D/G file passage beyond the agreed output.
      - The suitability report describes capacity for loss as 'medium' and states it is 'consistent with your agreed Attitude to Risk level of 5', which conflates the CFL assessment with the ATR outcome rather than deriving CFL independently; however the report does provide a separate financial factual basis for the medium CFL rating.
      - Tolerance for loss is not quantified: no maximum acceptable loss percentage or monetary amount is stated in either the suitability report or any supporting document, and no large-loss scenario is modelled or evidenced.
      - The suitability report internal text contains an ambiguity: it states both 'We agreed that you are a risk rating 5 rather than a 4' (adviser-led) and 'You confirmed that you wish to increase your Risk rating to 5' (client-led), which are different characterisations of the same override event.
    The most material issue identified under G3.10 is the investment term discrepancy. The suitability report explicitly relies on a 10-year investment horizon as a justification for the agreed risk level of 5 and the growth-oriented strategy ('best growth potential over a 10-year period'). However, the risk profile document [P8] — the primary category D evidence for the ATR assessment — shows an investment period of 2 years was input into the profiling tool's projection engine. This is a direct contradiction between a key statement the recommendation rests on and the supporting evidence. A 10-year horizon is materially different from a 2-year horizon in the context of risk suitability and capacity for loss; the projections used to agree risk level 5 with the client were produced on a 2-year basis, not 10 years. This undermines the evidential basis for the agreed risk rating and the strategy alignment.
    
    The upward override from risk rating 4 to 5 is acknowledged and the client acknowledgement is recorded [P17]. The justification 'We felt that this didn't fully reflect your risk appetite' is thin — it is an assertion rather than a documented rationale — but the risk profile document does confirm the agreed output is 5 and the SIPP research [P2/P5] also records 5. The absence of additional notes in [P3/P4] means the file does not document what specific aspects of the discussion led the adviser to conclude rating 4 was insufficient. This is a concern but secondary to the term mismatch.
    
    The capacity for loss assessment provides a genuine financial factual basis (income, expenditure, savings, no liabilities, client confirmation of no detriment), so the CFL is not merely a restatement of ATR. The phrase 'consistent with your agreed Attitude to Risk level of 5' is a cross-reference rather than a derivation, and the separate financial rationale is present. This is not a freestanding concern for G3.10.
    
    Tolerance for loss is not quantified. No percentage or monetary maximum loss figure is given anywhere in the file. This is a low-severity gap per the severity guidance.
    
    The dual characterisation of the override (adviser-led vs client-led) is an internal inconsistency already flagged in the extraction ambiguities. It does not change the substantive finding but adds to the lack of clarity around the override process.
    
    No vulnerability overlay factors from the evidence materially affect this assessment: no vulnerability characteristics are recorded in the passages provided that would alter the risk, CFL or strategy conclusions.
    
    The investment term contradiction (10 years in the report vs 2 years in the risk profiling tool) is a material unsupported/contradictory statement about a factor explicitly used to justify the risk strategy, and it is not explained anywhere in the file. This drives the outcome to Potential Concern at Moderate-to-High severity, as the projections used to agree the risk level were produced on a materially shorter horizon than the report asserts.

CHK-004 — Knowledge, Experience and Product Complexity
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 7 requirement(s). 7 raised a concern (G4.1, G4.2, G4.3, G4.4, G4.5, G4.6, G4.7). The report asserts 'Moderate' knowledge; the risk profile questionnaire records 'No understanding / knowledge' against all three knowledge questions covering stocks and shares, EIS/VCTs, and pension planning. The report asserts 'Moderate' knowledge; the questionnaire records no investment experience in any product category other than cash/savings accounts in the last 5 years. The 'Investor Experience' section of the risk profile contains no additional notes, providing no qualitative basis on which a 'Moderate' grading could have been derived. 1 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G4.1] Client's stated product and investment knowledge level
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The suitability report asserts the client has a 'Moderate' level of knowledge and experience.
    File:     The risk profile questionnaire (category D) records 'No understanding / knowledge' against all three directly assessed knowledge questions: stocks and shares, Enterprise Investment Schemes/VCTs, and pension planning/income in retirement. The client has not invested in unit trusts, GIAs, stocks and shares, EIS/VCTs, or pension products in the last 5 years. The only investment experience recorded is cash/savings accounts, managed independently. No additional notes were recorded in the 'Investor Experience' section of the risk profile. The agreed risk level description (level 5 Balanced) states 'If you have some experience of investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters then you may be suited to a Balanced approach' — framed as a conditional, not a confirmed characteristic of this client. The fact find (category B) does not contain a graded knowledge assessment. No category C file note records any explanation of product complexity or confirms client comprehension.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts 'Moderate' knowledge; the risk profile questionnaire records 'No understanding / knowledge' against all three knowledge questions covering stocks and shares, EIS/VCTs, and pension planning.
      - The report asserts 'Moderate' knowledge; the questionnaire records no investment experience in any product category other than cash/savings accounts in the last 5 years.
      - The 'Investor Experience' section of the risk profile contains no additional notes, providing no qualitative basis on which a 'Moderate' grading could have been derived.
      - The agreed risk level description references having 'some experience of investments and a degree of understanding' as a condition for suitability at level 5 Balanced, but the file does not confirm this condition is met for this client.
      - No category C file note evidences that product complexity was explained to or understood by the client, despite the recommendation being a SIPP invested in two multi-asset funds with pension consolidation.
    The report describes the client as having 'Moderate' knowledge and experience. The only formal knowledge assessment on file is the risk profile questionnaire (category D), which records 'No understanding / knowledge' against every relevant knowledge question and records no investment experience in pension, equity or fund products in the last 5 years. This is a direct and unambiguous contradiction of the report's 'Moderate' assertion. The guard cautions that a report describing a client as comfortable while the fact find records 'limited' knowledge is a real mismatch; here the discrepancy is starker — the questionnaire records 'no' knowledge, not 'limited'. The agreed risk level narrative mentions 'some experience' as a condition for suitability at Balanced level 5, but this is descriptive of the risk level, not a confirmed characteristic attributed to this client. The Investor Experience section contains no supplementary notes that could provide a qualitative basis for upgrading the assessed knowledge level. No file note in category C records any explanation of product features or confirms client comprehension, which is a further concern given the complexity of a SIPP with pension consolidation and multi-asset fund investment. There is no vulnerability marker in the evidence that directly bears on this check, so no overlay adjustment is required. The mismatch between the report's 'Moderate' grading and the questionnaire's uniform 'No understanding / knowledge' responses constitutes a material contradiction between the report and the file, meeting the threshold for a High severity Potential Concern.

  [G4.2] Previous investment and pension experience
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The report (page 4, Knowledge & Experience section) states: (1) the client has previously taken out cash savings products; (2) the client has not taken out a stocks & shares investment by themselves; (3) the client has held group pensions for some time. The report does not assign a formal knowledge grade but describes the client as having pension experience through existing group pension holdings.
    File:     The risk profile report (category D) records the following questionnaire responses: 'Do you understand how pension planning and income in retirement works and do you know the risks associated with them?' — answer: 'No understanding / knowledge'. 'Have you invested in pension planning and income in retirement products within the last 5 years?' — answer: 'No'. 'Have you invested in Current Accounts, Savings/Deposit Accounts, Cash ISA, National Savings and Investments within the last 5 years?' — answer: 'Yes, by myself'. 'Have you invested in stocks and shares within the last 5 years?' — answer: 'No'. 'Have you invested in Unit Trusts and General Investment Accounts within the last 5 years?' — answer: 'No'. The agreed risk level for the recommendation is 5 (Balanced). The existing file evidences five workplace/personal pension plans held in the client's name (People's Pension, Scottish Widows, Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life). The recommendation is for an Aviva Platform Personal Pension invested in two balanced multi-asset funds (CT Universal MAP Balanced and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio) at 50% each.
    Discrepancies:
      - 1. The report states the client 'has held group pensions for some time', which implies pension experience. The risk profile questionnaire records 'No understanding / knowledge' of pension planning and income in retirement, and also records that the client has NOT invested in pension planning and income in retirement products within the last 5 years — directly contradicting both the implied experiential claim and the file evidence that five pension plans exist in the client's name.
      - 2. The report does not record any formal knowledge grade or investor-experience score, whereas the risk profile questionnaire provides explicit knowledge-level answers that are materially more negative (no understanding of pensions) than the report narrative implies.
      - 3. The report acknowledges no prior stocks & shares experience (consistent with the file's 'No' answer), but the recommendation involves multi-asset funds invested in equities — a level of complexity that the file records as outside the client's stated understanding, without any recorded explanation or comprehension check in the evidence pack.
      - 4. The risk profile report's 'Investor Experience' section contains no additional notes ('No additional notes were recorded'), providing no corroboration of the experiential narrative in the suitability report.
    The report's narrative that the client has 'held group pensions for some time' is factually supported by the existence of five pension plans in the case file. However, the false-positive guard explicitly states that holding a workplace pension is passive experience and that plan ownership is not evidence of knowledge. Applying that guard, the material answer for this check — where the recommendation is a pension — is the investor-experience questionnaire's explicit record of 'No understanding / knowledge' of pension planning and income in retirement. The report's framing implies meaningful pension experience, yet the formal knowledge assessment contradicts this. More significantly, the questionnaire records that the client has NOT invested in pension planning products in the last 5 years, which sits in tension with the existence of five live pension plans (though those may be legacy/inactive arrangements). The recommended product is an Aviva Platform Personal Pension in two balanced multi-asset equity funds — this is a moderately complex product for a client the file formally records as having no understanding of pension planning. There is no record of adviser explanations, client questions answered, or any comprehension check in the evidence (the Investor Experience notes section is blank). This combination — a complex pension product, a client formally recorded as having no understanding of pension planning, and no documented comprehension check — meets the Potential Concern threshold. The vulnerability overlay does not appear to be triggered by any specific vulnerability-related finding in the evidence that would alter this assessment, though the low financial literacy implied by the knowledge responses is itself relevant to product complexity suitability. Severity is assessed as High: the report's description of the client's pension knowledge (implied experience from group pension holding) contradicts the explicit fact-find/risk questionnaire finding of no understanding.

  [G4.3] Complexity of recommended products and investments, including risks and features
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report classifies the recommended Aviva Pension Portfolio / Personal Pension with Flexi Access Drawdown as 'Complex', driven by: pension freedoms and flexible access drawdown options; multiple fund choices within the platform; tax-efficient withdrawal strategies required; death benefit options and nominations; and Inheritance Tax planning considerations from April 2027. The product is a Personal Pension with FAD, with a transfer value of £110,185, invested 50% HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio and 50% CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund. The complexity classification is inferred by the extractor rather than explicitly stated as 'Complex' by the report.
    File:     The suitability report itself (passages P4, P17) explains FAD features including the 25% tax-free lump sum, remainder invested with flexible withdrawals taxed at marginal rate, death benefits before and after age 75, and IHT treatment from April 2027. Key Features document (P20, P1) confirms the Aviva Pension Portfolio supports flexi-access drawdown, multiple retirement options, fund choice, and death benefit nominations — corroborating that these features exist. The Terms and Conditions (P2, P3, P5, P7, P10, P11, P13, P14, P15) confirm the platform supports a wide range of investment options including commercial property, structured products, ETIs, and DIM arrangements. Fund factsheets (P8, P9, P21, P23, P24) confirm both recommended funds are multi-asset balanced funds rated 4 on the risk/reward scale — mainstream multi-asset collective investments. The SIPP research report (P16, P19) confirms investment selection, risk level 5 agreed, and performance data for both recommended funds. Policy research (P6, P12) confirms FAD and UFPLS are available on the recommended platform. Crucially, the file contains no fact-find or client knowledge/experience record grading the client's investment knowledge, no record of client questions asked or explanations given about the product's complex features, and no explicit knowledge/experience assessment form in the retrieved passages.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report's complexity classification ('Complex') is inferred by the extractor, not explicitly stated in the report text — the report describes FAD features at length but does not label the product 'Complex' in those terms.
      - No fact-find or client knowledge/experience grading is present in the retrieved passages — the report's implicit assertion that the client is suitable for a FAD/drawdown product cannot be corroborated from the file evidence provided.
      - No record of client questions, comprehension checks, or explanations of unusual features (IHT from April 2027, marginal rate taxation on drawdown withdrawals, annual allowance trigger from FAD) is evidenced in the retrieved passages.
      - The two recommended funds (HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio and CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund) are mainstream multi-asset balanced funds (risk rated 4, UK UCITS) — the product-level complexity drivers relate to the pension wrapper and drawdown mechanics rather than the underlying funds, which are not themselves complex instruments.
      - The file evidences a risk rating ambiguity (questionnaire indicated 4, agreed at 5) but no knowledge/experience level is recorded anywhere in the retrieved passages to contextualise whether a FAD arrangement is appropriate for this client's demonstrated understanding.
    The guard states that a mainstream personal pension in multi-asset funds is not complex and should not be escalated on product volume or value alone. The two recommended funds are mainstream multi-asset balanced funds (HSBC Global Strategy Balanced and CT Universal MAP Balanced, both UK UCITS, risk-rated 4) — these are not complex instruments in themselves. However, the pension wrapper recommended includes Flexi-Access Drawdown, which carries genuinely complex features: tax on withdrawals at marginal rate, the Money Purchase Annual Allowance trigger, IHT treatment changes from April 2027, death benefit elections, and the need for ongoing tax-efficient withdrawal strategy. The suitability report discusses these features (P4, P17) but the complexity classification is inferred rather than explicit. The more significant issue is that the retrieved passages contain no fact-find or client knowledge/experience record whatsoever. There is no grading of the client's investment knowledge, no record of prior pension or investment experience beyond the five existing pension arrangements visible in the report, and no evidence of comprehension checks or explanations of the FAD-specific features that make this product genuinely complex. The existence of five pre-existing pension arrangements does evidence some degree of prior engagement with pension products, but this falls short of corroborating that the client has sufficient knowledge and experience to understand FAD mechanics, particularly the IHT changes and marginal rate taxation implications. The severity guidance indicates 'Moderate' where there is no record of client questions or explanations where the product has unusual features — the IHT from April 2027 and MPAA trigger from FAD are unusual features, and no comprehension evidence is on file. The vulnerability overlay: no vulnerability indicators are present in the retrieved passages that would further affect this assessment. The outcome is Potential Concern at Moderate severity: the recommended product involves genuinely complex features beyond a standard personal pension, but the file lacks any client knowledge/experience record or comprehension evidence to corroborate suitability for those features.

  [G4.4] Recommended product is too complex for the stated level of knowledge, or the knowledge level has been misinterpreted
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The suitability report asserts the client has a 'Moderate' level of knowledge and experience, and that the recommended product is classified as 'Complex'. The report describes the client as a Balanced investor (risk rating 5) with some experience of investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters, noting the client's experience is 'limited to your workplace pension arrangements'. The report states the client wants to keep the investment strategy 'fairly simple' and that multi-asset funds have been recommended partly because they provide a 'professionally managed, diversified investment solution'. The report records that the client understands pension freedoms and prefers the flexibility of Flexi Access Drawdown.
    File:     The risk profile questionnaire (category D) records the client as having: 'No understanding / knowledge' of stocks and shares and their risks; 'No understanding / knowledge' of Enterprise Investment Schemes and Venture Capital Trusts; 'No understanding / knowledge' of pension planning and income in retirement. The client has invested in current accounts/savings/deposit accounts/cash ISAs/NSI within the last 5 years by themselves, but has not invested in unit trusts, GIAs, stocks and shares, EIS/VCTs, or pension planning products within the last 5 years. No additional notes were recorded under 'Investor Experience'. The risk profile report describes the natural risk level (4 – Cautious Balanced) as suited to someone with 'limited or moderate experience of investments'. The agreed risk level description (5 – Balanced) states it is suited to someone who 'have some experience of investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters'. No category C record of client comprehension of the complex product features is present in the pack.
    Discrepancies:
      - The suitability report asserts a 'Moderate' knowledge and experience level, but the risk profile questionnaire records 'No understanding / knowledge' for stocks and shares, pension planning and income in retirement products — all directly relevant to the recommended pension and fund investments.
      - The report states the client has 'some experience in investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters' (reproducing the Balanced investor description), but the fact-find questionnaire records no investment in pension planning or investment products within the last 5 years and no understanding of how those products work.
      - The report classifies the recommended product as 'Complex', yet the client is recorded in the file as having no knowledge of pensions or investment products, no recent experience of them, and expressly wanting to keep the strategy 'fairly simple'.
      - No category C evidence of client comprehension or explanation of complex product features (e.g. Flexi Access Drawdown, multi-asset fund structure, pension consolidation, drawdown mechanics) is present in the pack despite the product being classified as Complex.
      - No additional notes were recorded under 'Investor Experience' in the risk profile report, leaving the 'Moderate' assertion in the suitability report entirely unsupported by documented adviser notes.
      - The natural risk profile questionnaire result (level 4 – Cautious Balanced) is described as suited to someone with 'limited or moderate experience', which is consistent with the file's recorded knowledge responses, but the report upgrades the description to level 5 – Balanced, whose precondition ('some experience of investments and a degree of understanding') contradicts the questionnaire knowledge responses.
    The suitability report asserts 'Moderate' knowledge and experience and classifies the recommended product as 'Complex'. The file evidence materially contradicts the 'Moderate' assertion. The risk profile questionnaire (P9) records 'No understanding / knowledge' for stocks and shares and — critically — for pension planning and income in retirement, which is the exact product domain being recommended. The client has no recent experience of pension or investment products beyond cash-based savings. The 'Investor Experience' section of the risk profile report (P10) contains no additional notes, providing no corroborating narrative. The suitability report's description of the client as having 'some experience in investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters' (P7) reproduces the Balanced investor profile description, but the guard warns that a risk-profile description carries preconditions about the client's experience: where those preconditions are contradicted by the file, the description cannot serve as evidence of knowledge. Here, the preconditions are directly contradicted by P9. A complex product — a personal pension with Flexi Access Drawdown flexibility, consolidating multiple arrangements into a multi-asset CIP — is being recommended to a client the file records as having no understanding of pension planning. No category C record of the client's comprehension of the complex features exists in the pack. The adviser's statement 'you understand the pension freedoms' (P16) is an assertion by the adviser, not corroborated evidence, and the guard requires a category C record for corroboration. The report's own acknowledgement that the client's experience is 'limited to your workplace pension arrangements' (P7) is more consistent with the file's evidence of limited/no knowledge than with a 'Moderate' classification. Taken together, a complex product is being recommended to a client the file records as having no relevant knowledge of pension or investment products, with no documented comprehension check, and the knowledge level stated in the report is directly contradicted by the questionnaire responses. This meets the highest severity criterion. No vulnerability factors in the pack are identified as bearing on this specific requirement beyond the knowledge gap itself.

  [G4.5] Evidence that the client can evaluate the risks and features of a complex product, based on their questions and comprehension
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The suitability report states (page 4, Knowledge & Experience section): 'You feel that you are comfortable with how investments work and have a reasonable understanding of potential risks & rewards'. This is the sole understanding evidence cited in the canonical model for this client.
    File:     The fact find (category B) records the client's name as Alan Sullivan and notes a video call meeting on 18/09/2025, but contains no graded knowledge or experience level and no record of questions asked or comprehension demonstrated. The risk profile report (category D, passages P14 and P16) has explicit sections for 'Investor Experience' and 'Agreed risk level' but both record 'No additional notes were recorded'. The file notes (category C, passage P2) describe the client's experience as 'limited to your workplace pension arrangements' and confirm awareness that 'returns are not guaranteed' and that 'investment values can rise as well as fall', but record no specific questions asked by the client about product features or risks. There is no category C note recording client questions about the recommended Aviva Platform Personal Pension with CIP (a multi-fund, platform-based arrangement involving two funds — HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio and CT Universal MAP Balanced — overseen by an investment committee), nor any record of the client demonstrating comprehension of its features or risks beyond general comfort with investment risk.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts the client has a 'reasonable understanding of potential risks & rewards' (a moderate knowledge level), but the risk profile report investor experience section records 'No additional notes were recorded', providing no corroboration of any specific level of understanding.
      - The file notes (P2) characterise the client's experience as 'limited to your workplace pension arrangements', which is materially narrower than the report's assertion of a 'reasonable understanding', suggesting the report may overstate the client's knowledge relative to the file.
      - No record of client questions or explanations about the recommended product's specific features (CIP structure, two-fund allocation, platform mechanics, investment pathway considerations) appears anywhere in the case file, despite the product having unusual features (CIP overlay, split fund approach, investment committee governance).
      - The report does not record any comprehension check or explanation given to the client regarding how the CIP works or how the two-fund blend achieves his risk profile, yet the product is structured with layered complexity beyond a standard single multi-asset fund.
    The recommended product is an Aviva Platform Personal Pension invested in a Centralised Investment Proposition (CIP) comprising two funds (HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio 50% and CT Universal MAP Balanced 50%), with active oversight by an investment committee and a layered charge structure. The suitability report itself explains at length (passages P4, P5, P7) why this CIP approach is more complex than a single multi-asset fund, citing diversification, active governance and cost efficiency as distinguishing features. This is therefore a product with non-standard features that warrants evidence of client comprehension. Against this, the only understanding evidence in the canonical model is a single self-assessed statement that the client 'feels comfortable with how investments work and has a reasonable understanding of potential risks and rewards'. The file notes (P2) record experience as 'limited to your workplace pension arrangements' — a characterisation that sits below the moderate understanding the report asserts. The risk profile report (P14, P16) records no investor experience notes and no notes on how the agreed risk level was reached. There is no record anywhere in the case file of the client asking questions about the CIP structure, the two-fund blend, investment pathways (which the report itself addresses and discounts), or any other product-specific feature. The guard acknowledges that absence of recorded questions is not proof of non-understanding, but instructs that this absence should be weighted by product complexity. Given the product's complexity, the absence of any corroborating comprehension evidence — combined with the file's own description of the client's experience as limited — constitutes a material evidence gap. The discrepancy between 'limited to workplace pension arrangements' (file) and 'reasonable understanding of potential risks and rewards' (report) is not explained by any guard. No vulnerability concerns in the file (capacity for loss references in P2 are limited to financial impact, not cognitive or communication needs) materially alter this finding. The outcome is Potential Concern at High severity, because the report's description of the client's knowledge is not corroborated by the fact find and risk profile, and a complex product has been recommended to a client the file records as having limited investment experience, with no documented evidence of comprehension of its specific features.

  [G4.6] Existing holdings evidencing investment and pension experience
  Potential Concern — severity Low — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts: (1) the client has held cash savings products; (2) the client has NOT held stocks & shares investments by themselves; (3) the client has held group pensions for some time.     Discrepancies:
      - The report states the client 'confirmed' holding cash savings products, but no factfind or knowledge/experience questionnaire is present in the file to corroborate this confirmation.
      - The report states the client has 'not taken out a stocks & shares investment by yourself', which is consistent with the file showing only pension holdings, but again no factfind is present to confirm this was explicitly stated by the client.
      - The Standard Life policy value at 30 September 2025 is £2,994.30 per [P4], whereas the canonical model records £3,002.00 — a minor discrepancy of ~£8.

The file corroborates the existence of all five pension arrangements through policy documents [P2, P3, P4, P5, P8, P11, P12, P13, P14], confirming the fund names and holdings stated in the report. This supports the assertion that the client holds group/personal pensions. However, the assertion regarding cash savings products is sourced solely from the suitability report itself ('You confirmed that you have previously taken out cash savings products') with no corroborating factfind or separate evidence in the file. No factfind, client questionnaire or knowledge and experience record exists in the retrieved passages to independently verify what the client confirmed. The auto-enrolment guard is relevant: the People's Pension (OccupationalDC) and Scottish Widows Master Trust arrangements are workplace pensions and likely auto-enrolled, meaning their existence evidences ownership rather than active investment experience. The Zurich plan (Managed AP, value ~£103k, no contributions in last 10 years per [P8]) and the Standard Life plan are personal pensions, providing stronger evidence of longer-term pension holding. The minor value discrepancy on Standard Life (£2,994.30 in file vs £3,002.00 in report) is noted but is low significance for this check. The absence of any factfind or knowledge/experience assessment means the cash savings assertion and the framing of pension holding as demonstrating active experience cannot be independently corroborated from the file. This represents a moderate gap: the pension holdings are evidenced, but the broader knowledge and experience narrative rests on the report alone. No vulnerability factors in the retrieved passages appear to materially affect this specific check.

  [G4.7] Adequacy of the rationale supporting a complex recommendation
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report asserts the recommended product (Aviva Platform Personal Pension) is Complex, with complexity drivers including pension freedoms and flexible access drawdown options, multiple fund choices within platform, tax-efficient withdrawal strategies, death benefit options and nominations, and Inheritance Tax planning considerations from April 2027. The report provides rationale covering: employer contributions (retain People's Pension), small pots encashment tax treatment, lower costs, lifestyling risk, greater investment choice, adviser charging facilitation, ongoing monitoring, multi-asset fund access (Zurich limitation), and enhanced retirement flexibility.
    File:     The file contains: (1) Risk profile report [P22] recording a natural ATR of 4 (Cautious Balanced) with experience described as 'limited or moderate'; (2) File notes [P8] stating 'your experience is limited to your workplace pension arrangements' and that the client 'understand[s] that returns are not guaranteed'; (3) File notes [P8/P10] providing adviser rationale for discounting alternatives (Scottish Widows, Aviva internal, Zurich blend, Standard Life) on grounds of risk rating mismatch, fund performance, and cost; (4) The Aviva Platform Key Features [P2] and illustration [P7] confirm the product's flexible drawdown, fund choice, and death benefit features; (5) HSBC KIID [P1] and fund factsheet [P5] cover the recommended funds; (6) Policy research [P3/P4] shows platform shortlisting. The risk profile report [P14] records no additional notes under 'Investor Experience'. The risk profile report [P13] records no additional notes under 'Agreed risk level'. The fact find [P20] records the meeting as a video call.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts the client has sufficient knowledge and experience to support a complex product recommendation, but the risk profile report [P22] formally records the client's natural ATR as risk level 4 (Cautious Balanced) described as having 'limited or moderate experience of investments', and file notes [P8] explicitly state experience is 'limited to your workplace pension arrangements'. No upgrade of the knowledge/experience assessment is documented.
      - The Investor Experience section of the risk profile report [P14] records 'No additional notes were recorded', meaning there is no documented exploration or corroboration of the client's understanding of the complex features (drawdown, IHT from 2027, tax-efficient withdrawal strategies, death benefit nominations).
      - The Agreed risk level section of the risk profile report [P13] records 'No additional notes were recorded', providing no documented basis for the override from ATR 4 to ATR 5 in terms of knowledge/experience considerations.
      - The report identifies IHT planning considerations from April 2027 as a complexity driver, but no evidence in the file demonstrates the client's understanding of or experience with IHT planning or pension IHT changes.
      - The report identifies tax-efficient withdrawal strategies as a complexity driver, but beyond the small pots encashment calculation, no evidence in the file demonstrates the client's understanding of drawdown tax strategy.
      - The file notes [P8] acknowledge the client's experience is limited, yet the report presents a complex product without any documented evidence that comprehension of complex features was tested or explained and understood by the client.
    The report recommends a product it classifies as Complex, with five identified complexity drivers including pension freedoms/drawdown, IHT planning, and tax-efficient withdrawal strategies. Under the decision logic, a complex product recommendation requires that comprehension is corroborated in category C (file notes/meeting records). The file notes in [P8] explicitly record that the client's experience is 'limited to your workplace pension arrangements', which aligns with the risk profile report's [P22] formal classification of the client as 'Cautious Balanced' with 'limited or moderate experience'. The Investor Experience section of the risk profile [P14] contains no additional notes, meaning no documented exploration of the client's understanding of complex features was recorded at the time of the risk assessment. While the file notes [P10] provide adviser-side rationale for discounting alternatives, this addresses switching suitability rather than the client's comprehension of the complex product being recommended. The report does include client-specific rationale for the recommendation (e.g. lifestyling risk, multi-asset fund access limitations at Zurich, lower costs), which is corroborated by the file notes [P10]. However, none of the file evidence demonstrates that the client's understanding of the complex features — particularly drawdown mechanics, IHT from 2027, and tax-efficient withdrawal strategies — was assessed, explained, or confirmed. The vulnerability overlay: the file notes [P8] note limited investment experience but no other vulnerability characteristics are recorded that would further aggravate or mitigate this finding. The discrepancy between the complexity of the recommendation and the documented limited experience, combined with the absence of any comprehension evidence in category C for the complex features, constitutes a Potential Concern at Moderate severity (no record of client questions or explanations where the product has unusual features). The rationale in the report for the switch itself is present and client-specific, so this is not the highest severity level, but the absence of documented comprehension evidence for the complex features (IHT, drawdown strategy, death benefits) in the supporting file is a material gap.

CHK-005 — Financial Resilience, Affordability and Liquidity
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 8 requirement(s). 8 raised a concern (G5.1, G5.2, G5.3, G5.4, G5.5, G5.6, G5.7, G5.8). The report states combined income 'comfortably meets' household expenditure of approximately £1,700/month, but the fact find [P1] records a negative monthly cash flow of -£288 (income £1,430, expenditure £1,718), directly contradicting the affordability assertion. The report states expenditure of 'approximately £1,700 per month'; the fact find records £1,718 per month — a difference of £18/month. This is minor but the rounding conceals the deficit. The report states pension funds 'totalling approximately £106,000'; the evidenced total across all five arrangements is £116,998.47, and the recommended transfer value is £110,185 — neither matches the £106,000 figure, an understatement of at least £4,185 against the transfer value. 6 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G5.1] Affordability of initial and ongoing commitments
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report asserts: (1) initial advice fee of 3% = £3,305.55, paid directly by the pension schemes on transfer; (2) ongoing service charge of 1% p.a. = circa £1,101.85 for the first year, paid via Aviva Platform pension scheme; (3) combined income comfortably meets household expenditure of approximately £1,700 per month; (4) savings of around £6,000; (5) no outstanding liabilities; (6) pension funds totalling approximately £106,000 form the basis of retirement provision; (7) affordability is asserted as satisfactory on the basis that income covers expenditure with savings and pension funds available.
    File:     The fact find [P1] records total net monthly income of £1,430 and total monthly expenditure of £1,718, producing a monthly disposable income of NEGATIVE £288. The cashflow model [P5/P8] uses £15,600 p.a. (£300 weekly) from CDH Recruitment, £1,040 p.a. (£20 weekly) from Sea Cadets, and £13,200 p.a. (£1,100 monthly) from Kim — combined annual income approximately £29,840 or circa £2,487/month, against which the cashflow modelling shows surpluses in the proposed scenario. Savings of £6,000 are corroborated [P1, P5]. The suitability report states expenditure of approximately £1,700/month; the fact find records £1,718/month. The initial fee of £3,305.55 at 3% is corroborated [P7]. The ongoing fee of £1,101.85 at 1% is corroborated [P22]. The report states pension funds of approximately £106,000; the canonical model and evidence total £116,998.47 across all five arrangements, with the recommended transfer value stated as £110,185 [P19]. No explicit emergency fund discussion or quantified liquidity reserve beyond the £6,000 savings is evidenced. The fees are taken from the pension fund, not from income [P7, P22].
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states combined income 'comfortably meets' household expenditure of approximately £1,700/month, but the fact find [P1] records a negative monthly cash flow of -£288 (income £1,430, expenditure £1,718), directly contradicting the affordability assertion.
      - The report states expenditure of 'approximately £1,700 per month'; the fact find records £1,718 per month — a difference of £18/month. This is minor but the rounding conceals the deficit.
      - The report states pension funds 'totalling approximately £106,000'; the evidenced total across all five arrangements is £116,998.47, and the recommended transfer value is £110,185 — neither matches the £106,000 figure, an understatement of at least £4,185 against the transfer value.
      - The fact find monthly income of £1,430 appears to reflect only one income source (possibly John's HGV income alone), whereas the cashflow model includes Kim's income of £1,100/month and Sea Cadets £20/week. The combined household income used in the cashflow model (approx. £2,487/month) is not reflected in the fact find cash flow section, creating an unresolved inconsistency between the two documents.
      - The emergency fund of £6,000 is identified but there is no analysis of whether it is sufficient given the negative monthly surplus shown in the fact find — the report asserts affordability without addressing this.
      - No explicit commentary in the suitability report addresses the negative cash flow shown in the fact find, nor reconciles the fact find figures with the cashflow model assumptions.
    The most material concern is the direct contradiction between the suitability report's affordability assertion — that combined income 'comfortably meets' household expenditure — and the fact find [P1], which records a net monthly cash flow of negative £288 (income £1,430 vs expenditure £1,718). This is not a rounding difference; it is a structural deficit. The report's affordability claim appears to rely on a combined household income figure that includes Kim's income and Sea Cadets income (as used in the cashflow model at approximately £2,487/month combined), but this combined figure is not what appears in the fact find cash flow section. The fact find section records only £1,430/month net income, which may represent only John's income, and £1,718/month expenditure. The suitability report does not explain this discrepancy or state that the affordability assessment is based on combined household income rather than the fact find's recorded monthly figure. The cashflow model [P5/P8] does include both incomes and shows surpluses, which could support affordability if the fact find income figure is incomplete — but the report does not make this reconciliation explicit, and the fact find cash flow section as recorded shows a deficit. The fees guard applies: the initial fee (£3,305.55) and ongoing fee (£1,101.85) are both taken from the pension fund, not from income, so they do not need to be tested against monthly surplus. The transfer of existing pension money is also guarded as a non-new affordability commitment. However, the underlying income/expenditure consistency failure is not guarded and represents a Moderate-to-High concern: the affordability basis stated in the report is contradicted by the fact find's own cash flow figures, and the report provides no reconciliation. The pension fund figure of approximately £106,000 is also an understatement relative to both the total evidenced value (£116,998.47) and the transfer value (£110,185), though this does not directly affect affordability of the recommendation. The emergency fund of £6,000 is identified but not quantified as sufficient relative to the demonstrated income shortfall. The vulnerability overlay does not appear to introduce additional material factors beyond those already assessed, as no specific vulnerability characteristics are recorded that would amplify the affordability concern further than the income deficit itself. Overall, the primary concern is the unreconciled negative monthly cash flow in the fact find versus the affordability assertion in the report, which constitutes a material inconsistency requiring clarification.
