  [G5.2] Financial situation plus cashflow supports the recommendation
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   Total monthly net income £4,486.67 (C1 HGV £1,300 + C1 Sea Cadets £86.67 + C1 State Pension £1,000 + C2 Employment £1,100); total monthly expenditure £1,700; monthly surplus £2,786.67; total assets £6,000 cash (no liabilities, net worth £6,000). Cashflow modelled to age 100 with no shortfall projected on baseline. High-inflation stress test at 6% — no shortfall. Combined market crash −30% plus 6% inflation — no shortfall. Assumptions: general expenditure 2%, savings 2%, state pensions 2.5%, Aviva personal pensions 5%, 0% surplus saved.
    File:     Fact find (P2) records total net monthly income £1,430 and total monthly expenditure £1,718, giving a monthly disposable income of −£288. The fact find income breakdown (P21) shows basic income £1,200/month and state pension £230/month for one client only, totalling £1,430. The cashflow model (P12, P8) shows first-year combined employment income £29,840 p.a. (JS £16,640 + KS £13,200) and state pension £11,973 p.a. for JS, total regular income £41,813 p.a. (~£3,485/month). Monthly expenditure in the model is derived from total regular expenditure of £24,001 p.a. (~£2,000/month). Cash savings confirmed at £6,000 (P14). No liabilities recorded (P14). Cashflow baseline scenario confirms no shortfall to age 100 with surpluses throughout (P5, P11, P22). Proposed scenario also shows no shortfall with growing liquid assets and accessible pensions (P9, P12). Stress tests corroborated: 6% inflation and −30% combined crash both show no shortfall (P6). Inflation assumptions corroborated: general 2%, state pension 2.5%, 0% surplus saved (P17, P4). Aviva personal pensions 5% corroborated (P4). However, the Baseline scenario cashflow (P11) shows a deficit appearing from age 100 (2056 onwards for one client), with liquid assets and accessible pensions declining from £122,976 to £111,171 by age 104, implying a shortfall beyond age 100 in the baseline — whereas the report states no shortfall to age 100.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states total monthly net income of £4,486.67; fact find (P2) records only £1,430 total net monthly income. The report figure appears to incorporate Kim's income (£1,100/month) and state pension estimates not captured in the fact find's single-client income summary, but the fact find figure and the report figure cannot be reconciled without further information on how the second client's income was recorded.
      - Report states monthly expenditure of £1,700; fact find (P2) records calculated total monthly household expenditure of £1,718 — a difference of £18/month. The cashflow model uses an annual expenditure of £24,001 (P8/P12), equivalent to ~£2,000/month, which also differs from both the report's £1,700 and the fact find's £1,718.
      - Report states monthly surplus of £2,786.67 (income £4,486.67 minus expenditure £1,700); fact find (P2) shows a monthly disposable income of −£288 (income £1,430 minus expenditure £1,718). These figures are entirely inconsistent.
      - Report states C1 HGV income of £1,300/month (£300/week); cashflow model first-year income statement (P12) shows JS employment income of £16,640 p.a. = £1,387/month, not £1,300/month (£300 × 52/12 = £1,300; £16,640/12 = £1,387 — a £87/month difference, possibly reflecting 52-week annualisation vs the model using a different annualisation).
      - Report states C1 State Pension of £1,000/month; cashflow model (P12, P11) shows state pension £11,973 p.a. = £997.75/month — minor rounding difference but within 5% tolerance. Fact find (P21) shows state pension £230/month for the recorded client — significantly lower than the report's £1,000/month.
      - Report states C2 (Kim) employment income of £1,100/month; cashflow model (P12) shows KS employment income £13,200 p.a. = £1,100/month — consistent. However, this income does not appear at all in the fact find (P2/P21), which shows only £1,430 total income.
      - Report says cashflow runs to age 100 with no shortfall; the Baseline scenario table (P11) shows deficits appearing from age 100/96 (year 2056 onwards), with required withdrawals and declining liquid assets to age 104/100 in the baseline. The proposed scenario (P12) also shows deficits from age 101/97. The report's 'no shortfall' claim appears to refer to no shortfall of liquid assets/accessible pensions reaching zero, but the model does record in-year deficits beyond age 100.
      - The emergency fund of £6,000 cash is noted in the report and confirmed in the model (P14/P23). However, the cashflow model shows liquid assets declining only from the surplus account (which starts at £0), and the £6,000 cash is modelled as a static savings account — the adequacy of £6,000 as an emergency reserve for a couple, relative to monthly expenditure of £1,700–£2,000, is asserted but not explicitly discussed in the suitability report.
      - Fact find (P2) income total of £1,430 includes only one client's income (basic income £1,200 + state pension £230), which does not match any income figure in the suitability report or cashflow model, suggesting the fact find may be incomplete or not updated to reflect the joint household position used in the cashflow.
    The suitability report presents a household financial position with total monthly net income of £4,486.67, expenditure of £1,700 and a surplus of £2,786.67. The cashflow model (proposed scenario, P12/P8) uses first-year combined income of £41,813 p.a. (~£3,485/month) against expenditure of £24,001 p.a. (~£2,000/month), producing an annual surplus of approximately £17,812 (~£1,484/month). Neither the cashflow model's income/expenditure figures nor the fact find figures match the report's stated figures precisely. The most significant concern is that the fact find (P2) records a total monthly disposable income of −£288, which is wholly inconsistent with the report's claimed surplus of £2,786.67. The likely explanation is that the fact find only captured one client's income (JS: basic income £1,200 + state pension £230 = £1,430) and did not properly record Kim's employment income (£1,100/month) or the full state pension entitlements. This is not explained or reconciled in the suitability report. The report's income figures are partially corroborated by the cashflow model (Kim's £1,100/month employment income matches the model's £13,200 p.a.; JS state pension ~£1,000/month is broadly consistent with £11,973 p.a. in the model), but the fact find — as the primary fact-recording document — does not support the report's income position. The expenditure discrepancy (report £1,700/month vs fact find £1,718/month vs model ~£2,000/month) is a moderate but real inconsistency. The cashflow model expenditure of £24,001 p.a. includes Tax & NI of £3,385, which explains part of the difference from the pre-tax expenditure figures, but the household expenditure line items total approximately £20,616 p.a. (~£1,718/month) — consistent with the fact find but not the report's £1,700. The stress tests (6% inflation, −30% crash) are corroborated by evidence (P6). The 'no shortfall' conclusion is broadly supported by the proposed scenario cashflow data (P9, P12), where liquid assets and accessible pensions remain positive throughout, though in-year deficits do appear beyond age 100 in both baseline and proposed scenarios — this is beyond the stated modelling horizon of age 100 so does not constitute a contradiction. The £6,000 emergency fund is confirmed by the model (P14) but its adequacy is not quantified relative to expenditure needs. The most material concern is the irreconcilable gap between the fact find's income/cashflow position (deficit of −£288/month) and the report's surplus (£2,786.67/month), with no explanation or cross-reference in the suitability report. This is a moderate-to-high concern because the cashflow model's conclusions rest on income figures that are not reflected in the fact find. The vulnerability overlay: no specific vulnerability indicators in the evidence affect this particular financial resilience assessment materially beyond what is already noted.

  [G5.3] Emergency fund and liquidity needs, foreseeable and unforeseeable
  Potential Concern — severity Low — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts the client holds £6,000 in liquid cash as an emergency fund (page 3). It states the client has confirmed they retain sufficient liquid capital to cover any unexpected emergencies, giving reassurance as they progress with retirement planning. It further states the client is comfortable with the concept as they have liquid cash should they require instant access funds (page 8). The required amount of the emergency fund is not stated in the report — sufficiency is asserted by client confirmation only. Access to pension funds is noted as taking 10–15 working days for administration, disinvestment and banking processes.
    File:     The fact find [P6] records cash/savings of £6,000.00 (dated 22/09/2025). The cashflow model proposed scenario [P7] confirms liquid assets opening at £6,000 in 2025, declining gradually (e.g. £5,956 in 2026, £5,912 in 2027) as the cash reserve is slowly drawn upon to meet a small annual deficit in liquid assets. The baseline scenario [P16] shows the same pattern of declining liquid assets from £6,000. The fact find [P14] records a current monthly cash flow deficit: total net monthly income £1,430, total monthly expenditure £1,718, giving a monthly disposable income of -£288. No separate emergency fund requirement or target is quantified anywhere in the evidence. No document corroborates the assertion that £6,000 is sufficient; sufficiency is only asserted via client confirmation.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts the emergency fund is confirmed sufficient, but provides no quantified required amount against which £6,000 can be tested — the required amount is absent from both the report and the evidence.
      - The fact find records a monthly cash flow deficit of -£288 (income £1,430 vs expenditure £1,718), which means the client is already in a net deficit position before any retirement transition. The report does not address this deficit or explain how it is reconciled with the assertion of sufficient liquid capital.
      - The cashflow model shows liquid assets declining from £6,000 throughout the plan in both baseline and proposed scenarios, indicating the emergency fund is being gradually eroded rather than preserved — this is not addressed in the report's liquidity commentary.
      - The report states the client has liquid cash should they require instant access funds, but the pension access commentary (10–15 working days) relates to pension drawdown, not the emergency fund — no separate accessible non-pension liquid reserve above £6,000 is evidenced.
      - The income figure used in the cashflow model [P3] is £15,600 p/a (£300 weekly) from employment plus £1,040 p/a Sea Cadets, whereas the fact find [P18] records employed income of £1,200 monthly net — these differ and create uncertainty about the income base supporting the liquidity assertion.
      - No foreseeable future expenditure or planned large outgoings are addressed in the report's liquidity section beyond the standard retirement transition.
    The £6,000 emergency fund figure is corroborated by the fact find [P6] and the cashflow model opening position [P7], so the figure itself is consistent across documents. However, two material concerns arise. First, the report asserts sufficiency solely by client confirmation with no quantified required amount — this satisfies the 'unquantified' guard condition and is recorded as such, but the guard directs a low severity finding rather than dismissal. Second, and more substantively, the fact find reveals a current monthly cash flow deficit of -£288 [P14], meaning the client's income does not cover expenditure before any retirement adjustment. The cashflow model confirms liquid assets will gradually decline from £6,000 throughout the plan [P7][P16]. Neither the report's emergency fund commentary nor its liquidity section addresses this deficit or explains how £6,000 is adequate when it is being slowly eroded and the client has no evidenced surplus income to replenish it. The income figures also show a discrepancy between the fact find (£1,200/month net from CDH Recruitment [P18]) and the cashflow model (£300/week = £1,300/month [P3]), adding uncertainty to the affordability base. There is no evidence of competing claims on the £6,000 beyond the cashflow erosion shown in the model. No vulnerability factors are identified in the evidence that would materially alter this assessment beyond what is already captured. The combination of an unquantified required reserve, a demonstrated monthly deficit, and confirmed gradual erosion of the only liquid asset means the report's assertion of sufficient liquidity is not fully supported by the evidence.

  [G5.4] Initial and ongoing commitments are unaffordable based on income, expenditure and cashflow
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts: (1) initial advice fee of 3% of amount invested = £3,305.55 (one-off, page 17); (2) ongoing adviser charge of 1% per annum = circa £1,101.85 for the first year (page 17); (3) combined income comfortably meets household expenditure of approximately £1,700 per month; (4) savings of around £6,000 with no outstanding liabilities; (5) pension funds totalling approximately £106,000; (6) derived monthly surplus of £2,786.67. Affordability is asserted as satisfied.
    File:     The Fact Find (P1, P2) records total net monthly income of £1,430.00 (basic employment £1,200 + state pension £230) and total monthly expenditure of £1,718.00, producing a monthly disposable income of NEGATIVE £288.00. The cashflow planning tool (P11) shows annual employment income of £16,640 for one client and £13,200 combined, with total regular expenditure of £24,001 in the first year of plan. The proposed scenario (P21) shows liquid assets (savings) of £6,000, consistent with the report. The Fact Find (P15) records the client's required retirement income as £1,700 net per month, consistent with the report's expenditure figure. No document in the pack corroborates a monthly surplus of £2,786.67 or any positive monthly surplus. The Fact Find explicitly records a monthly deficit of £288.00. The initial fee of £3,305.55 and ongoing charge of £1,101.85 are not corroborated by any figure in the retrieved passages.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts combined income comfortably meets household expenditure of approximately £1,700 per month and implies a positive surplus; the Fact Find records total net monthly income of £1,430 against total monthly expenditure of £1,718, producing a deficit of £288 per month — not a surplus.
      - The report derives a monthly surplus of £2,786.67; no document in the pack supports this figure. The Fact Find records a deficit of £288.00 per month.
      - The report states household expenditure is approximately £1,700 per month; the Fact Find records total monthly household expenditure of £1,718 — a minor difference but the report's characterisation of income 'comfortably meeting' this is contradicted by the fact find deficit.
      - The report states income comfortably meets expenditure with no outstanding liabilities; the Fact Find cashflow section shows a negative monthly disposable income, contradicting the 'comfortably meets' assertion.
      - The initial fee of £3,305.55 (3% of £110,185) and ongoing charge of £1,101.85 (1% p.a.) are asserted as affordable but no positive surplus is evidenced to fund these charges — the initial fee would need to come from savings of £6,000, which would substantially deplete the emergency reserve.
      - The report references pension funds totalling approximately £106,000; the Fact Find and canonical model record individual plan values totalling £116,998.47 and the proposed scenario shows a new Aviva pension of £110,185, neither matching £106,000.
      - The cashflow planning baseline (P11) shows annual employment income of £16,640 for one party and combined income of £29,840 — these annualised figures are inconsistent with the Fact Find's monthly net income of £1,430 (£17,160 p.a.) recorded for the same client, suggesting different gross/net or dual-client figures are being conflated.
    The central affordability assertion in the suitability report — that combined income comfortably meets household expenditure — is directly contradicted by the Fact Find, which records a monthly deficit of £288. The report's derived surplus of £2,786.67 finds no support anywhere in the evidence pack and is irreconcilable with the Fact Find figures. The guard regarding employment income ceasing within the term is relevant: the cashflow tool (P13, P21) shows retirement events planned within one to two years, which would further reduce income. The initial advice fee of £3,305.55 is not corroborated numerically in the passages, and given that savings are only £6,000, paying this fee would consume over half the liquid emergency reserve. The ongoing charge of £1,101.85 p.a. cannot be funded from a evidenced monthly surplus because no positive monthly surplus is demonstrated; the Fact Find shows a deficit. The report's affordability basis is therefore not supported by the evidence and is actively contradicted by it. The vulnerability overlay: the file records retirement approaching within two years and income that 'typically' varies (P3 internal inconsistency noted in extraction); this income variability and proximity of retirement heighten the affordability concern as future income is at risk of falling further. No material vulnerability characteristic is identified in the passages that independently affects this check beyond what is already captured.

  [G5.5] Emergency funds not taken into account, or depleted or unavailable when required
  Potential Concern — severity Low
    Report:   The suitability report asserts that liquidity and access needs are funded from 'Pension'. There is no explicit emergency fund shortfall captured in the canonical model. The report does not separately quantify an emergency fund or address its sufficiency in the extracted model.
    File:     The cashflow planning files show liquid assets (cash savings) of £6,000 at plan inception in 2025, held in a Cash Account owned by JS. The proposed scenario shows liquid assets declining slowly from £6,000 in 2025 to approximately £5,912 by 2027, with pensions remaining fully accessible throughout. The surplus income percentage saved is 0%, meaning no surplus is being added to liquid reserves. Cashflow statements show consistent in-year surpluses (around £20,000+) and no deficits requiring drawdown from liquid assets. The pension assets are all classified as 'Accessible' throughout the plan horizon, with no Non-Accessible pension amounts shown.
    Discrepancies:
      - The canonical model records liquidity/access needs as funded from 'Pension' only, with no mention of the £6,000 cash savings as an emergency fund or its adequacy being assessed.
      - No emergency fund quantum or sufficiency assessment is present in the extracted report model; the £6,000 cash figure appears only in the cashflow planning files.
      - The suitability report does not address whether £6,000 is an adequate emergency reserve, or how many months of expenditure it represents (total regular expenditure is approximately £20,616 p/a per the proposed scenario, implying £6,000 covers roughly 3.5 months — not explicitly acknowledged in the report).
      - Surplus income percentage saved is set to 0%, meaning no surplus is being directed to build or replenish liquid reserves, yet the report does not discuss this in the context of emergency fund adequacy.
      - The liquid asset balance gradually declines from £6,000 to approximately £5,573 by 2035 in the proposed scenario, suggesting mild erosion of the cash reserve over time, which is not discussed in the report.
    The recommendation involves a pension consolidation with no new cash being drawn from liquid savings — the transfer is between pension wrappers. The guard therefore applies: the pension transfer itself does not deplete the emergency fund. However, the check requires the report to address emergency fund provision and access to liquid assets. The case file evidences only £6,000 in a Cash Account as the liquid reserve. Against total regular expenditure of approximately £20,616 p/a, this represents roughly 3.5 months of expenses — a borderline emergency fund. The cashflow model shows this sum declining slowly and no surplus being saved (0% surplus saved assumption), meaning the reserve is not being replenished. Critically, the suitability report does not quantify the emergency fund, does not assert it as sufficient, and does not discuss it in the context of liquidity needs. The canonical model simply records 'Pension' as the liquidity source, which, while technically accurate for the recommended wrapper (all pension assets are accessible), does not address the separate question of liquid cash reserves for short-term emergencies. This is the scenario described in the severity guidance as 'Low': emergency fund asserted as sufficient but never quantified — except here it is not even asserted as sufficient; it is simply not addressed. There is no vulnerability overlay concern identified in the evidence that materially changes this conclusion. The outcome is a Potential Concern at Low severity: the emergency fund is present in the file but is unquantified and unaddressed in the suitability report.

  [G5.6] Liquidity mismatch with access needs
  Potential Concern — severity Low — citations partly verified
    Report:   The recommendation is a Personal Pension wrapper with a selected retirement age of 71. No explicit liquidity or access needs with a required-from date are recorded in the extracted canonical model — the path /financialPosition/liquidityAndAccessNeeds[]/requiredFrom is absent.
    File:     The Aviva Platform Personal Pension (Key Features [P4]) states access is available from the normal minimum pension age, currently 55 (rising to 57 from 6 April 2028). The cashflow tool (proposed scenario [P19]) shows 'Retire (John)' at age 71 in 2027 and 'Retire (Kim)' at age 66 in 2026. The baseline cashflow ([P12]) records liquid assets of £6,000 (savings) alongside pension assets. The cashflow graphs ([P24]) show pensions categorised as 'Pensions (Accessible)' throughout the plan. No evidenced near-term capital access need or liquidity requirement falling before age 57 is recorded in the passages. The client (John, born 07/06/1956) is currently 69 years old ([P12],[P19]), already well past the minimum pension age of 55/57.
    Discrepancies:
      - The canonical model contains no /financialPosition/liquidityAndAccessNeeds[]/requiredFrom entry, so no specific access date is asserted by the report to compare against the wrapper's restrictions.
      - The selected retirement age of 71 is set in the future (2027 per [P19]), but the client is already 69 and above the minimum pension age, so the wrapper does not lock capital away from an accessible state.
      - Emergency fund / liquid assets are recorded as £6,000 in savings ([P12],[P21]) but the suitability report does not explicitly quantify or confirm the adequacy of this reserve as an emergency fund separate from the pension.
    The guard instruction is to check the client's age before flagging pension access restrictions. John is 69 years old ([P12],[P19]), already above both the current minimum pension age of 55 and the future threshold of 57. The Personal Pension wrapper therefore imposes no effective lock-in: capital is already accessible. The selected retirement age of 71 is a planning target, not a restriction on access. The cashflow modelling ([P19],[P24]) treats pensions as 'Accessible' throughout the plan, consistent with this. No evidenced near-term access need or withdrawal requirement conflicts with the wrapper. However, the report is silent on emergency fund adequacy: £6,000 in liquid savings is recorded ([P12],[P21]) but the suitability report does not confirm whether this constitutes a sufficient emergency reserve. This is a low-severity gap — the fund is identified but its adequacy is asserted implicitly rather than explicitly quantified in the report. No vulnerability factors in the evidence are identified as bearing materially on this specific liquidity/access question. Overall, the wrapper appropriately permits access, no liquidity mismatch is evidenced, but the emergency fund confirmation is absent from the report.

  [G5.7] State Pension entitlement and other anticipated income sources
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The canonical model records five income entries with categories: Employment (×3), StatePension (×2). One income entry has a startsOn date of 2026-03-26. No gross income figures were extracted from the report (path /financialPosition/incomes[]/gross is absent from the model).
    File:     The fact find (P6) records a State Pension income entry of £230.00 net per month for one party, alongside a Basic Income (employed) of £1,200.00 net per month. The cashflow tool (P8) shows Employment income of £16,640 and £13,200 (combined £29,840) in the baseline first year, with Total Regular Income of £28,613 and £13,200 (combined £41,813), implying State Pension income of approximately £11,973 in year one (£28,613 − £16,640 = £11,973 for one party). The proposed scenario cashflow graph (P2) explicitly shows 'State Pension' as an income bar. The intelliflo planning files (P1, P4) confirm State Pension Age of 66 for both JS and KS, with JS retiring at age 66 in 2026 and KS retiring at age 71 in 2027. The 2026 year milestone aligns with the reported startsOn of 2026-03-26. The fact find State Pension field (P5) is present but the amounts for Basic State Pension, Additional State Pension and BR19 Projection are blank — no quantified State Pension entitlement forecast is on file.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report records two StatePension income entries but the fact find (P6) records only one State Pension income line (£230.00/month) — a second State Pension income entry is not explicitly quantified in the fact find.
      - The gross income figure for the State Pension entries is absent from the canonical model, so the report's implied State Pension amount cannot be compared to any figure in the evidence.
      - The fact find State Pension entitlement section (P5) has blank fields for Basic State Pension, Additional State Pension and BR19 Projection — no formal State Pension forecast or entitlement statement is on file.
      - The fact find records State Pension of £230.00/month net (£2,760/year) but the cashflow baseline (P8) implies State Pension of approximately £11,973/year for JS alone — a significant difference that is not explained in the evidence pack.
      - The report startsOn date of 2026-03-26 (day -03-26 portion) is not corroborated in any passage; only the year 2026 is confirmed by the cashflow timeline (P4).
    The report asserts two State Pension income streams, consistent with a couple (JS and KS), both with State Pension Age 66 per the planning tool (P1, P4). The year 2026 as the start of one State Pension is corroborated by the timeline showing JS retiring at age 66 in 2026 (P4). However, several material gaps exist. First, the fact find State Pension entitlement section is entirely blank (P5) — no BR19 or equivalent forecast is on file for either party. This means the State Pension amounts feeding the cashflow model and underpinning the affordability case are unverified by any entitlement evidence. Second, there is a stark inconsistency between the fact find State Pension figure of £230/month (£2,760/year) recorded for one party (P6) and the cashflow baseline which implies a State Pension contribution of approximately £11,973/year for JS (P8: Total Regular Income £28,613 minus Employment £16,640). This discrepancy of approximately £9,213/year is unexplained and material to the affordability conclusion. Third, the gross amounts for State Pension entries are absent from the canonical model, preventing direct verification. The affordability case in the cashflow relies on State Pension income that is neither supported by a forecast on file nor consistent with the fact find figure. The specific day/month portion of the startsOn date (2026-03-26) is also uncorroborated, though this is a lower-order concern. No vulnerability factors in the evidence appear to affect this specific finding. The missing State Pension entitlement evidence and the income figure inconsistency between fact find and cashflow model constitute a material concern under the affordability and financial resilience requirement.

  [G5.8] Planned future expenditure and foreseeable financial needs
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report identifies four foreseeable changes: (1) client (JS) retirement within the next two years (expected ~October 2027), resulting in loss of HGV employment income (~£1,300/month) and Sea Cadets income (~£86.67/month); (2) Kim (KS) retirement at age 66 (~March 2026), with loss of £1,100/month employment income; (3) Kim's State Pension commencing at age 66 (~March 2026) at ~£230/week (~£1,000/month equivalent); (4) IHT regime change for pensions from 6 April 2027. For liquidity/access, the report notes pension drawdown access will take 10–15 working days but does not quantify an emergency fund amount.
    File:     The Fact Find (P1, P14) records total net monthly household income of £1,430 and total monthly expenditure of £1,718, producing a deficit of -£288/month. The Fact Find income section (P14) shows Kim's income as £1,200/month basic employment and £230/month State Pension for JS, with no income changes expected recorded. The Fact Find records no foreseeable expenditure changes (P1). Cash savings of £6,000 are recorded (P16, P6). The cashflow tool (P6) shows the same £6,000 liquid assets opening position. The proposed scenario planning tool (P12) shows JS retirement event at age 71 in 2027 and KS retirement event at age 66 in 2026, consistent with the report's foreseeable changes. The cashflow model (P5, P6) models income streams including employment income and State Pension through the plan. The Fact Find need (P17) states client needs a household income of £1,700 net/month in retirement. No emergency fund quantum is evidenced anywhere in the file beyond the £6,000 savings balance. The Fact Find records no income changes expected (P14), yet the planning tool models retirement events and associated income changes.
    Discrepancies:
      - The Fact Find (P14) records 'No' to expected income changes in the foreseeable future, yet the suitability report identifies two imminent retirement events (KS in ~2026, JS in ~2027) that will materially reduce household income — these are not flagged as foreseeable income changes in the Fact Find income section.
      - The Fact Find (P1) records 'No' to expected expenditure changes, yet the retirement of both clients within approximately 1–2 years would typically be accompanied by expenditure changes; the report does not quantify any expenditure change on retirement.
      - The current monthly cash flow already shows a deficit of -£288/month (income £1,430 vs expenditure £1,718 per P1), yet the suitability report does not explicitly address this pre-existing deficit or explain how ongoing pension charges are affordable in the context of this deficit.
      - The report asserts Kim's State Pension will be ~£230/week (~£1,000/month equivalent), but the Fact Find income section (P14) records a State Pension of £230/month (not £230/week) for the client (JS), not Kim — creating ambiguity about whether £230/week is Kim's confirmed entitlement or an assumption.
      - The emergency fund/liquidity need is noted in the report as pension drawdown access taking 10–15 working days, but no emergency fund quantum is stated — the £6,000 cash savings are not explicitly referenced as an emergency fund in the suitability report or assessed for sufficiency against the pre-existing -£288/month deficit and forthcoming income reductions.
      - The IHT foreseeable change (April 2027) is noted in the report but no financial impact amount is quantified (amount recorded as null), leaving the financial resilience implications unaddressed.
      - The Fact Find records JS's income as State Pension £230/month plus employment, totalling £1,430/month; the suitability report states JS earns ~£300/week from HGV driving and ~£20/week from Sea Cadets — the Fact Find income table (P14) does not separately itemise Sea Cadets income, only showing basic income of £0 gross / £1,200 net and State Pension of £230/month, which does not reconcile cleanly with £300/week (~£1,300/month) HGV plus £86.67/month Sea Cadets equalling ~£1,616.67/month versus the recorded £1,430/month total.
    The suitability report identifies four foreseeable financial events, three of which are partially corroborated by the planning tool (P12) which models KS retirement at 66 in 2026 and JS retirement at 71 in 2027. However, several material concerns arise. First, the Fact Find income section records 'No' to expected income changes (P14), which directly contradicts the report's identification of two imminent retirement events causing significant income reduction — this is a genuine inconsistency, not explained by any guard. Second, the current household cash flow already shows a pre-existing deficit of -£288/month (P1), yet the suitability report does not address how ongoing pension charges are affordable now, nor does it model the compounding effect of further income loss on retirement. Third, the emergency fund is entirely unquantified: the £6,000 in cash savings (P6, P16) is the only liquid non-pension asset, but the report does not reference this as an emergency fund or assess its sufficiency. Given the pre-existing monthly deficit of -£288 and two retirement events reducing income by a further ~£2,186.67/month net (combined loss of JS's £1,386.67 and KS's £1,100, partially offset by KS's State Pension of ~£1,000/month, net reduction ~£1,486.67/month), the £6,000 emergency reserve would be exhausted very quickly. The report notes Kim's State Pension at ~£230/week but the Fact Find records £230/month for JS's State Pension — this ambiguity affects the income projection at retirement. The IHT change is noted but unquantified. The vulnerability overlay: no specific vulnerability flags are identified in the evidence that would materially alter this assessment beyond what is already noted. The Fact Find 'No income changes expected' entry is inconsistent with the planned retirement events modelled in the cashflow tool and described in the report, representing a moderate consistency concern. The unquantified emergency fund against a pre-existing deficit and imminent large income reductions is a material concern.

CHK-006 — Recommendation Rationale and Suitable Alternatives
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 8 requirement(s). 6 raised a concern (G6.1, G6.2, G6.3, G6.4, G6.5, G6.6). 1. The report states the Aviva existing plan is '0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' in the Aviva rationale, but then states the Standard Life plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' in a separate rationale bullet. The internal inconsistency flags (from extraction) note that the Aviva charge comparison text reads 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive' yet the arithmetic shows the new solution is cheaper than Aviva existing (0.50% vs 0.44%), meaning the direction of the statement ('more expensive than the recommended solution') is ambiguous — the report's phrasing could mean the existing plan is 0.06% more expensive (i.e. new is cheaper), which is consistent, but the wording is confusing and potentially misleading. 2. Several rationale bullets are repeated verbatim across multiple recommendations (e.g. 'Adviser Charging Facilitation', 'Ongoing Monitoring and Adjustments', 'Greater Investment Choice', 'Enhanced Retirement Flexibility', 'Lifestyling') with identical or near-identical wording, and are marked isClientSpecific false or true inconsistently. These are boilerplate in substance. 3. The policy research file ([P4]/[P8]) leaves 'Which objective/goal is this achieving?' blank for all individual plan recommendations, meaning the file-level research does not link recommendations to client objectives, even though the suitability report does. 4 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G6.1] Client-specific rationale for each recommendation, linked to specified goals
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The suitability report provides rationale across six recommendation groups: (1) Retain People's Pension – active workplace scheme with ongoing employer contributions; (2) Encash Scottish Widows – small pot rules, tax-free cash of 25% with estimate of £170 net; (3) Switch Aviva to new Aviva Platform – lower costs (0.06% cheaper), lifestyling concern, greater investment choice, adviser charging facilitation, ongoing monitoring; (4) Switch Zurich – greater investment choice, multi-asset fund limitation (no risk-5 funds, blend of risk-7 and risk-2 used), enhanced flexibility, adviser charging, ongoing monitoring, lower costs (0.08% cheaper), lifestyling; (5) Switch Standard Life – lower costs, lifestyling, greater investment choice, enhanced flexibility, adviser charging, ongoing monitoring; (6) Fund selection – CT Universal MAP Balanced 50% and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced 50%, justified by risk profile alignment, diversification, blend of active/passive, simplicity, competitive charges, peer performance, CIP inclusion, and multi-asset rationale. Rationale items are marked isClientSpecific true for most, with several generic bullets (isClientSpecific false) including fund charges competitiveness, peer performance, diversification, and multi-asset fund explanation. Objectives linked are OBJ1, OBJ2, OBJ3 across recommendations. The report states the new Aviva plan is '0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' in one place and '0.08% more expensive' in another.
    File:     [P4] Policy research file confirms: People's Pension – Retain (active workplace pension); Scottish Widows – Encash (small pot); Aviva – Switch (lifestyle, expensive, no adviser servicing); Zurich – Switch (expensive, no adviser servicing); Standard Life – Encash/Switch (small pot). The 'Which objective/goal is this achieving?' field is blank for all individual plan recommendations in the research file. [P5]/[P7] SIPP research report confirms the recommended investment is £110,185 into Aviva, with HSBC Global Strategy Balanced and CT Universal MAP Balanced both appearing as recommended funds with cost disclosures. [P8] Policy research notes confirm Aviva and Zurich switches are recommended due to lifestyling, cost and no adviser servicing. [P12] Risk profile report confirms natural risk level 4 (Cautious Balanced) with an agreed level discussion, but notes section records 'No additional notes were recorded' for both investment target and agreed risk level sections. [P11] confirms no additional notes recorded for agreed risk level discussion. [P1] Policy research table shows adviser charging applicability: only the Aviva arrangement (EA3) supports adviser charging; People's Pension, Scottish Widows, Zurich and Standard Life all show 'No' for adviser charging. [P9] Suitability report confirms initial advice fee of 3% = £3,305.55 and ongoing 1% p.a. charged via Aviva Platform.
    Discrepancies:
      - 1. The report states the Aviva existing plan is '0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' in the Aviva rationale, but then states the Standard Life plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' in a separate rationale bullet. The internal inconsistency flags (from extraction) note that the Aviva charge comparison text reads 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive' yet the arithmetic shows the new solution is cheaper than Aviva existing (0.50% vs 0.44%), meaning the direction of the statement ('more expensive than the recommended solution') is ambiguous — the report's phrasing could mean the existing plan is 0.06% more expensive (i.e. new is cheaper), which is consistent, but the wording is confusing and potentially misleading.
      - 2. Several rationale bullets are repeated verbatim across multiple recommendations (e.g. 'Adviser Charging Facilitation', 'Ongoing Monitoring and Adjustments', 'Greater Investment Choice', 'Enhanced Retirement Flexibility', 'Lifestyling') with identical or near-identical wording, and are marked isClientSpecific false or true inconsistently. These are boilerplate in substance.
      - 3. The policy research file ([P4]/[P8]) leaves 'Which objective/goal is this achieving?' blank for all individual plan recommendations, meaning the file-level research does not link recommendations to client objectives, even though the suitability report does.
      - 4. The risk profile report ([P11]/[P12]) records 'No additional notes were recorded' for both the investment target and the agreed risk level sections, meaning there is no documented narrative from the adviser discussion about why risk level 5 was agreed over level 4, beyond what the suitability report asserts.
      - 5. The rationale for retaining the People's Pension (active workplace scheme) is linked only to OBJ3 (consolidation), yet retaining it is in tension with the stated consolidation objective — the report does not explain why consolidation does not apply here beyond noting employer contributions, but no cross-reference to why this is an exception to OBJ3 is made in the research file.
      - 6. Fund selection rationale contains several bullets marked isClientSpecific false (charges competitive, peer performance, diversification, multi-asset explanation), indicating generic language not tied to the client's specific figures or circumstances.
      - 7. The total transfer value used in the SIPP research (£110,185) does not match the sum of the four plans being switched (£3,744.36 + £103,439.24 + £3,002 + £211.32 = £110,396.92 if all four are transferred, or various subsets), creating an unexplained discrepancy that is not addressed in the rationale.
      - 8. The report's rationale for the Zurich switch references that 'Zurich does not offer risk level 5 funds' and a blend of risk-7 and risk-2 is used — this is client-specific and corroborated by the policy research noting the fund limitation, but the research file does not explicitly document the unavailability of risk-5 funds at Zurich as a formal finding.
      - 9. No alternatives to Aviva Platform as the receiving wrapper are documented in the suitability report or the research file with reasons for rejection. The research file ([P4]) notes 'Alternative Strategies: Current set up isn't working because it is too much information for him to understand. Wants to simplify things' but does not name or reject any alternative provider or wrapper.
    The suitability report contains rationale for each of the six recommendation outcomes, and most are linked to named client objectives (OBJ1, OBJ2, OBJ3). Several rationale points are genuinely client-specific: the People's Pension retention references ongoing employer contributions; the Scottish Widows encashment references the small pot rules with a specific net receipt estimate of £170; the Zurich switch references the absence of risk-5 funds at Zurich and the poor performance of the blended alternative. These are corroborated at a high level by the policy research file ([P4]/[P8]) which confirms the same recommendations and broadly the same reasons (lifestyle, cost, no adviser servicing). However, material weaknesses are present: (a) A significant number of rationale bullets are verbatim boilerplate repeated across three separate pension switch recommendations (Adviser Charging Facilitation, Ongoing Monitoring, Greater Investment Choice, Enhanced Retirement Flexibility, Lifestyling) with no differentiation between plans. The guard permits marking these isClientSpecific false, and several are so marked. (b) No alternatives to Aviva Platform as the receiving scheme are named or rejected anywhere in the report or research file. The research file's 'Alternative Strategies' section is wholly generic. This is a High-severity gap under the decision logic: 'provider or wrapper choice is asserted without comparison, or alternatives are absent or listed without reasons.' (c) The policy research file leaves the 'Which objective/goal is this achieving?' column blank for all plans, meaning the file-level evidence does not corroborate the objective linkages stated in the report. (d) The risk profile discussion notes record nothing, meaning the adviser's reasoning for agreeing risk level 5 (over the questionnaire result of 4) is unsupported by any file evidence beyond the report's own assertion. (e) The confusing wording around cost comparison ('The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution') creates ambiguity about direction that is not resolved in supporting documents. On vulnerability: no specific vulnerability flags are present in the evidence that would alter this assessment. The discrepancy regarding the transfer value total (£110,185 vs sum of parts) is noted but is primarily a CHK-001 matter; it does not independently undermine the rationale narrative. On balance, the absence of any documented alternative provider/wrapper comparison, combined with the volume of boilerplate rationale, constitutes a Potential Concern at High severity.

  [G6.2] Justification for the particular product, provider, wrapper and investment
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report asserts Aviva as the recommended provider on the basis of: financial strength (A/Stable S&P, £407bn AUM), investment choice (6,900+ funds), manager expertise, competitive/transparent charges with tiered pricing, online convenience, governance, and innovation — all generic. It also asserts three client-specific reasons linked to objectives: Lower Costs (OBJ1), Greater Investment Choice (OBJ2), and Adviser Charging Facilitation / Ongoing Monitoring (OBJ3). The wrapper is a Personal Pension (Aviva Pension Portfolio / Pension Portfolio – Choice SIPP on the Aviva Platform). The investment strategy is a 50/50 split between HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc and CT Universal MAP Balanced C, justified on: risk-profile alignment (both individually and combined), diversification, active/passive blend for cost efficiency, client preference for simplicity, competitive charges, peer-relative performance, CIP membership with quarterly review committee and external research, and multi-asset rationale. The report states the 'Lower Costs' rationale on page 7, linking the receiving scheme to a lower overall cost structure vs existing pensions. However, a pre-extracted internal inconsistency notes that page 6 text claims the new plan is '0.06% more expensive' — contradicting the 'Lower Costs' claim.
    File:     [P5] and [P9]: A Defaqto SIPP research report was carried out. The research area is SIPP; the recommended product is Pension Portfolio – Choice, Aviva Life & Pensions UK Limited. Filters produced 1 product from 118 matching. [P1]: A platform research report shows 4 products shortlisted (Aviva Platform, Elevate/Aberdeen, Aegon ARC, Wealthtime Platform) with no filters applied and no DNA analysis performed. No comparative scoring or rejection rationale for the three non-recommended platforms is documented. [P4]: Fund recommendation table in the report confirms HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc at 50% (risk rating 5, 0.18% charge) and CT Universal MAP Balanced C at 50% (risk rating 5, 0.29% charge). Investment rationale bullet points are present in the report. [P10]: Defaqto research confirms ex-ante costs: HSBC ongoing 0.18%, CT Universal MAP ongoing 0.29%; both risk-rated 5. [P12]: Performance data for both funds is present: CT Universal MAP 1YR 11.17%, 3YR 36.66%, 5YR 44.73%; HSBC 1YR 9.92%, 3YR 32.52%, 5YR 40.06%. [P13]: Policy research pre-advice notes show Aviva rated 'Switch' (reason: lifestyle, expensive, no adviser servicing) and Zurich rated 'Switch' (expensive, no adviser servicing). [P14]: Pension switch report confirms consolidation from Aviva, Zurich and Standard Life into Aviva Pension Portfolio – Choice. [P2]: Investment pathways considered and discounted for stated client-specific reasons. No comparative scoring between the four shortlisted platforms is documented in [P1]; rejection rationale for Elevate, Aegon ARC and Wealthtime is absent from the evidence pack.
    Discrepancies:
      - 1. The report asserts 'Lower Costs' as a client-specific reason for selecting Aviva (page 7, OBJ1). An internal inconsistency recorded in the extraction notes that page 6 text states 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' — which contradicts a straightforward 'lower costs' claim and creates ambiguity about whether costs are higher or lower overall.
      - 2. The platform shortlist ([P1]) contains four products (Aviva Platform, Elevate/Aberdeen, Aegon ARC, Wealthtime) but no DNA analysis was performed and no rejection rationale is documented for the three alternatives. The report does not explain why Elevate, Aegon ARC or Wealthtime were not recommended.
      - 3. The report's provider rationale is almost entirely generic (financial strength, choice, expertise, value, convenience, governance, innovation) with only three client-specific reasons. No comparative evidence in the pack benchmarks these generic claims against the shortlisted alternatives.
      - 4. The SIPP product filter ([P9]) returned only 1 from 118 products, but the filter criteria are not documented in the pack — it is unclear what criteria narrowed the field to a single result, so the basis for product selection is not fully transparent.
      - 5. The report states the wrapper is a 'Personal Pension' in one place (page 9 canonical model) and the research/switch report describes it as a SIPP (Pension Portfolio – Choice). This is a minor terminology inconsistency but not a substantive mismatch given the product is the same.
      - 6. No alternative investment options are explicitly considered and rejected in the report beyond the investment pathways section ([P2]). The CIP rationale is asserted but no comparison of non-CIP fund alternatives is evidenced.
    The report provides a structured rationale for both provider and investment selection. The investment rationale is well-supported: [P4] confirms the fund table, [P10] confirms charges and risk ratings, [P12] confirms performance data, and [P2] documents why investment pathways were discounted. The CIP basis is stated and is a legitimate justification per the guard (firm CIP membership is acceptable without a fresh whole-of-market fund search). The SIPP product selection is supported by Defaqto research ([P5], [P9]) showing a filtered shortlist, though the filter criteria are opaque. The most material concern is the platform selection: [P1] shows four platforms were shortlisted but no comparative scoring, DNA analysis or rejection reasoning is documented for the three non-recommended platforms. The report does not explain why Elevate, Aegon ARC or Wealthtime were not chosen. This is a missing comparative justification for the wrapper/platform choice. The 'Lower Costs' claim is also internally contradicted: the extraction flagged that page 6 states 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' while page 7 asserts 'Lower Costs' as a client-specific rationale linked to OBJ1. The charge arithmetic in the canonical model supports a lower total charge for the new solution relative to the Zurich arrangement (0.44% vs 0.93%), so the 'lower costs' claim may be directionally correct in aggregate, but the internal contradiction in the report text is itself a suitability concern — the adviser appears to have stated both that costs are lower and (in a different passage) that the plan is more expensive. This contradictory language in the report, combined with the absence of platform comparison rationale, constitutes a Potential Concern. The vulnerability overlay has no bearing on this check as no vulnerability factors are identified in the retrieved evidence that would affect the rationale assessment. Severity is Moderate: rationale is present and partially client-specific, supported by research, but the platform rejection reasoning is absent and the cost narrative is internally contradictory within the report itself.

  [G6.3] Personalised language linked to individual circumstances
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The canonical model asserts that the vast majority of rationale items (23 out of 28) are client-specific (isClientSpecific = true), with 5 items marked false. The report therefore claims to contain personalised, client-linked justification for almost all recommendations.
    File:     The policy research note (P14, P15) shows that for each pension, the objective column is blank — no objective is recorded against the Aviva Switch, Zurich Switch, People's Pension Retain, Scottish Widows recommendation or Standard Life recommendation. The 'Why/Why isn't this scheme suitable' fields contain only brief generic phrases: 'Lifestyle, expensive, no adviser servicing' (Aviva), 'Expensive, no adviser servicing' (Zurich), 'Active workplace pension' (People's Pension). The fact find (P5) records the client's actual stated objective: to consolidate pensions, retire in approximately 2 years, and achieve a net household income of £1,700 per month. The suitability report extract visible in P13 links the recommendation to the client by name and refers to switching the Aviva pension, but the policy research that underpins it leaves the objective column empty for every arrangement. No alternatives document with reasons for rejection is visible in the pack.
    Discrepancies:
      - The canonical model claims 23 of 28 rationale items are client-specific, but the supporting policy research (P14, P15) leaves the 'Which objective/goal is this achieving?' field blank for every pension arrangement reviewed, meaning the recommendations are not demonstrably linked to named client objectives in the research file.
      - Reasons given for switching or retaining each plan in the policy research are generic one-liners ('Lifestyle, expensive, no adviser servicing'; 'Expensive, no adviser servicing'; 'Active workplace pension') rather than client-specific reasoning tied to Alan's circumstances, timeline or income target.
      - The client's stated objective of achieving £1,700 net per month in retirement and retiring within approximately 2 years (P5) does not appear to be referenced in the rationale fields of the policy research for any individual arrangement.
      - No alternatives considered with reasons for rejection are evidenced in the pack; the policy research 'Alternative Strategies' field (P14) states only 'Current set up isn't working because it is too much information for him to understand. Wants to simplify things.' — this describes the problem rather than alternatives evaluated and rejected.
      - The canonical model flags 5 rationale items as not client-specific (isClientSpecific = false), confirming the report itself contains generic rationale for those items, which is consistent with the generic language found in the supporting research.
      - The internal inconsistency noted in the extraction (People's Pension retained rather than consolidated despite OBJ3 being pension consolidation) suggests the rationale for retaining that plan is not clearly linked to a client objective — the policy research notes only 'Active workplace pension' without explaining why consolidation was unsuitable for this specific plan given the client's consolidation goal.
    The comparison can be made. The report asserts personalised, client-linked rationale for almost all recommendations. The evidence from the policy research (P14, P15) contradicts this for the following reasons. First, every 'Which objective/goal is this achieving?' field is blank, meaning the research file — which is the foundation of the advice — does not connect any recommendation to a named client objective. Second, the suitability reasons given are brief and generic: lifestyle, cost and servicing issues are not individualised to Alan's two-year retirement horizon, his £1,700/month income target, or his five-pension consolidation goal recorded in the fact find (P5). Third, no alternatives document or reasoned rejection of alternatives is present; the sole reference to alternatives in P14 is a restatement of the client's problem rather than a consideration of different strategies. The guard about standard regulatory wording does not apply here — the absence of client-specific rationale is not a matter of boilerplate tax caveats. The 5 rationale items the report itself marks as not client-specific further confirm that generic language is present. The vulnerability overlay does not materially change this finding: no specific vulnerability drivers are identified in the evidence that would alter the suitability assessment. Overall, the report's assertion that 23 of 28 rationale items are client-specific is not adequately supported by the underlying research documentation, which is wholly generic at the arrangement level. This is a moderate-to-high concern: rationale exists but is wholly generic across all recommendations in the research file, and alternatives are absent with reasons.

  [G6.4] Alternatives considered and reasons for rejection
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report considers five structured options (OPT1–OPT5) and recommends OPT5 (new Aviva Platform SIPP). It also assesses each existing arrangement individually. Alternatives recorded include: (1) Leave pot to grow until retirement (Scottish Widows small pot) — rejected because encashing now is preferred to simplify arrangements, though the report notes it would not detract from the retirement plan; (2) Internal fund switch within Zurich — rejected because consolidation is the main priority, multi-asset funds are not available within the client's risk range, and blended funds have not performed as well as Aviva options; (3) Employer scheme (People's Pension) as consolidation vehicle — rejected because employer contributions would be forfeited, funds do not meet risk profile, plan is 0.06% more expensive, no multi-asset funds available, and limited fund range; (4) Stakeholder pension — rejected because the recommended solution is more cost effective, and stakeholder pensions typically do not allow flexible access and have limited fund ranges; (5) Investment Pathway solutions — rejected because the client requires a more tailored solution, it does not reflect individual circumstances, withdrawal requirements and risk profile, and an ongoing advice service with regular reviews is required. The report also notes OPT1 (leave untouched) and OPT2 (in-situ fund switch) as options considered, but no rejection reasons are recorded in the canonical model for those two options.
    File:     The pension switch report (Defaqto) provides quantitative comparison tables for Aviva (existing) only, Zurich only, Standard Life only, and all three combined versus the new Aviva Platform SIPP solution. In every scenario the new solution produces a lower projected maturity value than retaining the existing pension(s): e.g. Zurich-only mid-growth £125,000 existing vs £115,000 new solution (£12,000 or 9.4% decrease at mid growth including adviser charges at 2.2% RIY vs 0.3% existing RIY). The SIPP research report shows a shortlist of four platforms (Aviva, Aberdeen Elevate, Aegon ARC, Wealthtime) with Aviva recommended, but records 'No additional justification text has been entered' for platform selection. No Platform DNA analysis was carried out and no filters were applied. The case file notes (category C) confirm: Scottish Widows discounted due to no servicing and reduced flexibility; Aviva existing discounted because the only mixed-asset fund is risk-rated 4 (not 5); Zurich discounted because no risk-5 funds exist — a blend of risk-7 and risk-2 was examined but blended funds underperformed marginally and singular asset types would not be recommended; Standard Life discounted because all funds in the 20–60% equity range are more expensive than Aviva. The stakeholder pension comparison table in the pension switch report includes a generic stakeholder column. Investment pathway rejection is stated on page 21 of the suitability report. The report's claim that the People's Pension employer scheme is '0.06% more expensive' appears in the report and is corroborated by the pension switch report charge comparison (existing Aviva 0.50% vs new 0.44% = 0.06% difference), but the report text applies this '0.06% more expensive' characterisation to the employer scheme (People's Pension), not to the Aviva existing plan — the charge comparison in the evidence relates to the Aviva existing plan, not the People's Pension.
    Discrepancies:
      - OPT1 (leave existing untouched) and OPT2 (in-situ fund switch) are listed in the report as options considered but carry no rejection reasons in the canonical model — no supporting reasoning appears in the retrieved passages either.
      - The pension switch report shows the new solution produces a lower maturity value than the existing pensions in every scenario (e.g. Zurich-only mid-growth: existing £127,000 vs new solution £125,000 without adviser charges [P3], and with adviser charges Zurich-only mid-growth existing £127,000 vs new £115,000 [P5]). The report's rationale emphasises cost savings and better positioning for retirement but does not acknowledge that the projected maturity value is lower under the new solution in all modelled scenarios.
      - The SIPP research report records 'No additional justification text has been entered' for platform selection and no DNA analysis or filters were applied to the platform shortlist [P7][P8], meaning the choice of Aviva Platform over Aberdeen Elevate, Aegon ARC or Wealthtime is asserted without documented comparative reasoning beyond being on panel.
      - The report states the People's Pension employer scheme is '0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' — the charge comparison evidence (0.50% existing Aviva vs 0.44% new) relates to the Aviva existing plan, not the People's Pension/employer scheme. No charge schedule for the People's Pension employer scheme is evidenced to support the 0.06% claim as applied to that plan.
      - The stakeholder pension rejection reasons ('more cost effective', 'no flexible access', 'limited fund ranges') are entirely generic with no client-specific figures or fund-range comparison evidenced [P16].
      - The Investment Pathway rejection includes the reason 'It has not performed as well as the recommended investment strategy' — no performance comparison between investment pathway solutions and the recommended funds is present in the retrieved evidence.
      - The internal fund switch (Zurich) rejection in the report states blended funds 'have not performed as well as Aviva options' — the file note [P22] says 'marginally' underperformed but no quantified performance comparison is present in the evidence pack.
      - The Scottish Widows small-pot encashment alternative ('leave pot to grow') rejection reason is flagged as isClientSpecific: false in the canonical model, and the rationale ('encashing now is preferred to simplify arrangements') is a preference assertion rather than a reasoned client-specific explanation tied to the client's tax position or retirement timeline.
    The report presents a broader set of alternatives than many suitability reports and several rejections are genuinely client-specific (employer contribution loss for People's Pension; Zurich fund range; Scottish Widows adviser management restriction). However, multiple material concerns remain after applying the guards. First and most significant: the pension switch report consistently shows the new solution produces a lower projected maturity value than retaining the existing arrangements in every scenario modelled — both before and after adviser charges. The report does not acknowledge this ranked outcome anywhere in its alternatives analysis. Under the decision logic, a ranked comparison placing the recommendation below the existing arrangement is a finding in its own right, regardless of whether alternatives were considered. Second, OPT1 and OPT2 appear as labelled options in the report but carry no rejection reasons — options listed without individual rejection reasons are a partial answer under the stated guard. Third, the platform selection research document explicitly records no justification text and no DNA analysis, meaning the choice of Aviva Platform over three comparably shortlisted platforms is unsupported by documented comparative reasoning. Fourth, the '0.06% more expensive' claim about the People's Pension employer scheme is not corroborated by any evidenced charge schedule for that plan; the charge evidence in the file relates to the Aviva existing plan. Fifth, the stakeholder and investment pathway rejections contain generic language not anchored to client-specific data. The vulnerability overlay: no specific vulnerability characteristics are recorded in the evidence that would independently alter this assessment. Taken together, the most serious issue is the unacknowledged downside projection under the recommended switch — this is a Highest-tier concern under the severity guidance ('rationale that contradicts the case facts'). The absence of platform comparison justification and the OPT1/OPT2 gap are High-tier. The generic stakeholder/investment pathway rejections are Moderate. Overall severity is High (dominated by the unacknowledged adverse projection and platform justification gap).

  [G6.5] Every existing arrangement has a stated recommended outcome and a rationale supporting it
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   Five existing arrangements are identified: (1) People's Pension – Retain, rationale: current workplace scheme with ongoing employer contributions, transferring would forfeit employer contributions. (2) Scottish Widows – Encash under small pots rules, rationale: £211.32 fund, 25% tax-free / 75% taxed at marginal rate, estimated net receipt £170; alternative to leave to grow until retirement within personal allowance also noted. (3) Aviva pension my future focus consolidation – Full Transfer to Aviva Platform, rationale: lower costs, lifestyling risk, greater investment choice, adviser charging facilitation, ongoing monitoring. (4) Zurich Managed Pn AP – Full Transfer to Aviva Platform, rationale: lower costs, lifestyling risk, greater investment choice, multi-asset fund unavailability at risk level 5 on Zurich, enhanced retirement flexibility, adviser charging facilitation, ongoing monitoring. (5) Standard Life – Full Transfer to Aviva Platform, rationale: lower costs (0.08% saving), lifestyling risk, greater investment choice, enhanced retirement flexibility, adviser charging facilitation, ongoing monitoring. All 21 rationale items are flagged isClientSpecific = true.
    File:     The policy research document confirms five arrangements exist across People's Pension, Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life and Scottish Widows. The suitability report (P21, P23) corroborates the recommended outcomes and associated rationale for all five arrangements. P21 shows a comparison table listing all five schemes with retirement options, fund counts, and servicing adviser flags. P23 reproduces the Standard Life switch rationale verbatim, stating '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution'. The canonical model records the Aviva/Zurich rationale as stating 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive' (page 7), while P23 states '0.08% more expensive' for Standard Life. The policy research file (P12) notes the overall strategy as consolidation where necessary. No evidence passage contradicts the Retain outcome for People's Pension, the Encash outcome for Scottish Widows, or the Full Transfer outcomes for Aviva, Zurich and Standard Life. However, the charge-saving assertion for the Aviva arrangement (canonical model page 7: '0.06% more expensive') conflicts with the internal inconsistency noted in the extraction: the Aviva existing charge is 0.50% and the new solution is 0.44%, a saving of 0.06%, yet the report text claims the plan is '0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' — which would mean the new plan is cheaper, not more expensive, but the wording direction is inverted (saying the existing is more expensive is correct, but if read as the new plan being more expensive it is wrong). For Standard Life P23 states 0.08% saving. The People's Pension retention rationale does not acknowledge that the report's own OBJ3 (consolidate pensions) is not met by retaining this plan, and no explanation of why this specific plan is excluded from consolidation beyond employer contributions is provided beyond that single sentence.
    Discrepancies:
      - The canonical model records the Aviva transfer cost-saving rationale as 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' (page 7); P23 records the Standard Life version as '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' — these are different arrangements with different figures, which is expected, but the Aviva-specific cost differential stated in the report (0.06%) is not directly confirmed or contradicted by a retrieved passage for Aviva specifically.
      - The Standard Life policy value document (P6) shows a fund value of £2,994.30 at 30 September 2025, whereas the canonical model and P5 (policy research) record the Standard Life fund value as £3,002.00 — a discrepancy of £7.70 between the valuation statement and the figure used in the suitability report.
      - The extraction flags that the People's Pension is recommended 'Retain' while OBJ3 is to consolidate pensions, with no documented rationale explaining why the People's Pension is excluded from the consolidation objective beyond the employer contribution point — the report does not explicitly address the tension between OBJ3 and the Retain recommendation.
      - The Scottish Widows rationale presents two alternative outcomes (encash now vs leave to grow) without unambiguously committing to a single recommended course of action in the extracted rationale items, creating a presentation of multiple outcomes.
      - The charge arithmetic section notes that the solution total ongoing implies a fund value of ~£110,185, not the sum of all five arrangements (£116,998.47), and no rationale in the report explains what value is being transferred or why £6,813.47 appears unaccounted for — this affects whether the 'lower costs' rationale is fully supported for each arrangement.
    The report provides a stated recommended outcome and at least one rationale item for all five existing arrangements, satisfying the basic coverage requirement. The rationale items are marked client-specific and reference named objectives for most items. However, several issues reduce the quality of the rationale: (1) The Scottish Widows encashment section presents two possible outcomes (encash now or leave to grow) without making unambiguous which is the single recommended course of action — this is the 'multiple possible outcomes without explaining which is recommended' flag in the check criteria, though the primary recommendation does appear to be encashment given the ordering and language. (2) The People's Pension retention is supported by the employer contribution argument, which is client-specific and reasonable, but the report does not address the tension with OBJ3 (consolidate pensions). The guard states a plan the client asked to leave alone still needs the instruction recorded with a rationale — here the rationale is the employer contribution benefit, which is substantive, but the conflict with the stated consolidation objective is not resolved in the report text. (3) The Standard Life fund value used in the report (£3,002) differs by £7.70 from the valuation statement (£2,994.30 per P6) — this is a minor factual inconsistency but does not materially undermine the rationale. (4) The 'lower costs' rationale for the Aviva arrangement is noted in the extraction as potentially contradictory: the existing Aviva charge is 0.50% and the new charge is 0.44%, so the new scheme is cheaper — but the report wording 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' is ambiguous as to direction. P23 confirms the Standard Life version says 0.08% more expensive, which is correct in direction (existing is more expensive than new). For Aviva the 0.06% figure is arithmetically correct (0.50% - 0.44% = 0.06%) and the direction appears to mean the existing Aviva plan costs 0.06% more than the new solution — this is consistent with the charge data. No vulnerability considerations are identified in the evidence that would materially affect this assessment. Overall the rationale coverage is present but the Scottish Widows dual-outcome presentation and the unresolved OBJ3/People's Pension tension are moderate concerns.
