CHK-007 — Costs, Charges and Value of the Recommendation
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 11 requirement(s). 10 raised a concern (G7.1, G7.2, G7.3, G7.4, G7.5, G7.6, G7.7, G7.9, G7.10, G7.11). The illustration (P8) shows a weighted average OCF/TER of 0.23% for the two funds (CT 0.29% + HSBC 0.18% at 50/50 = 0.235% rounded to 0.23%), but the report states the Investment Management charge as 0.22%. A 0.01% rounding difference exists between the illustration's weighted average (0.23%) and the report's stated investment management figure (0.22%). The SIPP research report (P10) explicitly identifies transaction costs for CT Universal MAP Balanced C as 0.26% (£145.05) and total aggregated transaction costs of 0.13% (£147.80). The report excludes transaction costs with stated reasons, but the research tool has quantified them at 0.13–0.26% depending on fund, which means an estimate was available and was not provided to the client. The report's total ongoing charge of 0.44% covers only platform (0.21%) and investment management (0.22%) but does not separately itemise the individual fund OCF charges (0.18% HSBC and 0.29% CT) in the summary cost table, though they are disclosed on a later page (page 12). 8 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G7.1] All material costs disclosed — product, platform, fund, transaction, advice
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report discloses: Platform charge 0.21% (£231.39 pa), Investment Management charge 0.22% (£253.43 pa), Total ongoing charge 0.44% (£484.81 pa) for the Aviva Platform Personal Pension. Fund-level charges: HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc 0.18%, CT Universal MAP Balanced C 0.29%. Initial advice fee 3% of amount invested (£3,305.55). Ongoing adviser charge referenced via Client Agreement. Transaction costs excluded with stated reasons (variable, already reflected in net fund performance figures). Aggregated total and cumulative effect disclosed. DFM fee not applicable. No product-level charge (e.g. Aviva platform product wrap charge) separately itemised beyond the 0.21% platform line.
    File:     The Aviva illustration (P8) confirms the Aviva platform charge at 0.21% for balances above £0, and lists investment-related OCF/TER charges: CT Universal MAP Balanced C Acc 0.29%, HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc 0.18%, with a weighted average charge of 0.23%. The KIID for HSBC (P1) confirms ongoing charge 0.18% with no performance fee and zero entry/exit charges. The Columbia Threadneedle KIID (P2) confirms the ongoing charges figure is based on expenses for the year ending 29/11/24 and excludes costs of buying/selling assets (transaction costs). The SIPP research report (P10) shows CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund C Acc ongoing costs 0.29% (£159.77), transaction costs 0.26% (£145.05), total aggregated fund costs £406.74 (0.37%), explicitly identifying transaction costs as a separate layer not included in the OCF. The suitability report charges table (P3) matches the report's stated figures: Platform 0.21%/£231.39, Investment Management 0.22%/£253.43, Total 0.44%/£484.81. The pension switch analysis (P23) shows Zurich investment charge 0.50% used in projections. The adviser initial fee is stated in the report (P17) at 3%/£3,305.55. The illustration (P8) references adviser charges as a category but the specific ongoing adviser charge rate is not shown in the retrieved illustration text. The weighted average investment management charge in the illustration is 0.23% (P8), while the report states 0.22% as the Investment Management charge. The Standard Life illustration (P20) shows fund charges 1.0% less discount 0.5% = net 0.5% for that existing plan.
    Discrepancies:
      - The illustration (P8) shows a weighted average OCF/TER of 0.23% for the two funds (CT 0.29% + HSBC 0.18% at 50/50 = 0.235% rounded to 0.23%), but the report states the Investment Management charge as 0.22%. A 0.01% rounding difference exists between the illustration's weighted average (0.23%) and the report's stated investment management figure (0.22%).
      - The SIPP research report (P10) explicitly identifies transaction costs for CT Universal MAP Balanced C as 0.26% (£145.05) and total aggregated transaction costs of 0.13% (£147.80). The report excludes transaction costs with stated reasons, but the research tool has quantified them at 0.13–0.26% depending on fund, which means an estimate was available and was not provided to the client.
      - The report's total ongoing charge of 0.44% covers only platform (0.21%) and investment management (0.22%) but does not separately itemise the individual fund OCF charges (0.18% HSBC and 0.29% CT) in the summary cost table, though they are disclosed on a later page (page 12).
      - No ongoing adviser charge percentage or monetary amount is visible in the retrieved illustration text (P8), so the ongoing adviser remuneration layer cannot be confirmed from the illustration as corroborated.
      - The arithmetic of the 0.44% total charge implying a fund value of ~£110,184 does not match the sum of existing arrangement values (£116,998.47), suggesting the charge calculation is based on transfer value of £110,185 rather than the full current value total — this discrepancy in the base used is not explained in the report.
    Overall, the report discloses the principal cost layers — platform, investment management/fund, initial advice fee — and provides an aggregated total and cumulative effect reference pointing to the illustration. The transaction cost exclusion is expressly noted with reasons, satisfying the guard for stated exclusions. However, the SIPP research report (P10) has quantified transaction costs at 0.13% in aggregate (£147.80), meaning an estimate was available. Regulatory guidance (COBS 6.1ZA) requires that where transaction costs can be estimated, they should be included or the estimated figure disclosed; merely stating they are variable and reflected in net performance is a softer treatment than the evidence supports. This is a moderate concern rather than a high one because the exclusion is disclosed and reasoned, but the availability of an estimate in the file means the exclusion explanation is potentially incomplete. The 0.01% rounding difference between the illustration's weighted average OCF of 0.23% and the report's 0.22% investment management figure is within normal rounding tolerance (guard applies) and is not a material finding. The fund-level charges (0.18% and 0.29%) are disclosed on page 12 of the report even if not in the primary summary table, so this is not a silent omission. The ongoing adviser charge is referenced in the Client Agreement and the report text, but no specific ongoing % or £ amount appears in the retrieved illustration text; this is a gap in corroboration but not a direct contradiction. The base value discrepancy (£110,185 vs £116,998.47) in the charge arithmetic is noted in the extraction's internal inconsistencies and relates to CHK-009 transfer value issues; it does mean the monetary charge figures are understated relative to the full fund value, but the report does not explicitly claim they are calculated on the full current value. No vulnerability factors are identified in the evidence that would materially alter this cost disclosure assessment.

  Potential Concern — severity Low
    Report:   The suitability report asserts an initial advice fee of 3% of the amount invested, equating to £3,305.55 with current transfer values, to be paid directly by the pension schemes on transfer.     File:     The client agreement (IMPORT_2.md / TFAS Wealth Client Agreement) confirms a standard initial advice charge of 3% of the value of funds advised on (minimum £1,350), and an ongoing annual review management charge of 1% (minimum £100 per annum). The suitability report itself (P9) states 'we charge an initial advice fee of 3% of the amount invested as per the terms stated in our Client Agreement' and quotes £3,305.55.     Discrepancies:
      - The monetary amount £3,305.55 at 3% implies an investment value of approximately £110,185, which corresponds to the recommended transfer value rather than the sum of all existing arrangements (£116,998.47). The report states the fee is '3% of the amount invested' and refers to 'current transfer values' — the basis for the £3,305.55 figure is thus the net transfer value post-retention of People's Pension (£110,185), not the gross sum of all plans. This is an internally consistent reading but the report does not explicitly reconcile the £110,185 basis figure with the total existing arrangements value of £116,998.47.
      - The ongoing fee of £1,101.85 at 1% implies a fund value of approximately £110,185, consistent with the recommended transfer value but not explicitly stated as such in the costs section.
      - No explicit confirmation that the specific fee amounts have been accepted/signed off by the client in a separate charges confirmation document — the client agreement (P12) notes fees are set out in the suitability report and require express or written consent, but no such acceptance document is in the pack.
      - The client agreement states the minimum initial fee is £1,350 and minimum ongoing fee is £100 per annum — both are exceeded by the quoted amounts, so no minimum-breach issue, but this was noted for completeness.
    The percentage rates (3% initial, 1% ongoing) quoted in the suitability report are exactly corroborated by the client agreement (P13: '3% of the value of the funds that we advise on'; P3: '1% per annum'). The monetary amounts (£3,305.55 and £1,101.85) are both corroborated verbatim in the suitability report passages (P9 and P5 respectively). The arithmetic basis of £110,185 for both monetary figures is consistent with the recommended transfer value (the four plans being switched, excluding the retained People's Pension of £6,601.55 and excluding the Scottish Widows plan of £211.32 — though the report is not fully explicit about this exclusion in the costs section). The discrepancy between the total existing values (£116,998.47) and the fee basis (£110,185) is explainable by the retention of the People's Pension and the non-transfer of the Scottish Widows plan, but this reconciliation is implicit rather than explicit in the costs section. This is a low-level concern rather than a material one, as the basis ('current transfer values') is stated. No client agreement specifying the agreed fees for this particular engagement beyond the standard schedule is in the pack; however, the client agreement at P12 explicitly states fees are confirmed in the suitability report itself and require client acceptance — the standard rates match exactly. No fees exceed the client agreement rates. No vulnerability factors identified in the file appear to materially affect this cost disclosure assessment. Overall, the rates are fully corroborated; the monetary figures are arithmetically consistent with the transfer value basis stated; the main residual gap is the absence of an explicit reconciliation of the fee basis in the costs section and no separate signed charges acceptance document in the pack.

  [G7.3] Cumulative impact of costs and charges
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   Page 18 of the suitability report states the following for the recommended solution vs existing pension at age 75: Low growth (0.00%): RIY 2.1%, Critical yield 1.9%, Effect of switching £10,400 / 9.6% decrease. Mid growth (2.95%): RIY 2.2%, Critical yield 4.8%, Effect of switching £12,000 / 9.4% decrease. High growth (5.88%): RIY 2.3%, Critical yield 7.8%, Effect of switching £15,000 / 10.0% decrease. Existing pension RIY shown as 0.3% across all three growth rates. Page 11 states the recommended plan would need to grow by 4.8% per year (mid growth 2.94%) to match the existing plan's value at age 75.
    File:     [P1] (suitability report appendix, page 18) reproduces the identical Critical Yield table with the same figures: Low RIY 0.3%/2.1%, CY 1.9%, effect £10,400/9.6%; Mid RIY 0.3%/2.2%, CY 4.8%, effect £12,000/9.4%; High RIY 0.3%/2.3%, CY 7.8%, effect £15,000/10.0%. [P11] corroborates the 4.8% critical yield and 2.94% mid growth rate. [P10] (provider illustration) states the reduction in growth rate is 2.1% after all charges under mid growth (at age 75 value £115,000 after all charges vs £129,000 before, reducing growth from 2.9% to 0.8%), consistent with 2.1% RIY stated for low growth scenario but noting the illustration uses a 5% growth rate (2.9% real) while the report uses a 2.95% mid rate. [P3] (provider illustration) projects £115,000 at medium (5%/2.9% real) and £135,000 at higher (8%/5.9% real), matching the report's mid and high projected values. [P23] (pension switch report, Defaqto) presents a different comparison table for the consolidated pensions, showing: Low RIY 0.3%/0.6%, CY 0.3%, effect £2,000/1.9%; Mid RIY 0.3%/0.6%, CY 3.2%, effect £2,000/1.6%; High RIY 0.3%/0.6%, CY 6.2%, effect £3,000/2.0%. These figures differ materially from those in the suitability report.
    Discrepancies:
      - The Defaqto pension switch report [P23] shows Recommended RIY of 0.6% across all growth rates, versus the suitability report's 2.1%/2.2%/2.3% — a difference of approximately 1.5-1.7 percentage points, suggesting the Defaqto figures exclude adviser charges while the suitability report figures include them.
      - The Defaqto report [P23] shows Critical Yield of 0.3% (low), 3.2% (mid) and 6.2% (high), compared to the suitability report's 1.9%, 4.8% and 7.8% respectively — materially different at every growth rate.
      - The Defaqto report [P23] shows Effect of Switching of £2,000/1.9% (low), £2,000/1.6% (mid) and £3,000/2.0% (high), compared to the suitability report's £10,400/9.6%, £12,000/9.4% and £15,000/10.0% — the suitability report shows an effect roughly 5x larger.
      - [P10] illustration states the RIY after all charges reduces growth from 2.9% to 0.8%, a reduction of 2.1%, which matches the suitability report's Low scenario RIY (2.1%) but the illustration is presented on a mid-growth basis (5% gross / 2.9% real), creating a labelling inconsistency between the illustration and the suitability report's growth rate categories.
      - The illustration [P3] uses growth rates of 1.9% (lower), 5% (medium) and 8% (higher) while the suitability report uses 0.00% (low), 2.95% (mid) and 5.88% (high) — different growth rate assumptions across documents, though this is expected given prescribed vs report-specific rates (guard applies).
      - The suitability report and [P1] both show the effect of switching as a large negative impact (e.g. £10,400–£15,000), whereas the Defaqto switch report [P23] shows a much smaller impact (£2,000–£3,000), suggesting a material difference in the scope or methodology of the two analyses that is unexplained in the suitability report.
    The RIY, Critical Yield and Effect of Switching figures stated in the suitability report (page 18) and reproduced in the appendix [P1] are internally consistent and the key figures (2.1%, 1.9%, £10,400, 9.6%; 2.2%, 4.8%, £12,000, 9.4%; 2.3%, 7.8%, £15,000, 10.0%) are all corroborated verbatim in [P1]. The provider illustration [P10] also corroborates the 2.1% all-in RIY figure at mid-growth, and [P11] corroborates the 4.8% critical yield and 2.94% growth rate reference. The guard regarding different growth rate assumptions between illustrations and cashflow/report scenarios is noted and does not itself constitute a finding.
    
    However, the Defaqto pension switch report [P23] presents materially different RIY, Critical Yield and Effect of Switching figures for what appears to be the same switch transaction. The Defaqto figures show a RIY of only 0.6% for the recommended solution and an effect of switching of only £2,000–£3,000, compared to the suitability report's 2.1–2.3% RIY and £10,400–£15,000 effect. The most likely explanation is that [P23] excludes adviser charges (1% ongoing + £3,305 initial) whereas the suitability report figures include them — this is consistent with the suitability report appendix [P17] explicitly noting that the critical yield calculation includes the initial charge of £3,305 and ongoing annual charge of 1.00%. The Defaqto report [P23] separately discusses 'The effect of advice charges' as a distinct section, suggesting its comparison table excludes them.
    
    While this difference may be explainable by the inclusion/exclusion of adviser charges, the suitability report does not cross-reference or reconcile the Defaqto figures with its own appendix figures, nor does it explain why the two documents in the file present such divergent switching impacts. The large gap (£10,400 vs £2,000 at low growth) is unexplained within the suitability report itself, and a reader relying on the Defaqto report alone would receive a substantially more favourable impression of the switch's cost impact. This creates a material risk of inconsistency across file documents without adequate explanation, and warrants a Potential Concern at Moderate severity — the figures are arithmetically plausible once adviser charges are factored in, but the lack of explicit reconciliation between the two analyses represents a material clarification gap.

  [G7.4] Cost comparison with alternative options
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report (page 11, 'Pension Switch Charges Only Assessment') states: (1) EA3 (Aviva SP50163762): existing 0.50% / £18.72 pa, impact -0.06% / -£2.25; (2) EA4 (Zurich P10026-719): existing 0.93% / £961.98 pa, impact -0.49% / -£506.85; (3) EA5 (Standard Life D2301334000): existing 0.18% / £186.19 pa, impact +0.26% / +£268.94. Overall the Aviva plan is stated to be '0.24% less expensive than your current plans' and the proposed plan 'can underperform your current plan by £264 in the first year'.
    File:     [P1] (suitability report existing arrangements table, page 5) shows: Aviva charge 0.50% fund; Zurich charge 0.18% fund + £2.12/month admin; Standard Life charge 0.52% fund. [P3] (suitability report page 11 switch charges table) shows Aviva existing 0.50% / £18.72, Zurich existing 0.93% / £961.98, Standard Life existing 0.18% / £186.19, with impacts -0.06%/-£2.25, -0.49%/-£506.85, +0.26%/+£268.94 respectively, and the £264 underperformance figure. [P6] (Pension switch report) shows investment charge of 0.18% for the fund at 100%. [P2] (Zurich Policy Info) shows an annual management charge of ¾% per year (0.75%) plus a bid-to-offer spread of 5%. [P8] (Standard Life illustration) shows fund charge 1.0%, discount -0.5%, net 0.5% — not 0.52% and not 0.18%. [P13] (Pension switch report) shows projected value existing £141,000, new solution £138,000, critical yield 6.3%, effect of switching -£3,000 or -2.1%. [P21] (suitability report cashflow/critical yield appendix) shows mid-growth critical yield 4.8% for new solution, and mid-growth projected value existing £127,000 vs new £115,000. [P19] (suitability report) states required outperformance of £4,672 in first year.
    Discrepancies:
      - EA5 (Standard Life): report page 5 states fund charge 0.52%; the switch comparison table (page 11) uses 0.18% as the existing charge for EA5. These are contradictory within the report itself. 0.18% belongs to EA4 (Zurich), not EA5. Standard Life's own illustration ([P8]) shows 1.0% gross / 0.5% net charge, neither 0.52% nor 0.18%.
      - EA4 (Zurich): report page 5 states Zurich charge as 0.18% fund + £2.12/month admin. The switch comparison table uses 0.93% for Zurich. Zurich policy documentation ([P2]) shows 0.75% AMC plus a 5% bid-offer spread, not 0.93% or 0.18%. The 0.93% figure is not corroborated by provider documentation in the pack.
      - EA5 existing charge of 0.18% in the switch table appears to be the Zurich fund charge (EA4) misapplied to Standard Life (EA5), suggesting rows may be transposed.
      - EA5 monetary charge: 0.18% of Standard Life fund value £3,002 = £5.40, not £186.19. £186.19 at 0.18% implies a fund of ~£103,439 (the Zurich fund value), confirming row transposition.
      - EA4 monetary charge: 0.93% of Zurich fund £103,439.24 = £961.98 — arithmetic is internally consistent for EA4 using the 0.93% rate, but that rate is not corroborated by Zurich provider documentation (Zurich shows 0.75% AMC per [P2]).
      - EA3 (Aviva): 0.50% of £3,744.36 = £18.72 — arithmetic is consistent; charge rate matches page 5 ([P1]). No Aviva provider illustration in the pack to corroborate independently.
      - Standard Life charge in the provider illustration ([P8]) is net 0.5% (1.0% less 0.5% discount), contradicting the report's page 5 figure of 0.52% and the switch table's 0.18%.
      - The overall saving of -0.24% / -£264 is derived from the per-arrangement figures; if two of those rows are transposed or use incorrect rates, the aggregate comparison figure is also unreliable.
      - Critical yield figures are inconsistent between the pension switch report ([P13]: 6.3%) and the suitability report appendix ([P21]: mid-growth 4.8%), with no reconciliation.
      - The switch comparison covers only three of the five existing arrangements (EA3, EA4, EA5); EA1 (People's Pension, retained) and EA2 (Scottish Widows, transfer value £211.32) are not in the switch table — this is likely appropriate given retention/small value, but EA2's omission is not explained in the table.
    The most significant finding is an apparent row transposition in the Pension Switch Charges Only Assessment table at page 11. The existing charge for EA5 (Standard Life, fund value £3,002) is stated as 0.18% / £186.19. Arithmetically, £186.19 ÷ 0.18% = ~£103,439, which is the Zurich fund value (EA4), not the Standard Life fund value. Conversely, the impact row for EA5 of +0.26% / £268.94 also implies a fund of ~£103,439. This strongly suggests the Standard Life row in the switch table has been populated using Zurich-sized figures, i.e. the Zurich charge rate has been applied to the Zurich fund value but labelled as Standard Life. The Zurich row (EA4) at 0.93% / £961.98 is arithmetically consistent with the Zurich fund value of £103,439, but 0.93% is not corroborated by the Zurich policy information in the pack, which shows an AMC of 0.75% plus a 5% bid-offer spread. The Standard Life illustration in the pack shows a net ongoing charge of 0.5% (1.0% gross less 0.5% discount), which matches neither the 0.52% stated on page 5 of the suitability report nor the 0.18% in the switch table. The EA3 (Aviva) row is arithmetically consistent (0.50% × £3,744.36 = £18.72) and the charge matches the report's own page 5 disclosure, though no independent Aviva provider document is in the pack to corroborate. Because two of the three rows in the switch table appear to use incorrect or misattributed charge figures, the aggregate -0.24% / -£264 cost saving claim cannot be verified and is likely incorrect. The report's central value-for-money conclusion — that the recommended plan is cheaper — rests on this table. An additional inconsistency exists between the critical yield shown in the pension switch report appendix (6.3% per [P13]) and the suitability report appendix (4.8% mid-growth per [P21]); this compounds the unreliability of the cost comparison. The vulnerability overlay does not appear to materially affect this specific arithmetic check beyond noting that an inaccurate cost comparison is harmful to any client.

  [G7.5] Benefit versus cost — fair value
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The report asserts seven distinct benefits to justify the recommendation: (1) Lower overall cost structure compared to existing pension (page 7); (2) Lifestyling risk — existing funds will move outside agreed risk range (page 7); (3) Greater investment choice (page 7); (4) Adviser charging facilitation (page 7); (5) Ongoing monitoring and adjustments (page 7); (6) Multi-asset fund diversification (page 12); (7) Enhanced retirement flexibility (page 7). For cost justification, the report states the critical yield is 4.8% per year to age 75 and that 'based on the past performance of your recommended funds, I do believe that this is achievable' (page 11), cites stronger performance of recommended Aviva funds versus existing Aviva holdings (page 13), and states the Aviva platform funds align with agreed risk tolerance and have delivered comparable performance with reduced volatility (page 13). The report also contains an internal inconsistency noted in the extraction: the Aviva existing arrangement charges 0.50% and the new solution charges 0.44%, yet text on page 6 states 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' — which arithmetically is consistent (0.50% minus 0.44% = 0.06% saving), but the extraction flags the text as contradictory. The solution total ongoing charge is 0.44% (0.21% platform + 0.22% investment management + 0.01% unaccounted rounding) plus adviser charges not fully itemised in the canonical model excerpt. The lower-cost claim therefore relates primarily to the Aviva-to-Aviva switch component.
    File:     P1 and P7 provide a 5-year performance chart and table comparing existing Aviva My Future Focus Consolidation (A), recommended Aviva Risk 5 portfolio (B), IA Mixed Investment benchmark (C) and Zurich Managed Pn AP (D). The recommended funds (B) outperform existing Aviva (A) over 5 years (42.40% vs 15.58% per P7/P1) but do NOT outperform Zurich (D: 44.32% over 5 years per P7). P2 states explicitly: 'The recommended Aviva advised funds have demonstrated stronger performance compared to your existing Aviva holdings. However, they have not outperformed your current Zurich funds.' P2 also states the Aviva platform funds align with agreed risk tolerance and have delivered comparable performance with reduced volatility relative to Zurich. P3 provides the SIPP research report with fund performance for CT Universal MAP Balanced C Acc and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc. P6 shows the fund recommendation table with annual charges of 0.18% (HSBC) and 0.29% (CT Universal MAP). P19 (file notes) confirms alternative options were discounted: Zurich has no risk level 5 funds, Standard Life funds in the same range are more expensive than what Aviva offers. P8 provides policy research confirming lifestyle strategy status of existing plans. No provider illustration is present in the retrieved passages to confirm projected values, RIY, or critical yield calculation. No aggregated all-in cost figure (including adviser charges) is visible in the retrieved passages.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report justifies the lower-cost claim primarily against the Aviva existing arrangement (0.50% vs 0.44%), but the Zurich arrangement is the largest holding (£103,439.24) and carries a 0.93% charge; the new solution at 0.44% is cheaper than Zurich, yet the report does not explicitly calculate or state the overall weighted average charge saving across all four switching plans versus the new single solution — no aggregated cost comparison table is cited in the retrieved evidence.
      - The report's critical yield justification states 'based on the past performance of your recommended funds, I do believe that this is achievable' at 4.8% per year. P1 and P7 show the recommended Aviva Risk 5 portfolio achieved 42.40% cumulative over 5 years (approximately 7.3% annualised), which superficially supports the claim. However, the critical yield figure of 4.24% (per the computed figures) conflicts with the report's stated 4.8% — one of these figures is either a report error or refers to a different arrangement, and no provider illustration is present in the retrieved passages to resolve this.
      - The report states the recommended funds outperform existing holdings. P2 explicitly states the recommended funds have NOT outperformed the Zurich funds over 5 years (44.32% Zurich vs 42.40% recommended). The report's cost justification relies partly on outperformance to justify switching the Zurich plan, but the performance comparison does not support outperformance for the largest holding.
      - No provider illustration, RIY figure, or cumulative cost projection is present in the retrieved passages, making it impossible to verify the critical yield calculation or projected end values.
      - The canonical model's charge arithmetic shows the solution total ongoing of £484.81 at 0.44% implies a fund value of £110,184, not the total of all switching plans (£116,998.47 minus the People's Pension retained £6,601.55 = £110,396.92). A small unexplained gap of approximately £213 exists between the implied fund value and the expected transfer-in amount.
      - Adviser ongoing charge rate is not confirmed in the retrieved passages against the client agreement, so no reconciliation can be performed for this cost layer.
    The report's lower-cost claim is partially supported: P19 confirms Standard Life funds are more expensive and Zurich has no suitable risk-5 funds, and the charge arithmetic (0.93% Zurich vs 0.44% new solution) supports cost saving on the largest plan. However, three material concerns arise. First, the performance justification for switching Zurich is weakened by P2's explicit statement that recommended funds have NOT outperformed Zurich over 5 years; the report's reliance on past performance to justify critical yield achievability is therefore partially undermined for the largest plan component. Second, the critical yield figure stated in the report (4.8%) does not match the computed figure (4.24%) derived from the canonical model's own numbers; without a provider illustration in the pack, this cannot be resolved, meaning the quantitative cost justification cannot be verified. Third, no aggregated all-in cost figure encompassing all charge layers (platform, investment management, adviser) is present in the retrieved passages, so the cumulative impact on the client has not been demonstrated from the evidence available. The vulnerability overlay: no vulnerability characteristics are recorded in the retrieved fact find passages (P10–P17) that would materially alter this assessment. The guard regarding 'achievability' as a claim rather than analysis applies here: the statement that 4.8% outperformance is achievable based on past performance is a bare assertion, and P2's data partially contradicts it for the Zurich component. These combine to constitute a Potential Concern at High severity given that: (a) the critical yield figures are irreconcilable between the report and the computed model without an illustration; (b) the performance justification for the largest plan component is contradicted by evidence in the file; and (c) no aggregated all-in cost has been demonstrated.

      · Suitability Report Test 1.md [I] (P2)
        "the Aviva platform funds selected for you align with your agreed risk tolerance and have delivered comparable performance with reduced volatility."

  [G7.6] Costs are excessive relative to the benefits provided
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The suitability report makes two distinct and co-existing cost comparison statements on page 11. First, it states: 'As we have agreed to be remunerated via the policy (initial and ongoing), our calculations showed that the Aviva plan is 4.24% more expensive than your current plans.' Second, on the same page, it states: 'our calculations show that the Aviva plan is 0.24% less expensive than your current plans.' The requiredOutperformancePercentage field records 4.24% (including adviser fees) and the differencePercentage field records -0.24% (annual recurring charges).
    File:     This requirement is assessed from the report alone — it compares the report against itself. No separate supporting document was searched for or retrieved. The canonical model records both figures as 'Stated' on page 11, meaning both appear in the report itself.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report contains two directly contradictory statements on the same page (page 11): one stating the Aviva plan is 4.24% MORE expensive than current plans (including adviser fees), and another stating the Aviva plan is 0.24% LESS expensive than current plans (annual recurring charges). These two statements cannot both be simultaneously correct as an overall cost comparison without a clear and explicit reconciliation distinguishing what each figure represents and to which cost basis each applies.
      - The basis for the 4.24% figure is described as 'including adviser fees' while the 0.24% figure is described as 'annual recurring charges', but the report does not explicitly explain to the reader how these two figures relate to each other, which is the true like-for-like comparison, or what net conclusion the client should draw about whether the recommendation costs more or less overall.
      - The charge arithmetic derived from the canonical model shows that the solution total ongoing charge of £484.81 at 0.44% implies a fund value of approximately £110,184, which corresponds to the recommended transfer value (£110,185) rather than the total of all existing arrangement values (£116,998.47). This means the percentage comparisons are computed on different bases — existing charges are applied to existing fund values while new charges are applied to the (lower) transfer value — and this is not explained in the report.
      - The canonical model's own internal inconsistency note flags that page 6 states 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' yet the pension switch charges table implies the new charge is 0.06% cheaper, indicating a further directional inconsistency within the report on this topic.
    The comparison is performed entirely within the report itself, as directed. The report presents two cost comparison figures on the same page without adequately reconciling them for the reader. The 4.24% figure (including adviser fees, i.e. the required outperformance or true all-in cost uplift) and the -0.24% figure (annual recurring charges, i.e. the ongoing product/platform/fund charge comparison excluding adviser fees) measure fundamentally different things. A sophisticated reader might infer this distinction, but the report does not state it clearly. A client reading both sentences in sequence would reasonably be confused about whether the recommendation costs more or less. The guard indicates that a higher cost is not automatically poor value if benefits are stated — and the report does reference consolidation, platform benefits and ongoing adviser service as justification. However, the guard requires testing: the 4.24% required outperformance figure is a substantial hurdle, representing the combined weight of initial and ongoing adviser charges on top of the product costs. The report does not explicitly set out what benefits are expected to justify this 4.24% outperformance requirement. The -0.24% saving on annual recurring charges is materially undermined by the 4.24% all-in cost figure. There is no aggregated narrative that says, in effect, 'excluding adviser charges the plan is 0.24% cheaper per year, but including our fees the plan needs to outperform by 4.24% in total — and here is why that is justified by [specific benefits].' The absence of this reconciliation and benefit-to-cost bridge constitutes a material presentation concern. The arithmetic issue (different fund values used as the denominator for existing vs. new charges) adds a further layer of opacity. There is no vulnerability flag in the evidence that materially alters this assessment. Overall, a potential concern arises from the contradictory and unreconciled cost statements and the absence of a clear explanation of the benefits justifying the 4.24% all-in cost premium.
      · Canonical model — /costsAndCharges/valueAssessment/requiredOutperformancePercentage [CostsAndCharges]
        "As we have agreed to be remunerated via the policy (initial and ongoing), our calculations showed that the Aviva plan is 4.24% more expensive than your current plans."
      · Canonical model — /costsAndCharges/comparison/differencePercentage [CostsAndCharges]
        "our calculations show that the Aviva plan is 0.24% less expensive than your current plans"

  [G7.7] Alternatives provide better cost or benefit to the client
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The canonical model holds no value at the comparativeCost path for any alternatives considered. The suitability report does, however, contain cost-related justifications for each switch recommendation. For the Aviva switch it states 'Lower Costs - The receiving scheme has a lower overall cost structure... The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' (this language is internally contradictory — 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution means the existing plan is more expensive, but the wording is ambiguous). For the Standard Life switch it states the plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution'. For the Zurich switch, cost is not the primary driver; instead investment choice, fund blend inadequacy, and retirement flexibility are cited. Alternatives considered include Scottish Widows (discounted — no suitable single multi-asset fund at risk 5, no adviser management, no flexible access), Aviva internal switch (discounted — only mixed asset fund available is risk rated 4, not meeting ATR), Zurich internal switch (discounted — no risk level 5 funds, blend of 7 and 2 used, blend underperformed), and Standard Life internal switch (discounted — all funds in 20-60% equity range more expensive than Aviva). A pension switching research report (Defaqto) provides projected maturity value comparisons at low, mid and high growth rates for each switch.
    File:     The pension switch research report provides side-by-side projected maturity values for each existing pension versus the new solution. For the overall comparison (all switches combined), at mid growth rate the new solution plus retained plans projects £127,000 total maturity value versus retaining all existing plans. For Zurich only at low growth rate: existing £102,000 RIY 0.3%; new solution £100,000 RIY 0.6%; critical yield 0.3%; effect of switching £2,000 or 2.0% decrease. For the full comparison the new solution shows 0.6% RIY versus retained Zurich at 0.29% and retained Aviva at 0.59%. The file notes confirm: Scottish Widows discounted for no servicing and reduced flexibility; Aviva internal discounted as only mixed asset fund is risk rated 4; Zurich internal discounted as no risk level 5 funds and blend underperformed; Standard Life internal discounted as all funds more expensive than Aviva. The Zurich switch specifically shows a higher RIY (0.6% vs 0.3%) and a projected decrease in maturity value at low growth rate of £2,000 (2.0%). The suitability report states for Zurich that cost is not the primary justification and instead relies on investment choice and flexibility benefits. The charge comparison table in the suitability report shows Aviva existing 0.50% vs new solution 0.44% (impact -0.06%), and separately a table showing Zurich existing charges. The Defaqto switching report confirms the new solution RIY of 0.6% across all scenarios, which is higher than the Zurich existing RIY of 0.3%.
    Discrepancies:
      - The suitability report states for the Aviva switch 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' — this wording is ambiguous and potentially contradictory: if 'the plan' refers to the existing Aviva plan, this is consistent with the charge table showing existing 0.50% vs new 0.44% (-0.06%). However, the phrase 'the plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' could be read as the recommended solution being 0.06% more expensive, which would contradict the lower-cost claim.
      - The Zurich-only switching analysis in the Defaqto report shows that at low growth rate the new solution produces a lower projected value (£100,000 vs £102,000) and a higher RIY (0.6% vs 0.3%), with critical yield of 0.3% required. The suitability report does not explicitly acknowledge the cost disadvantage of switching from Zurich in monetary/RIY terms or quantify the benefits needed to overcome the higher charges.
      - The overall comparison table in the Defaqto report shows a total projected maturity value of £127,000 (new solution + retained) vs retaining all plans — no explicit 'retain all' total is shown in the retrieved passages for direct comparison at mid growth, making it impossible to confirm the net benefit claimed.
      - The suitability report's charge comparison for Zurich shows existing annual charge 0.93% (£961.98) and impact of recommended alternative 0.26% (£268.94), implying a saving, but the Defaqto RIY comparison shows new solution RIY 0.6% vs existing 0.3%, indicating the new solution is more expensive on a total cost basis when all charges are included. This is a material discrepancy between the charge table and the RIY figures.
      - The canonical model records no value at /recommendations[]/alternativesConsidered[]/comparativeCost, meaning the report's structured cost comparison for alternatives is absent from the extracted model, though narrative justifications are present in the report text.
      - The Defaqto switching report includes a stakeholder pension comparison section (listed in contents) but no substantive content from that section appears in the retrieved passages, so it is unclear whether a stakeholder pension alternative was properly considered and documented.
    The suitability report does disclose charges for the recommended solution (0.21% platform + 0.22% investment management = 0.44% total) and contains a pension switch charges table comparing existing and new charges for each plan being switched. Non-cost reasons are documented for discounting alternatives (Scottish Widows, Aviva internal, Zurich internal, Standard Life internal), and the file notes corroborate these reasons. A guard applies: cheaper options rejected for evidenced non-cost reasons (fund range, access, servicing) are a valid outcome, and the evidence supports the non-cost justifications for Zurich, Scottish Widows, and Aviva internal.
    
    However, there is a material concern: the Defaqto switching report shows that for the Zurich switch specifically, the new solution carries a higher RIY (0.6% vs 0.3%) and produces a lower projected value at low growth rate. The suitability report's charge comparison table appears to show a cost saving (existing Zurich 0.93% vs impact 0.26%), but this reflects only the difference in product/platform/investment charges — the RIY in the Defaqto report captures the cumulative effect including advice charges, and that figure is higher for the new solution. The suitability report does not explicitly reconcile this discrepancy or quantify what investment outperformance or non-cost benefits are required to justify the higher overall charge burden on the Zurich fund (which at £103,439 is by far the largest fund being switched).
    
    The ambiguous wording 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' in the Aviva switch section could mislead a reader into thinking the new solution is more expensive, when the intent appears to be the opposite. This is a low-level concern given the context makes the direction of saving apparent from the charge table.
    
    The most significant issue is the absence of an explicit acknowledgement in the suitability report that the Zurich switch results in a higher RIY and lower projected value at low growth, and a clear benefits statement quantifying what the client gains in exchange for that cost increase. The report relies on investment choice and flexibility benefits but does not set these against the quantified cost disadvantage. This falls short of clearly explaining benefits expected in exchange for higher costs where higher costs arise.

  [G7.9] The service proposition attached to the ongoing adviser charge
  Potential Concern — severity Low
    Report:   The suitability report (page 17, 'Future contact and ongoing services') states that for an ongoing charge of 1% per annum the client will receive: (1) reviewing attitude to risk and capacity for loss; (2) reviewing ongoing suitability of the investment portfolio; (3) market updates and legislative updates; (4) access to a professional, experienced and qualified team of financial advisers for ad-hoc advice; (5) access to full holistic financial planning. Page 4 adds a commitment to keep the risk profile under continuous review in line with ongoing review meetings. Review frequency is stated as annual. The first-year monetary equivalent is stated as circa £1,101.85.
    File:     The client agreement (TFAS Wealth Client Agreement Privacy.md) confirms an ongoing annual review management charge of 1% (subject to a minimum of £100) taken automatically from funds invested. The 'Important Information' document (IMPORT_2.md) describes the ongoing service as including: a review meeting at least once a year (with possible bed and ISA, fund switches, drawdown withdrawals, portfolio rebalance, updated fact find and attitude to risk); statements and performance reports twice per year; cash flow analysis when relevant; family benefit; regular market updates; quarterly e-magazine; online access for portfolio valuations; and access to an adviser between reviews. The 1% charge is confirmed at the same rate. The suitability report itself (P15/P16) also restates the service list and cost. The research report (P17) notes the adviser charge on the Aviva platform is applicable.
    Discrepancies:
      - The suitability report service list omits several items that appear in the client agreement's service description: statements and performance reports twice per year, cash flow analysis, family benefit, quarterly e-magazine, and online portfolio access. These are present in IMPORT_2.md but not in the report.
      - The suitability report does not mention that statements and performance reports are provided twice per year, which is a material deliverable described in the client agreement.
      - The suitability report does not reference the quarterly e-magazine or online portfolio access described in IMPORT_2.md.
      - The suitability report does not reference the family benefit described in IMPORT_2.md.
      - The first-year monetary equivalent of £1,101.85 is stated in the suitability report (P15) but is not cross-referenced to a specific fund value basis in the report narrative, creating a minor presentation gap (though arithmetically it is broadly consistent with 1% of approximately £110,185).
    The core requirement — that the ongoing charge of 1% is disclosed and a service proposition is attached to it — is met. The rate matches exactly between the suitability report, the client agreement (P1/P8) and the service description document (P8). The annual review frequency is confirmed in both the report and the client agreement. The service items listed in the report (risk review, portfolio suitability review, market/legislative updates, ad-hoc adviser access, holistic planning) are all present in or consistent with the client agreement's service description. The discrepancies identified are that the report lists a subset of the full service described in the client agreement: it omits bi-annual statements, cash flow analysis, family benefit, e-magazine and online access. These omissions mean the report understates the breadth of service rather than overstating it, so they do not represent a false or unsupported claim. However, COBS and the check's own decision logic require that the service proposition is adequately described so the client can assess value for money; a partial description that omits material deliverables (particularly bi-annual statements and online access, which are operationally significant) is a moderate presentation gap. There is no contradiction between what the report says and what the agreement says — only incompleteness. The vulnerability overlay does not appear to be engaged here: no specific vulnerability-related communication or service adjustment needs are identified in the evidence that would affect this particular assessment. The outcome is Potential Concern at Low-to-Moderate severity: the charge rate and annual review are fully corroborated, but the service description in the report is incomplete relative to the client agreement.

  [G7.10] Benefits relied upon to justify a higher cost
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts seven benefits to justify the switch to Aviva: (1) Lower Costs — the receiving scheme has a lower overall cost structure; (2) Lifestyling Risk — existing funds will eventually move outside agreed risk range; (3) Greater Investment Choice — wider range of investment options; (4) Adviser Charging Facilitation — charges deducted directly from plan; (5) Ongoing Monitoring and Adjustments — active monitoring by adviser; (6) Multi-Asset Diversification — professionally managed diversified solution; (7) Enhanced Retirement Flexibility — more flexible retirement options including tax-efficient withdrawals. Cost justification relies on: outperformance belief based on past fund performance against a critical yield of 4.8% (stated elsewhere as 4.24% per the charge arithmetic); historical performance of recommended Aviva funds vs existing Aviva holdings; risk-adjusted performance of Aviva platform funds; and ongoing review service comprising ATR review, investment suitability review, market updates, legislation changes, ad-hoc advice and holistic planning.
    File:     The evidence shows: (1) The report's own pension switch charges table (P21) shows the recommended solution costs 0.06% less than existing Zurich (0.44% vs 0.50%), but the report text on page 6 states 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' — a self-contradictory statement within the report. The canonical model flags this as an internal inconsistency. The charge arithmetic in the evidence pack shows the 0.44% solution total is calculated against a fund value of ~£110,184, not the sum of all transferred arrangements (~£116,998). (2) P10 (SIPP research report) shows MiFID II pre-sale cost disclosures: HSBC Global Strategy Balanced ongoing 0.18%, transaction 0.01%, total 0.19%; CT Universal MAP Balanced ongoing 0.29%, transaction 0.26%, total 0.55%; aggregate fund costs £406.74 (0.37%). The suitability report fund table (P22) shows only annual charges of 0.18% and 0.29% respectively and does not disclose transaction costs. (3) P7 (policy research) confirms several existing arrangements (Aviva My Future Focus, Scottish Widows) are invested in lifestyle strategies. P21 (suitability report) references Zurich's fund limitation — no risk level 5 funds, requiring a blend of risk 7 and risk 2, supporting the lifestyling/investment choice benefit for Zurich. (4) P19 (suitability report) confirms Scottish Widows and People's Pension do not allow a servicing adviser; Aviva Pension Portfolio does (via facilitated adviser charging per P4 Key Features). (5) P4 and P8 (Aviva Key Features) confirm flexi-access drawdown, UFPLS, annuity and phased drawdown options, supporting the retirement flexibility benefit. (6) P19 comparison table shows People's Pension offers FAD and UFPLS; Scottish Widows offers UFPLS only; existing Aviva offers FAD and UFPLS; Zurich offers UFPLS only; Standard Life offers FAD. So the flexibility benefit is genuine for Zurich and Scottish Widows but partially already available in People's Pension and existing Aviva. (7) The SIPP research report (P3, P12) identifies Aviva Pension Portfolio — Choice as the recommended product from a panel of 118, supporting product selection rationale. (8) No provider illustration for the recommended Aviva plan is present in the evidence pack to confirm projected values, RIY or critical yield calculations. (9) The critical yield is stated as 4.8% in the report narrative but the charge arithmetic implies 4.24% based on the stated £4,672 required outperformance against £110,188 fund value. (10) The CT Universal MAP transaction costs (0.26%, £145.05) are material but not disclosed in the suitability report's fund charge table.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report claims 'lower overall cost structure' as a benefit on page 7, but the report itself contains an internal inconsistency on page 6 stating 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' which contradicts the lower cost claim (though the switch charges table shows 0.44% new vs 0.50% existing, implying the new is cheaper — the text contradiction is unresolved).
      - Critical yield stated as 4.8% in the report narrative (page 11) but charge arithmetic implies 4.24% based on the figures extracted from the canonical model, creating an unreconciled discrepancy.
      - Fund charge table in the suitability report (page 12/P22) discloses only annual management charges (HSBC 0.18%, CT Universal MAP 0.29%) but does not disclose transaction costs; P10 shows CT Universal MAP transaction costs of 0.26% (£145.05) which are material and would increase the total fund cost to 0.55% for that fund — the aggregate disclosed in P10 is 0.37% total vs 0.24% ongoing only.
      - The solution charge arithmetic is applied to a fund value of ~£110,185 (transfer value to Aviva) not the aggregate of all existing arrangements (£116,998.47); the lower-cost benefit comparison is therefore not applied to the full existing arrangement base.
      - Enhanced Retirement Flexibility benefit is stated as a general benefit of the new scheme, but People's Pension (retained, not transferred) and existing Aviva already offer FAD and UFPLS, meaning the flexibility benefit is not uniformly additional across all ceding plans.
      - No provider illustration for the recommended Aviva Pension Portfolio is present in the evidence pack, so projected values, RIY and critical yield cannot be independently verified against illustration documentation.
      - The ongoing review service described on page 16 (ATR review, investment suitability, market updates, ad-hoc advice, holistic planning) is asserted as justification for the ongoing adviser charge but no client agreement or fee disclosure document confirming the ongoing adviser charge rate is present in the evidence pack to verify the service proposition matches the charge agreed.
    The report relies on seven stated benefits and four cost-justification assertions to support the recommendation. Several of these are corroborated by the evidence: the lifestyling risk benefit is supported by P7 confirming existing arrangements (including Aviva My Future Focus and Scottish Widows) are in lifestyle strategies; the investment choice benefit for Zurich is supported by P21's own acknowledgement that Zurich lacks risk-5 funds; the adviser charging facilitation benefit is supported by P4 Key Features and P19 comparison table showing ceding plans do not allow servicing advisers; retirement flexibility is supported by P4/P8 Key Features and P19 comparison table, though this benefit is already partially available on People's Pension and existing Aviva, limiting its incremental value as a justification. The multi-asset diversification and ongoing monitoring benefits are generic and not contradicted by evidence, though their value as specific justifications is limited. However, three material concerns arise. First, the lower cost claim — the primary financial justification — is undermined by an internal contradiction in the report itself (page 6 text vs the switch charges table), which is flagged in the canonical model's internalInconsistencies. The guard on false positives does not apply here because the contradiction is within the report's own pages, not between the report and an external document. Second, the CT Universal MAP transaction costs of 0.26% disclosed in P10 are not disclosed in the suitability report's fund charge table, meaning the aggregated all-in cost presented to the client is understated. This is a moderate omission because the MiFID II pre-sale disclosure in P10 does contain this figure and was presumably provided to the client, but the suitability report itself does not draw attention to it or explain its impact on the cost comparison. Third, the critical yield figure is inconsistent between the report narrative (4.8%) and the arithmetic implied by the canonical model (4.24%), and no provider illustration is available to resolve this. The absence of a provider illustration also means the projected value and RIY cannot be verified. The vulnerability overlay: no vulnerability characteristics are identified in the evidence that would materially alter this assessment. Overall, the lower-cost benefit claim — the most prominent justification — is self-contradictory within the report, transaction costs for one fund are not disclosed in the suitability report charge table, and the critical yield figure cannot be reconciled or verified against an illustration. These are material concerns.

  [G7.11] Higher charges producing lower projected outcomes or an increased yield requirement
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The report asserts two separate cost conclusions that are in direct tension. First, it states 'our calculations show that the Aviva plan is 0.24% less expensive than your current plans' (page 11) — a 'RecommendedCheaper' direction. Second, in the same section it states 'As we have agreed to be remunerated via the policy (initial and ongoing), our calculations showed that the Aviva plan is 4.24% more expensive than your current plans' (page 11). The report also discloses a critical yield of 4.8% at a mid-growth rate of 2.94% to match the existing plan value, and a required monetary outperformance of £4,672 in year one. Projected values at age 75 are stated as £97,600 (low/0%), £115,000 (mid/2.95%) and £135,000 (high/5.88%), with RIY figures of 2.1%, 2.2% and 2.3% respectively for the new solution, against 0.3% for the existing. Critical yields are stated as 1.9%, 4.8% and 7.8% across the three growth scenarios.
    File:     The pension switching report (passages P10, P3, P5, P16) corroborates the projected values for the new solution (£97,600 low, £115,000 mid, £135,000 high at age 75) and the corresponding RIY figures (2.1%, 2.2%, 2.3% new solution; 0.3% existing). The critical yields of 1.9%, 4.8% and 7.8% are confirmed in P10. The existing pension projected values are higher in every scenario: £108,000 (low), £127,000 (mid), £150,000 (high) versus new solution £97,600, £115,000 and £135,000 — a consistent shortfall. P16 corroborates the 4.8% critical yield at a mid-growth rate of 2.94% and the £4,672 required outperformance. The switching report shows the new solution produces materially lower projected outcomes across all three growth rates, with the effect of switching shown as a £10,400–£15,000 decrease (9.4–10.0%). The 0.24% cheaper claim is not corroborated anywhere in the evidence; the switching report shows the new solution RIY of 2.1–2.3% versus the existing 0.3%, implying the new solution is substantially more expensive on charges once adviser fees are included. P3 (Zurich-only alternative comparison) shows RIY of 0.6% vs 0.3% and critical yields of 0.3%/3.2%/6.2%, which differ from the all-plan comparison figures in P10.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report simultaneously claims the recommended plan is '0.24% less expensive' (RecommendedCheaper direction) and '4.24% more expensive' (when including adviser fees) — these two statements are directly contradictory and cannot both be true as a single characterisation of the recommendation's cost impact.
      - The '0.24% less expensive' figure is not corroborated by any passage in the evidence pack; no document shows the new solution as cheaper on an excluding-adviser-fee basis.
      - The new solution produces lower projected outcomes than the existing pension across all three growth scenarios (low: £97,600 vs £108,000; mid: £115,000 vs £127,000; high: £135,000 vs £150,000), yet the report asserts a 'RecommendedCheaper' direction without clearly explaining this as an exclusively pre-adviser-fee comparison.
      - The critical yield of 4.8% at mid-growth of 2.94%/2.95% significantly exceeds the assumed growth rate, meaning the recommended solution is projected to underperform the existing arrangement at every growth scenario disclosed; this is not explained as a benefit trade-off in the report.
      - The report states the growth rate basis for the mid projection as both 2.94% (page 11 critical yield quote) and 2.95% (page 18 projection quote) — a minor but present discrepancy across the document.
      - The RIY for the new solution (2.1–2.3%) is materially higher than the existing arrangement (0.3%), yet the report characterises the recommendation as cheaper, which is inconsistent with the charge data.
      - The '4.24% more expensive' figure when including adviser fees is not directly matched in the switching report evidence (nearest corroborated figure is 4.8% critical yield), creating ambiguity about what the 4.24% represents relative to the 4.8% critical yield.
    The central and most serious issue in this check is the direct internal contradiction within the suitability report itself: the report asserts the recommendation is simultaneously '0.24% less expensive' and '4.24% more expensive' than the existing plans — the former excluding adviser fees, the latter including them. While it is possible to present both figures as components of a complete disclosure, the report designates the overall direction as 'RecommendedCheaper,' which is misleading because on an all-in basis (the basis that matters to the client) the recommendation is more expensive and produces materially lower projected outcomes across every growth scenario. The '0.24% cheaper' figure is not corroborated by any passage in the evidence pack. The switching report evidence (P10) clearly shows the new solution projections are £10,400–£15,000 lower than the existing pension at every growth rate, with a RIY of 2.1–2.3% versus the existing 0.3%. The critical yield of 4.8% at mid-growth (2.94/2.95%) substantially exceeds the assumed growth rate, meaning the plan must grow at nearly twice the assumed rate just to match what the existing arrangement would deliver — an inherently unfavourable comparison. The report does attempt to justify this by reference to consolidation benefits and fund access (page 11 and elsewhere in the suitability report), and the switching report confirms no guaranteed benefits are lost. However, the combination of (a) the '0.24% cheaper' assertion being unsubstantiated, (b) the contradictory labelling of the direction as 'RecommendedCheaper' when it is all-in more expensive, and (c) projected outcomes lower in every scenario without a clear, quantified explanation of what benefit justifies the cost increase, constitutes a material concern under G7.11. The 2.94% vs 2.95% growth rate discrepancy is a rounding difference and not independently material. No vulnerability factors are identified in the evidence that would materially alter this analysis, though the client's income variability (HGV driver, 'typically £300/week') noted in the extraction could bear on capacity to absorb a worse outcome — this is noted but is assessed under CHK-010 primarily. The outcome is Potential Concern at High severity because the report's own comparison table contradicts its claim of cost saving, and the all-in cost increase is unexplained relative to the projected outcome shortfall.

      [G7.8] No Issue
        - The Aviva illustration [P13] states the all-charges RIY at mid growth is 2.1% (reduces growth from 2.9% to 0.8%), whereas the suitability report states 2.2% for mid growth.         - Passage [P1] contains a different RIY/critical yield table (new solution RIY 0.6%, critical yield 3.2% at mid, effect −£2,000 / −1.6%) that relates to a narrower alternative comparison group (Aviva, Zurich and SL pensions only, not including advice fees), not the recommended all-in solution that includes advice charges. These lower figures co-exist in the file alongside the higher figures the report relies upon, creating a risk of confusion about which table applies to the main recommendation, though the report is internally consistent with [P2] and [P17].

CHK-008 — Disadvantages, Risks and Customer Understanding
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 8 requirement(s). 7 raised a concern (G8.1, G8.2, G8.3, G8.5, G8.6, G8.7, G8.8). The Defaqto pension switch report quantifies a reduction in projected value at retirement (effect of switching) for each existing pension under each growth scenario, including a specific mid-growth critical yield of 5.5% for the Aviva existing plan — the report states 4.8% critical yield without identifying which plan or growth scenario this applies to, creating a potential inconsistency or lack of precision. The pension switch report shows per-plan projected maturity values and effect-of-switching figures (e.g. £10 or 0.3% decrease for Aviva plan at mid-growth) which constitute quantified loss scenarios; the suitability report does not present a named loss scenario (e.g. projected fund value under low growth or adverse conditions) in its disadvantages section, relying instead on critical yield figures and a performance chart. The suitability report states the Aviva plan is '4.24% more expensive' than current plans, but the canonical model's own charge arithmetic (and the internal inconsistency noted in the extraction) flags that the text on page 6 states 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' — contradicting the 4.24% figure which appears to represent adviser charge impact rather than pure product cost difference, risking misleading framing. 9 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G8.1] Material disadvantages, risk factors and limitations of the recommendation
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report discloses three material disadvantages: (1) a critical yield requirement of 4.8% per year to age 75 to match current plan value at a mid-growth rate of 2.94% including advice fees; (2) the Aviva plan is 4.24% more expensive than current plans, requiring outperformance of £4,672 in the first year; (3) existing funds are in lifestyling arrangements that automatically reduce risk toward retirement, meaning the portfolio will eventually drift outside the agreed risk range. Two limitations are stated: (1) consolidating all pensions into the recommended plan would restrict flexible withdrawal access; (2) Scottish Widows does not offer a single multi-asset fund aligned to risk rating 5, requiring combination of two funds with 20–30% in money market holdings. A third limitation delegates remaining risks to product literature. One recommendation-level disadvantage is repeated three times: no guarantee the new investment will outperform existing arrangements. Tax treatment is addressed briefly: statements are based on current tax law and HMRC practice, subject to change. No explicit loss scenario (e.g. projected value at retirement under poor growth) is highlighted in the report's risk/disadvantage sections, though a performance chart and critical yield figures are present.
    File:     The pension switch report (Defaqto) provides projected maturity values for three existing pensions (Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life) under low, mid and high growth rates, with corresponding critical yields and effect-of-switching figures. For the Aviva existing plan (small fund ~£3,744), the mid-growth projected value is £3,980 existing vs £3,970 new solution (£10 or 0.3% decrease); critical yield at mid-growth is 5.5%. For Standard Life (small fund ~£3,002) and Zurich (largest fund ~£103,439), similar tables are provided. The overall comparison summary quantifies the effect of switching across all plans. The key features document (Aviva Pension Portfolio) states: value may go down as well as up; client could get back less than invested; income drawdown risks including depletion of fund if high income taken; tax position may affect income; favourable tax treatment may change if tax rules change; cancellation provisions including that previous provider not obliged to take plan back. The terms and conditions document details charge structures including adviser charges deducted from the portfolio, fund manager charges, and switching mechanics. The policy research document records that flexi-access drawdown is available on the recommended Aviva plan. The fact find records a monthly cash flow deficit of -£288 (income £1,430 vs expenditure £1,718), which is a potential vulnerability/financial resilience indicator. No explicit vulnerability flags are recorded in the fact find passages retrieved.
    Discrepancies:
      - The Defaqto pension switch report quantifies a reduction in projected value at retirement (effect of switching) for each existing pension under each growth scenario, including a specific mid-growth critical yield of 5.5% for the Aviva existing plan — the report states 4.8% critical yield without identifying which plan or growth scenario this applies to, creating a potential inconsistency or lack of precision.
      - The pension switch report shows per-plan projected maturity values and effect-of-switching figures (e.g. £10 or 0.3% decrease for Aviva plan at mid-growth) which constitute quantified loss scenarios; the suitability report does not present a named loss scenario (e.g. projected fund value under low growth or adverse conditions) in its disadvantages section, relying instead on critical yield figures and a performance chart.
      - The suitability report states the Aviva plan is '4.24% more expensive' than current plans, but the canonical model's own charge arithmetic (and the internal inconsistency noted in the extraction) flags that the text on page 6 states 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' — contradicting the 4.24% figure which appears to represent adviser charge impact rather than pure product cost difference, risking misleading framing.
      - The report's limitation on withdrawal restrictions (inability to access flexibly if all pensions consolidated) is stated, but the policy research confirms flexi-access drawdown IS available on the Aviva Pension Portfolio — the limitation as stated appears to refer to a specific consolidation scenario (e.g. retaining People's Pension separately) rather than the Aviva product itself, which may confuse the client.
      - The Key Features document identifies income drawdown-specific risks (fund depletion from high income withdrawals, impact on means-tested benefits, tax on drawdown income) that are not reflected in the suitability report's risk/disadvantage disclosures.
      - The report delegates 'all other risks' to product literature without enumerating them, while the Key Features and Terms and Conditions contain material product-specific risks (e.g. property liquidity risk, currency risk, delay in switching, cancellation rights caveat that prior provider need not accept the plan back) not individually named in the report.
      - The fact find records a monthly cash flow deficit of -£288 which suggests the client's financial resilience is limited; the suitability report does not address how this deficit interacts with ongoing adviser charges being deducted from the pension portfolio or the risk that the client cannot absorb investment losses — a material omission given the client's income position.
      - The canonical model notes /replacementAnalysis/0/featuresOrGuaranteesLost is absent from the document — features or guarantees being given up on transfer are not disclosed in the report.
      - The 'no guarantee of outperformance' disadvantage is recorded three times identically in the canonical model (for three separate recommendations), suggesting it is applied as boilerplate rather than tailored to each plan being switched.
    The suitability report provides a meaningful but incomplete risk and disadvantage disclosure. On the positive side, it contains client-specific quantified figures (4.8% critical yield, £4,672 required outperformance, 4.24% additional cost), identifies the lifestyling drift risk, and flags the withdrawal restriction and fund range constraint. These are genuine, personalised disclosures. However, several material gaps remain after applying the false-positive guards. First, the pension switch report (Defaqto) quantifies per-plan projected maturity values under low/mid/high growth and explicit effect-of-switching figures — these constitute the most material loss scenario available in the file. The suitability report does not present a named downside scenario (e.g. projected value under low growth conditions or if the critical yield is not achieved). The critical yield figure is present but is a break-even threshold, not a loss illustration. The Defaqto report's mid-growth critical yield for the Aviva plan (5.5%) differs from the 4.8% cited in the report — this may reflect aggregation across plans or a different methodology, but the discrepancy is unexplained and the report does not clarify which plans the 4.8% applies to. Second, the Key Features document contains income drawdown-specific risks that are not mentioned in the report — particularly fund depletion risk from high income withdrawals and the cancellation caveat (prior provider not obliged to accept return). These are material to a client being recommended a pension portfolio. Third, the report's 4.24% cost figure is framed as adviser charge impact, but the internal inconsistency in the extraction (page 6 text stating the plan is '0.06% more expensive') risks misleading the client about the nature of the cost difference. Fourth, features or guarantees lost on transfer are not disclosed (canonical path absent, confirmed as not present in document). Fifth, the client has a monthly cash flow deficit of -£288 per the fact find, indicating limited financial resilience. The report does not connect this to the risk that ongoing adviser charges drawn from the pension portfolio compound the cost impact. This is relevant to capacity for loss disclosure. The vulnerability overlay: the cash flow deficit is a potential driver of financial vulnerability. While no formal vulnerability flag is recorded in the retrieved passages, the deficit means the client has limited ability to absorb ongoing charge drag or investment underperformance, and this connection is absent from the report's risk disclosures. The delegation of 'all other risks' to product literature is a moderate concern — not a complete failure, but insufficient where material product-specific risks (drawdown depletion, cancellation caveats) should have been highlighted in the report itself. Overall, the most significant gap is the absence of a quantified downside loss scenario (which the Defaqto report provides but the suitability report does not present to the client), the unexplained discrepancy in critical yield figures, and the omission of features/guarantees lost on transfer. These collectively constitute a Potential Concern at moderate-to-high severity.
