  [G8.2] Product-specific and investment-specific risks explained
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report states the following product-specific and investment-specific risks: (1) AccessDelay — funds take 10-15 working days to access (p.8, Other Considerations of Switching); (2) MarketTimingRisk — market could rise or fall while transactions are pending, potentially disadvantaging the client if market rises before new fund purchase (p.8); (3) PerformanceVariability — recommended Aviva advised funds have demonstrated stronger performance vs existing Aviva holdings but have NOT outperformed current Zurich funds; Zurich investments are in higher-risk funds more susceptible to market volatility (p.13); (4) NoPerformanceGuarantee — switch does not guarantee greater pension fund at retirement; no guarantee new provider's funds will outperform existing (p.12); (5) NoOutperformanceGuarantee — no guarantee return on new investment will be greater than existing arrangement (p.8); (6) InvestmentRisk — unit prices and pension fund value can fall as well as rise; no guarantee of return of original capital, especially in early years or if income taken (p.12). These risks are stated across multiple sections of the report.
    File:     The product literature in the file (HSBC KIID, Aviva Key Features, Zurich Policy Info, CT Universal MAP KIID, SIPP research report) discloses numerous product-specific and investment-specific risks. HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio KIID: fund rated category 4 (medium fluctuations); material risks include counterparty risk, credit risk, default risk, derivatives risk; asset allocation 45-70% equities, 20-55% bonds; not appropriate for investors planning to withdraw within 5 years; historical data may not be reliable indication of future risk profile; charges reduce potential growth. Aviva Key Features: value of pension pot can go down as well as up; could get back less than invested; currency risk if sterling strengthens against underlying investment currency; property liquidity risk — may take time to sell, delay possible up to 12 months; fixed-term product access limitations; illustrations/projections not guaranteed; income drawdown risks including impact on means-tested benefits, need for growth to compensate for income withdrawn, tax risk, potential to deplete fund. Zurich Policy Info: generic investment risks including share fund volatility, fixed-interest suitability, derivatives risk, OEIC cross-contamination risk, inflation risk on fixed-interest income, concentration/emerging market/smaller companies/property/high-yield bond risks; pension transfer checklist noting features/benefits that may be lost on transfer. CT Universal MAP KIID (P9, P11): fund rated 4; derivative risk, fixed interest securities risk, credit risk, smaller companies risk, emerging markets risk, liquidity risk, currency risk, leverage risk. SIPP research report (P15): agreed risk level 5 Balanced; fund allocation 50% CT Universal MAP Balanced, 50% HSBC Global Strategy Balanced.
    Discrepancies:
      - 1. The report does not mention counterparty risk, credit risk, default risk, or derivatives risk identified in the HSBC KIID and CT Universal MAP KIID for the recommended funds, despite these being listed as material risks not fully captured by the Risk and Reward Indicator.
      - 2. The report does not mention currency risk, which is identified in the HSBC fund factsheet and CT Universal MAP KIID as a material risk for the recommended funds (both invest globally with currency exposure).
      - 3. The report does not mention leverage risk identified in the CT Universal MAP KIID as a specific risk of that fund.
      - 4. The report does not mention smaller companies risk or emerging markets risk identified in the CT Universal MAP KIID.
      - 5. The report does not mention liquidity risk identified in the CT Universal MAP KIID and HSBC fund factsheet.
      - 6. The Aviva Key Features document identifies income drawdown-specific risks (impact on means-tested benefits, risk of depleting fund, tax variation risks) — the report does not address these, though it is unclear whether the recommendation involves drawdown.
      - 7. The Aviva Key Features document identifies currency risk for investments held in foreign currencies — not mentioned in the report.
      - 8. The HSBC KIID states the fund is not appropriate for investors planning to withdraw within 5 years — the report does not address this investment horizon suitability limitation for the recommended fund.
      - 9. The CT Universal MAP KIID (P9) states the fund is rated 4 (medium volatility), while the HSBC KIID rates its fund as 3; the report does not distinguish the different risk ratings of the two recommended funds or explain the composite risk profile.
      - 10. The Zurich Policy Info pension transfer checklist (P16) notes that many Zurich plans have features or benefits that may have been forgotten — the extraction flags /replacementAnalysis/0/featuresOrGuaranteesLost as NotPresentInDocument, and the report does not appear to identify specific features or guarantees being lost on transfer from Zurich.
      - 11. The report's PerformanceVariability risk (p.13) acknowledges recommended funds have not outperformed Zurich funds, but the report does not explain that this is partly because Zurich funds are in higher-risk funds and the client will be moving to lower-risk funds — the risk of reduced return from de-risking is not fully articulated.
      - 12. The report does not mention the risk that charges reduce potential growth of the investment (noted in HSBC KIID charges section).
    The report does state several important risks: capital-at-risk (unit prices can fall as well as rise), market timing risk during switching, no guarantee of outperformance, access delay, and a client-specific note that recommended funds have not outperformed the Zurich funds being replaced. These are meaningful disclosures. However, the product literature on file — specifically the HSBC KIID, the CT Universal MAP KIID, and the Aviva Key Features document — identifies a materially broader set of product-specific and fund-specific risks that the report does not address. Most significantly: (a) currency risk is a named material risk in both recommended fund KIIDs (both funds invest globally) and is entirely absent from the report; (b) counterparty, credit, default, and derivatives risks are listed as 'material risks not fully captured by the Risk and Reward Indicator' in the KIIDs and are omitted from the report; (c) leverage risk and emerging markets risk appear in the CT Universal MAP KIID and are unaddressed; (d) the HSBC fund is rated risk level 3 and the CT Universal MAP is rated level 4 — neither rating is discussed in the report in relation to the fund KIIDs, and the difference in risk levels between the two recommended funds is not explained; (e) the HSBC KIID explicitly states the fund may not be appropriate for investors planning to withdraw within 5 years — no such horizon limitation is mentioned in the report. The guard against delegating risk disclosure to product literature is relevant: the report does not purport to defer to the KIIDs, it simply omits these risks. The omission of currency risk in particular is a product-specific risk that applies directly to both recommended funds and is entirely absent from the report. The omission of the minimum 5-year horizon recommendation from the KIID is also a meaningful gap for a pension consolidation client. The extraction confirms /replacementAnalysis/0/featuresOrGuaranteesLost is NotPresentInDocument, consistent with the Zurich transfer checklist in P16 noting benefits that may be overlooked — this is assessed under CHK-009 but adds context here. On vulnerability: no specific vulnerability characteristics are identified in the evidence that would materially change this assessment beyond the general expectation of clear explanation. The severity is Moderate: product-specific risks (currency, counterparty/credit/derivatives, leverage, fund horizon suitability) are absent from the report despite appearing in the product literature on file; the capital-at-risk warning and switching risks are present, so this is not a complete absence of risk disclosure, but the omissions are material and product-specific rather than merely generic.

  [G8.3] Tax consequences of the recommendation
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report states five tax-related items: (1) a generic caveat that tax treatment is based on current law and HMRC practice and may change (page 16); (2) the client understands pension freedoms and prefers Flexi Access Drawdown for flexibility over how and when benefits are taken (page 9); (3) death benefits before age 75 are paid free of tax to nominated beneficiaries; after age 75 the same options are available but taxed at the beneficiary's marginal rate (page 9); (4) personal pension contributions attract tax relief, with a net contribution of £80 becoming £100 gross (page 9); (5) under the small pot rules, the £211.32 Scottish Widows encashment (described as £200) will have 25% taken as tax-free cash and 75% taxed as income at the client's marginal rate, with an estimated net receipt of £170 (page 6). No explicit mention is made of: the Lump Sum Allowance (£268,275) or Lump Sum and Death Benefit Allowance (£1,073,100) affecting the client's future crystallisation; the Money Purchase Annual Allowance (MPAA) being triggered on entering Flexi Access Drawdown; the income tax treatment of drawdown income withdrawals at the client's marginal rate; or the IHT note about pension funds historically not forming part of the estate and the forthcoming 2027 IHT changes referenced in the body of the report.
    File:     The Key Features document (H) explains that up to 25% of the pension fund can be taken as a tax-free lump sum, income and lump sum payments are taxed as income at the client's personal rate, and HMRC's lump sum allowance and lump sum and death benefit allowance cap tax-free benefits. It notes that if the value of tax-free benefits taken during lifetime and on death exceeds the lump sum and death benefit allowance, the beneficiary may pay income tax on the excess. It states that benefits paid more than two years after death may also attract income tax. It also notes that death before 75 benefits are normally paid tax-free, with caveats around the lump sum and death benefit allowance. The suitability report itself (I) references the forthcoming IHT changes in 2027 that will bring pensions into estates, and notes the client's current IHT liability is £0. The pension switch research report (G) identifies that the new solution day-one death benefit is £106,880.60 versus the existing £110,185.60, a shortfall of £3,305.00 immediately after the switch (the initial advice charge). The Flexi-access drawdown section in the suitability report confirms the 25% tax-free lump sum (subject to the £268,275 Lump Sum Allowance) and that each subsequent withdrawal is taxed at marginal rate of Income Tax, but this appears in a generic product description section rather than being personalised to the client's circumstances. No passage in the file addresses the MPAA being triggered on crystallisation into FAD.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states death benefits before age 75 are paid 'free of tax' without qualification, but the Key Features document clarifies that if the lump sum and death benefit allowance is exceeded, the beneficiary may still pay income tax on the excess — the report's death-benefit tax statement is materially incomplete relative to the product literature.
      - The report's FAD description (page 9) notes the client 'understands pension freedoms and prefers FAD' but does not state that crystallising into FAD triggers the MPAA (currently £10,000), which is a material tax consequence for a client who is still employed as an HGV driver and making pension contributions — this consequence is absent from both the report and retrieved passages.
      - The report states death benefits after age 75 are taxed at 'the beneficiary's marginal rate of tax' but does not mention the 45% charge applicable where payment is made to a trust or personal representatives, which is stated in the Key Features document.
      - The report's small-pot tax explanation describes a '£200 pension fund withdrawal' whereas the actual encashment value is £211.32 — a minor but technically inaccurate personalisation.
      - The suitability report body (page 5 area) references that pensions will form part of estates for IHT from 2027, which is a material prospective tax consequence of retaining pension assets, but no explanation is given of what this means for the client's position or what mitigating steps, if any, were considered.
      - The Key Features document explains HMRC's lump sum allowance (£268,275) and lump sum and death benefit allowance (£1,073,100) and their effect on tax-free benefit limits, but the report does not personalise these allowances to the client's situation or confirm whether any lifetime allowance protection applies, even though the client is approaching crystallisation.
      - The report states tax relief at 20% basic rate (£80 net becomes £100 gross) without confirming the client's tax band; the client earns approximately £300/week net plus state pension of £230/week, making their marginal rate relevant to this illustration.
    The report addresses several tax topics: a generic caveat, death benefits taxation (pre- and post-75), tax relief on contributions, and the small-pot encashment taxation with a personalised net receipt estimate. These are positive features. However, several material tax consequences identified in the product literature and the file are absent or inadequately treated. Most significantly: (1) The death benefit tax-free statement on page 9 omits the lump sum and death benefit allowance caveat that appears in the Key Features document — this is a material omission relative to the product literature. (2) The MPAA trigger on crystallisation into FAD is entirely absent from the report. For a client who is still employed and potentially contributing, this is a material tax/regulatory consequence of the recommended action. No passage in the file addresses it. (3) The forthcoming IHT changes to pension assets (2027) are mentioned in the report body but no consequence or client-specific analysis is provided. (4) The small-pot figure is described as '£200' when the actual value is £211.32 — a trivial difference but technically inaccurate. The guard notes that a tax consequence stated without the client's tax band established is unverifiable rather than necessarily wrong; the basic-rate tax relief illustration is therefore not itself a finding. The death-benefit omission and MPAA absence are the most material issues. The MPAA is particularly significant given the client's employment status. Severity is assessed as Moderate: risks are stated but generically in places, and a product-specific material consequence (MPAA trigger) is entirely absent, while the death-benefit tax statement is incomplete relative to the Key Features document.

  [G8.5] Disadvantages and risks are fairly and prominently presented
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts it has a dedicated section headed 'Other Considerations of Switching' on page 8 that is not confined to an appendix, covering disadvantages and risks of the pension switch recommendation. The report also contains a Critical Yield appendix, a charges comparison table, performance analysis, capacity for loss discussion, and loss scenarios (effect of switching at low/mid/high growth rates showing decreases of £10,400–£15,000 or 9.6%–10.0%).
    File:     The case file (suitability report passages) confirms: (1) A section headed 'Other Considerations of Switching' exists on page 8 [P6], warning about market movements during transfer and that a switch does not guarantee better returns. (2) Loss scenarios at three growth rates are present in the body of the report [P1] showing e.g. mid-growth effect of switching = £12,000 or 9.4% decrease, and high-growth = £15,000 or 10.0% decrease. (3) Critical yield figures appear in both the body [P2] and appendix [P8]. (4) Charges are disclosed in a table [P4] with 0.44% total. (5) A pension switch charges comparison table [P4] shows the impact of charges on each plan. (6) The report states the client needs to outperform by £4,672 in year one [P2]. (7) Capacity for loss is addressed [P7]. However: (a) The 'Other Considerations of Switching' section [P6] for the Standard Life switch states 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' — but the same section for another switch [P6] also says '0.08% more expensive' — the framing of cost savings as benefits is prominent while the cost disadvantage figure is inconsistently stated. (b) The body prominently leads with benefits (lower costs, lifestyling, greater investment choice, enhanced flexibility) before risks are stated [P6]. (c) The appendix contains the Critical Yield table [P8], while the body cross-references it [P2]. (d) The Pension Switch Report (supporting document) [P11] corroborates the loss scenario figures. (e) No explicit mention of features or guarantees lost (extraction confirms /replacementAnalysis/0/featuresOrGuaranteesLost is absent from the document). (f) The immediate impact on death benefit is disclosed in the supporting pension switch report [P11/P15] (shortfall of £3,305 immediately after switch) but it is unclear this figure appears prominently in the body of the suitability report itself — passage [P8] mentions the appendix critical yield but does not quote the death benefit shortfall in the main body.
    Discrepancies:
      - The suitability report body leads prominently with a list of benefits (lower costs, lifestyling, greater investment choice, enhanced flexibility, adviser charging facilitation, ongoing monitoring) before stating risks, which is a potential prominence imbalance.
      - The immediate death benefit shortfall of £3,305 following the switch is evidenced in the pension switch report [P15] but does not appear to be stated in the main body of the suitability report — it is relegated to supporting documentation.
      - The extraction confirms /replacementAnalysis/0/featuresOrGuaranteesLost is absent from the document, meaning any features or guarantees lost on transfer are not disclosed.
      - Critical yield figures appear in both the body (cross-referenced) and the appendix, but the detailed Critical Yield table is in the appendix [P8], while the benefit claims are in the body.
      - The 'Other Considerations of Switching' section [P6] states the plan is '0.06% more expensive' (or '0.08%' depending on which switch) than the recommended solution — these figures are inconsistent across the document and the framing is ambiguous about which direction the cost difference runs.
      - The Zurich switch involves a very high critical yield of 4.8% at mid-growth [P1/P11], meaning the recommended solution must grow substantially faster to match the existing plan — this is disclosed in the appendix and cross-referenced in the body, but the prominence of this material disadvantage relative to the benefit claims is limited.
    The report has a dedicated 'Other Considerations of Switching' section on page 8, satisfying the requirement for a named section outside an appendix. Loss scenarios at three growth rates are present in the body of the report and corroborated by the pension switch report. Critical yield is cross-referenced in the body and detailed in the appendix. Capacity for loss and attitude to risk are addressed. However, two material concerns remain. First, features or guarantees lost on transfer are entirely absent from the report (confirmed by extraction metadata), which is a material omission under the 'what the client is giving up' limb of this check. Second, the immediate death benefit shortfall of £3,305 is evidenced only in the supporting pension switch report [P15] and does not appear in the main body of the suitability report — this is a material disadvantage that a client reading only the suitability report would not see. Third, the prominence balance is weighted toward benefits: the body lists six benefit bullet points before the risks section, and the most adverse quantified outcome (critical yield of 4.8% at mid-growth for the Zurich switch, requiring significant outperformance) is presented in the appendix with only a cross-reference in the body. The inconsistency in the cost-difference figure (0.06% vs 0.08%) also undermines clarity. The vulnerability overlay: the client (John) is noted as having limited investment experience [P7], which heightens the importance of clear, prominent risk disclosure. The omission of features/guarantees lost and the relegation of the death benefit shortfall to supporting documentation are more significant given this limited experience. On balance, the absence of features/guarantees lost and the death benefit shortfall from the main report body are material omissions, warranting a Potential Concern at Moderate severity (material disadvantage present in supporting file but absent from report body, and features lost entirely absent).

  [G8.6] Benefits, features or protections the client is giving up
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The suitability report asserts one feature being lost: the employer contribution from the People's Pension, stating 'If you were to transfer away, you would miss out on the valuable benefit of your employer's contributions.' The report also states that none of the five existing arrangements contain safeguarded benefits. The report does not mention any other features or protections being given up.
    File:     The policy research and provider documents reveal several features across the existing arrangements. The Standard Life policy (SL At Retirement Universal PP 10 Year) carries a Differential Fund Discount / Leaver Discount of 0.4800% on the TER, which Standard Life explicitly warns 'may not be available from other pension providers upon transfer.' The Standard Life document also confirms no GAR, no GMP, no guaranteed fund, no safeguarded benefits, and that partial transfers are not permitted for investment purposes. The Scottish Widows policy shows no GMP, no guaranteed minimum fund, no waiver of premium, no life cover, no fund rebates, no guaranteed annuity rates. The Aviva policy shows no exit charge, no indexation, and the plan does not allow adviser charging. The Zurich transfer checklist references life cover and waiver benefits as features that may be attached and warns they are no longer available on new plans. The policy research table confirms no life cover on any of the five plans. The People's Pension retains the employer contribution which the report correctly identifies. The retirement options available differ across plans: the SL plan offers only FlexiAccessDrawdown (not UFPLS), Scottish Widows offers only UFPLS/PartialUFPLS (no FAD unless fund value reaches £10k), and the Aviva plan offers FAD, UFPLS and PartialUFPLS — these differing access options could be lost or changed upon consolidation but are not discussed in the report. The Standard Life leaver discount of 0.4800% is a material feature being permanently surrendered on transfer.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report does not mention the Standard Life leaver/differential fund discount of 0.4800% which Standard Life's own policy documentation explicitly warns may not be available from other pension providers upon transfer — this is a material feature being given up that is entirely absent from the report.
      - The report does not address the loss of retirement access options: the Scottish Widows plan restricts FAD unless fund value reaches £10k, and the SL plan offers only FlexiAccessDrawdown; consolidating these into Aviva may alter the options available to the client but this is not discussed.
      - The report's featuresOrGuaranteesLost list contains only the employer contribution item; no other features or protections are mentioned despite the evidence file containing at least one material item (SL leaver discount) that the report omits.
    The report correctly identifies the loss of the employer contribution on the People's Pension as a feature being given up, and the confirmation that no safeguarded benefits exist across all five plans is consistent with the provider documents. However, the Standard Life policy documentation (P10) explicitly flags that the Leaver Discount of 0.4800% 'may not be available from other pension providers upon transfer' — this is a product-specific, policy-specific feature (not generic boilerplate) that represents a real financial benefit being surrendered. Its complete absence from the report is a material omission. The false-positive guard instructs that a guarantee recorded in provider literature but never mentioned in the report is the finding, and that a feature described in generic literature that the policy does not actually carry is not being given up — here, the leaver discount is confirmed as specific to this member's Standard Life plan, so it is genuinely being given up. The differing retirement access options (no FAD on Scottish Widows unless fund reaches £10k; SL offering only FAD) are a lesser concern as consolidation into Aviva may actually improve access, but the report does not address this at all. The Standard Life leaver discount omission is the primary concern and is assessed as High severity given it is a material, policy-specific financial benefit identified in provider documentation and wholly absent from the suitability report. No vulnerability flags in the file are identified as bearing on this specific sub-requirement beyond what CHK-010 would address.

  [G8.7] Justification for increased costs or reduced projected outcomes
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts the recommended solution is cheaper than the existing arrangements (costsAndCharges/comparison/direction = RecommendedCheaper). It justifies the recommendation on the basis that: (1) past performance of recommended funds makes a 4.8% critical yield achievable; (2) recommended Aviva advised funds have demonstrated stronger performance than existing Aviva holdings; (3) the Aviva platform funds align with agreed risk tolerance and have delivered comparable performance with reduced volatility; and (4) the ongoing review service includes ATR assessment, investment suitability review, market updates, legislation changes, ad-hoc advice access and holistic financial planning. A disadvantage of 'no guarantee of outperformance' is stated on page 8. Projections at low/mid/high growth rates (£97,600 / £115,000 / £135,000) are provided at page 18.
    File:     The pension switch report (P1, P15) shows that at mid growth rate the new solution projects £125,000 versus the existing pension £127,000 — a £2,000 (1.6%) decrease. At high growth the new solution projects £147,000 versus £150,000 — a £3,000 (2.0%) decrease. The new solution ranks 5th out of 10 in the product comparison, behind the existing solution (ranked 2nd) and Zurich (ranked 1st) (P11). The pension switch report (P4) shows existing pension projected maturity values (before adviser charges) at mid growth of £120,000 and high of £141,000, with the new solution's own projections at mid £125,000 and high £135,000 when the adviser charge effect is incorporated. The SIPP research report (P12, P13) confirms the recommended funds' past performance and risk level 5 alignment. The switch report (P17) discloses a day-one death benefit shortfall of £3,305 due to the initial adviser charge. The suitability report (P19) shows the new solution total annual charge is 0.44% versus Zurich existing at 0.93%, supporting a cost reduction narrative for the Zurich switch. However, P1 and P15 together show the new solution's projected maturity values are lower than the existing solution at every growth rate, meaning the client faces reduced projected outcomes. The report's projections at page 18 (£97,600 / £115,000 / £135,000) differ from both the switch report's existing pension projections and new solution projections in P1/P4/P15. The critical yield required for the Zurich switch is stated as 3.2% at mid growth in P1, but the report refers to a 4.8% critical yield without clearly connecting this to a specific plan.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts RecommendedCheaper, but switch report (P1, P15) shows new solution projected maturity values are lower than existing at every growth scenario (mid: £125,000 new vs £127,000 existing; high: £147,000 new vs £150,000 existing), meaning the client faces reduced projected outcomes alongside the cost reduction for some plans.
      - Report projects £97,600 (low), £115,000 (mid), £135,000 (high) at page 18, but the switch report (P4) shows existing pension mid projection of £120,000 and high of £141,000, and new solution mid of £125,000 — these figures do not align with the page 18 projections without further explanation of what population of assets they cover.
      - Report cites a critical yield of 4.8% per year to age 75 as achievable, but the switch report (P1) shows the critical yield for the main Zurich switch at mid growth is 3.2% and at high growth is 6.2% — the 4.8% figure is not directly reconciled to any single plan's critical yield in the supporting evidence.
      - The report's justification relies on past performance of recommended funds being strong enough to overcome the critical yield, but the switch report (P11) ranks the new solution 5th out of 10, behind the existing solution (2nd) and Zurich (1st) at mid growth, which is a material limitation not prominently addressed in the report.
      - Report states 'no guarantee of outperformance' as the sole named disadvantage on page 8, but does not explicitly address the quantified projected shortfall (e.g. £2,000 less at mid growth) in the disadvantages section, only in the charges table.
      - Day-one death benefit shortfall of £3,305 is evidenced in the switch report (P17) but it is not clear from the canonical model that this is disclosed as a disadvantage or risk in the suitability report body.
    The report correctly states the recommendation is cheaper in annual charge terms (0.44% new vs 0.93% Zurich existing per P19), and this is corroborated by the switch report. The cost reduction is therefore genuine and documented. However, the switch report evidence (P1, P15) clearly shows the new solution produces lower projected maturity values than the existing solution at every growth rate (mid: £2,000 less; high: £3,000 less). This is a classic 'cheaper but lower projected outcome' scenario. The report's justification rests principally on past performance claims and a belief that the critical yield is achievable, which the SIPP research report (P12) partially supports in terms of recent performance figures. However, the product comparison ranking (P11) places the new solution 5th, behind the existing solution, which materially undermines the outperformance narrative and is not addressed in the report. The 4.8% critical yield claim is not clearly reconciled to the switch report's figure of 3.2% (mid) or 6.2% (high) for the Zurich switch; it appears to be a different or aggregate figure, creating ambiguity that is not explained to the client. The disadvantages section on page 8 states only a generic 'no guarantee of outperformance' without quantifying the projected shortfall. The day-one death benefit shortfall of £3,305 (P17) does not appear to be highlighted as a named disadvantage in the canonical model. While the report is not wholly silent on justification — it references cost savings, service benefits, fund performance, and risk alignment — the combination of: (a) reduced projected outcomes not quantified in the disadvantages section; (b) a product ranking showing the new solution is not the best available option; and (c) the critical yield figure not being reconciled to the switch report evidence, means the justification for the reduced projected outcomes is incomplete. The vulnerability overlay does not appear to add a specific additional dimension here beyond what is already assessed, as no vulnerability-specific communication adjustments are identified in the evidence as relevant to cost disclosure. Severity is Moderate: the report provides substantive justification but omits the quantified projected shortfall from the disadvantages narrative and does not address the product ranking or reconcile the critical yield figure, leaving the client without a complete picture of why the reduced outcome is acceptable.

  [G8.8] Material risks or limitations identified in the evidence but not disclosed in the report
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report discloses: (1) a critical yield requirement of 4.8% per year at mid-growth of 2.94% for the recommended plan to match the current plan value at age 75; (2) the Aviva plan is 4.24% more expensive than current plans, requiring £4,672 outperformance in year one; (3) lifestyling risk — existing funds in lifestyling will eventually move outside the agreed risk range; (4) withdrawal restrictions — consolidating all pensions would limit flexible access; (5) Scottish Widows fund range constraint requiring two funds with 20-30% in money market/cash; (6) all other risks delegated to product literature; (7) access delay of 10-15 working days; (8) market timing risk during the switching period.
    File:     The pension switch report (Defaqto) shows: at mid-growth the consolidated switch produces a projected maturity value of £125,000 vs existing £127,000 (£2,000/1.6% decrease) before advice charges, and with advice charges the Zurich-only comparison shows a critical yield of 4.8% at mid-growth. The immediate impact table shows the new solution value after the initial adviser charge of £3,305 is £106,880.60 against an existing pension value of £110,185.60. The Standard Life policy info discloses a Differential Fund Discount/Leaver Discount of 0.48% which may not be available from other pension providers upon transfer. The Standard Life plan does not permit partial transfers. The Zurich policy info and Aviva key features document reference guaranteed annuity rates as a consideration. The Standard Life policy info explicitly states 'These low charges may not be available from other pension providers upon transfer.' No guaranteed annuity rates, GMP or safeguarded benefits are confirmed for Standard Life. The Aviva policy info confirms no guaranteed annuity rates. The key features document discloses income drawdown risks, currency risk, liquidity risk for property, and tax risks on drawdown income. The pension switch report confirms no benefits were listed for the existing products (i.e. no guaranteed benefits to lose). The Defaqto research does not show the existing arrangements ranking above the new solution on a like-for-like basis after advice charges are included.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states a critical yield of 4.8% at mid-growth (2.94%). The Defaqto pension switch report shows the consolidated Zurich-only comparison at mid-growth (2.95%) produces a critical yield of 4.8%, consistent with the report. However, the overall consolidated comparison at mid-growth shows a critical yield of 3.2% (P3), and the report does not clearly distinguish which comparison the 4.8% figure derives from.
      - The Standard Life policy info (P15) explicitly warns that the Differential Fund Discount/Leaver Discount of 0.48% 'may not be available from other pension providers upon transfer' and that these low charges may not be available elsewhere. The suitability report does not mention the loss of this specific leaver discount or low-charge benefit on the Standard Life plan.
      - The report states the recommended plan is '4.24% more expensive than your current plans'. The Defaqto switch report (P10) shows the Zurich-only mid-growth comparison has a reduction in yield of 2.2% for the new solution vs 0.3% for existing — a difference of 1.9 percentage points — and a critical yield of 4.8%. The 4.24% figure appears to relate solely to the Zurich plan comparison including advice charges, but this is not clearly explained in the canonical model.
      - The immediate fund value impact following the switch is shown in the Defaqto report (P10) as existing £110,185.60 vs new solution post-charge, implying an immediate reduction due to the initial adviser charge of £3,305. The report discloses the charge impact in monetary terms (£4,672 outperformance required) but the immediate capital reduction of £3,305 at outset is not separately highlighted as an immediate loss of capital.
      - No tax consequences of the pension switch (e.g. tax treatment on transfer, potential tax on any income taken, MPAA implications) are addressed in the disclosed risk warnings in the canonical model.
    The report makes a reasonable disclosure of the principal material risks: critical yield, adviser charge impact, lifestyling drift, withdrawal restrictions, fund range constraints, market timing risk, and access delays. These are client-specific and prominent. The Defaqto pension switch report corroborates the critical yield and projected value decrease figures, and confirms no guaranteed benefits are being lost on the existing plans. However, two material gaps stand out. First, the Standard Life policy information (P15) specifically flags that the Leaver Discount of 0.48% 'may not be available from other pension providers upon transfer' and that 'These low charges may not be available from other pension providers upon transfer.' This is a specific financial detriment — a benefit/feature the client is giving up — that is entirely absent from the report's risk warnings. Under the check's decision logic, a material benefit or protection the client is giving up that is identified in the supporting evidence but omitted from the report is a finding at the highest severity tier. Second, the report does not address any tax consequences of the recommendation. Although the client is not in drawdown and no immediate tax event is described, the key features document (P11) discloses income drawdown tax risks and the nature of pension taxation. The absence of any tax consequence discussion (e.g. the tax-free cash position, any MPAA risk, or tax on future withdrawals) is a moderate gap. The generic delegation to product literature partially addresses this but product literature delegation alone is insufficient under the decision logic. The immediate capital reduction of £3,305 at outset is implied in the charge discussion but not separately flagged as an immediate loss of capital to the client. The vulnerability overlay: no specific vulnerability drivers are identified in the canonical model that would heighten the risk disclosure requirement beyond the standard, so no additional overlay concern is raised here beyond the substantive gaps already identified. The finding on the Standard Life leaver discount omission is the primary concern — it is a specific, quantified benefit the client is giving up (0.48% leaver discount) that the policy documentation itself flags as important and that the suitability report entirely ignores.

CHK-009 — Replacement or Switch Justification
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 10 requirement(s). 10 raised a concern (G9.1, G9.2, G9.3, G9.4, G9.5, G9.6, G9.7, G9.8, G9.9, G9.10). 1. The report states People's Pension has 7 funds available [comparison dimension FundRange]. The policy research [P23] shows '7' for People's Pension fund count, consistent with the report. No discrepancy here — removing this after noting it is consistent. 2. The report states Standard Life has retirement option 'FAD' only [P23]; the report's comparison for Standard Life lists existing as 'FAD only, no UFPLS' and recommended as 'FAD/Full and partial UFPLS', assessed as Better. The policy research [P1] shows Standard Life FAD: Yes, UFPLS: Yes — this appears to contradict the report's claim that Standard Life does not offer UFPLS. The suitability report table [P23] shows Standard Life retirement option as 'FAD' only, but [P1] shows 'Full and partial UFPLS available: Yes' for Standard Life. This is a discrepancy between the report and the policy research evidence. 3. The report asserts for Scottish Widows: retirement options 'Full and partial UFPLS' (existing); policy research [P1] shows Scottish Widows FAD: No, UFPLS: Yes — broadly consistent. No material discrepancy. 21 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G9.1] Comparison of existing versus recommended product and investments
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report compares five existing pension arrangements against the recommended Aviva Platform Personal Pension across multiple dimensions: (1) People's Pension vs recommended — Charges: 0.50% fund + £4.50 annual admin vs 0.44% (0.21% platform + 0.22% IM), Better; FundRange: 7 funds vs 80+, Better; RiskAlignment: Risk level 9 (up to 85% shares) vs Risk level 5 Balanced, Better; AdviserServicing: not allowed vs allowed, Better; RetirementOptions: FAD/Full and partial UFPLS vs FAD/Full and partial UFPLS, Equivalent. (2) Aviva (SP50163762) vs recommended — Charges: 0.50% fund charge vs 0.44%, Better (-0.06%, -£2.25); FundRange: 80+ (lifestyling fund) vs 80+ Aviva platform, Better; RiskAlignment: Risk level 2 lifestyling fund (moves outside agreed risk range) vs Risk level 5 Balanced, Better; AdviserServicing: not allowed vs allowed, Better; RetirementOptions: FAD/Full and partial UFPLS vs FAD/Full and partial UFPLS/FAD, Equivalent. (3) Zurich vs recommended — Charges: 0.18% fund + £2.12/month admin (0.93% total) vs 0.44%, Better (-0.49%, -£506.85); FundRange: 60+ funds vs 80+, Better; RiskAlignment: Risk level 7 Managed AP, no risk-5 funds, blend of risk 7 and risk 2 required vs Risk level 5 Balanced, Better. Additional dimensions for Scottish Widows and Standard Life are also stated. The report asserts no safeguarded benefits exist for any of the five plans. A Pension Switching Report (Defaqto) is referenced for cost/benefit and maturity value projections. The report notes the initial adviser charge is £3,305 taken from the new solution and an ongoing 1% adviser charge. The report states the switch results in a required outperformance of 4.24% (£4,672) relative to the Zurich plan alone, while separately claiming the overall switch is justified by lower charges, better risk alignment, wider fund range and adviser servicing capability.
    File:     The case file contains: (1) Policy Research document [P1][P8][P13] covering all five existing plans with fund range, adviser charging, exit penalties, retirement options and other features. (2) Suitability Report pages [P16][P23] reproducing the existing arrangements comparison table showing fund values, charges, fund counts, adviser servicing, retirement options, safeguarded benefits and other considerations. (3) Pension Switch Report [P3][P4][P5][P9][P10][P11][P12][P15][P17][P20] containing maturity value projections at low/mid/high growth rates for each plan switched, the effect of switching, critical yields, reduction in yield, alternatives comparison (including Aviva-only, Standard Life-only, Zurich-only scenarios), stakeholder pension comparison and a ranking of top 10 solutions. (4) Aviva Policy Info [P18] confirming fund value £3,744.36, no guaranteed rates, no GMP, Lifestage Investment Approach (auto-switching), plan status paid-up. (5) Performance charts [P6][P14] comparing Aviva existing vs recommended vs Zurich vs benchmark. The switch report shows the new solution ranks 5th by projected maturity value (£125,000 at mid growth) behind the existing solution (£127,000) and even the Zurich-only retained option (£128,000). At all growth rates the new solution produces lower projected values than retaining all existing pensions. The effect of switching on the overall portfolio at mid growth is -£2,000 or -1.6% decrease. Alternatives (Aviva-only, SL-only, Zurich-only) are all documented. Stakeholder pension is compared. Benefits lost on switch: 'No benefits were listed for the existing products' per [P17]. Adviser charge impact: initial £3,305 taken from new solution (day-one shortfall in death benefit of £3,305) per [P12][P17]. Ongoing 1% adviser charge included in projections. Policy research [P8] shows exit penalties: People's Pension — No; Scottish Widows — Yes; Aviva — No (called provider); Standard Life — No; Zurich — No. Scottish Widows has an exit penalty noted but the report appears to recommend encashing/retaining rather than switching Scottish Widows. The switch report ranks new solution below existing solution at every growth rate. The report acknowledges a required outperformance of 4.24% but the switch report projections show the new solution underperforming at all growth scenarios. The Pension Switching Report fund values used (existing pension £110,185.60 per [P12]) differ from the sum of all five plans (£116,998.47) and from the four plans being switched (£116,998.47 minus People's Pension £6,601.55 = £110,396.92), creating an unexplained £211.32 discrepancy (likely Scottish Widows excluded) but a residual £0.32 rounding difference. People's Pension is a live workplace scheme with employer contributions (£897.13 PA) per [P16]; the report recommends retaining it. Standard Life shows 300 funds available per [P23] but the report states this as a positive for the recommended arrangement (80+ funds) without noting Standard Life's 300-fund range is superior.
    Discrepancies:
      - 1. The report states People's Pension has 7 funds available [comparison dimension FundRange]. The policy research [P23] shows '7' for People's Pension fund count, consistent with the report. No discrepancy here — removing this after noting it is consistent.
      - 2. The report states Standard Life has retirement option 'FAD' only [P23]; the report's comparison for Standard Life lists existing as 'FAD only, no UFPLS' and recommended as 'FAD/Full and partial UFPLS', assessed as Better. The policy research [P1] shows Standard Life FAD: Yes, UFPLS: Yes — this appears to contradict the report's claim that Standard Life does not offer UFPLS. The suitability report table [P23] shows Standard Life retirement option as 'FAD' only, but [P1] shows 'Full and partial UFPLS available: Yes' for Standard Life. This is a discrepancy between the report and the policy research evidence.
      - 3. The report asserts for Scottish Widows: retirement options 'Full and partial UFPLS' (existing); policy research [P1] shows Scottish Widows FAD: No, UFPLS: Yes — broadly consistent. No material discrepancy.
      - 4. The report claims for Zurich: 'Does not allow adviser servicing'; policy research [P8] shows 'Can adviser charging be applied: No' for Zurich — consistent.
      - 5. The report claims for Standard Life: 'Allows Servicing Adviser: Yes'; policy research [P8] shows 'Can adviser charging be applied: Yes' for Standard Life (column 3, identified as Aviva existing) — the column alignment in [P8] is ambiguous, but [P23] explicitly shows Standard Life 'Allows Servicing Adviser: Yes', consistent with the report.
      - 6. The Pension Switching Report [P9] ranks the new solution 5th (£125,000 mid-growth) behind the existing solution (£127,000) and multiple alternatives. The report asserts the switch is justified on charges, risk alignment and adviser servicing, but does not clearly disclose that the projected maturity value of the new solution is lower than retaining all plans, at every growth rate.
      - 7. The report states the Zurich comparison shows a charge improvement of -0.49% (-£506.85). The switch report [P17] shows at mid growth the effect of switching the overall portfolio is -£2,000 or -1.6% decrease even after the charge saving. The report does not resolve this apparent contradiction — lower charges yet lower projected maturity value.
      - 8. The report asserts the required outperformance to justify the Zurich switch is 4.24% (£4,672). The switch report [P17][P9] shows the new solution requires a critical yield of 3.2% at mid growth just to match the existing solution (all plans combined), not 4.24%. These figures appear to relate to different bases (Zurich-only vs all plans), creating potential confusion about the true hurdle rate.
      - 9. The report states for the Aviva existing plan (SP50163762): 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' (per the internal inconsistency noted in the canonical model extraction). The charges comparison dimension states 0.50% existing vs 0.44% recommended = -0.06% saving (Better). The switch report [P20] for Aviva-only shows both existing and new solution at 0.6% reduction in yield, suggesting comparable charges rather than a 0.06% improvement — the Defaqto switch report does not corroborate a meaningful charge saving on the Aviva plan in isolation.
      - 10. The report does not address the Scottish Widows exit penalty. Policy research [P8] shows 'Any exit penalties: Yes' for Scottish Widows. Although Scottish Widows is recommended for encashment/retention rather than a full switch to Aviva Platform, the exit penalty is not disclosed in the comparison table or replacement analysis.
      - 11. Standard Life is reported as having 300 funds available [P23] — more than the recommended Aviva Platform's 80+ funds. The report does not note this as a dimension on which the existing Standard Life arrangement is superior to the recommended solution; the fund range comparison for Standard Life is presented as Better for the recommended arrangement.
      - 12. The transfer value used in the Pension Switching Report (£110,185.60 per [P12]) does not match the sum of the four plans being switched (Zurich £103,439.24 + Aviva £3,744.36 + Scottish Widows £211.32 + Standard Life £3,002 = £110,396.92), a discrepancy of approximately £211.32 — likely reflecting exclusion of Scottish Widows from the switch, but not clearly reconciled in the report.
    The suitability report provides a multi-dimensional comparison of existing versus recommended arrangements for all five plans, supported by the policy research document, the Defaqto Pension Switching Report, Aviva policy documentation and performance data. The comparison framework is substantially evidenced. However, several material concerns arise: (1) Standard Life UFPLS availability — the report's comparison states Standard Life's existing retirement options are 'FAD only' and treats this as a disadvantage relative to the recommended solution. But the policy research [P1] shows UFPLS is available on Standard Life. This is a one-sided presentation of a comparison dimension that the evidence does not support. (2) Standard Life fund range — Standard Life offers 300 funds [P23], which exceeds the recommended Aviva Platform's 80+. The report presents the fund range dimension as Better for the recommended solution for Standard Life without acknowledging that Standard Life's range is superior. This is a one-sided comparison contrary to the methodology requirement. (3) The new solution produces lower projected maturity values than the existing solution at all growth rates [P9][P17]. The switch is justified on non-monetary grounds (risk alignment, charges, adviser servicing), but the switch report ranking and projections are not clearly disclosed or reconciled in the suitability report in a way that addresses the lower projected outcome. (4) The Scottish Widows exit penalty [P8] is not disclosed in the comparison or replacement analysis. (5) The transfer value discrepancy between the switch report (£110,185.60) and the sum of individual plan values is unexplained, though this is likely a presentation/rounding issue with Scottish Widows exclusion. (6) On the Aviva-only comparison, the Defaqto switch report shows both existing and new at 0.6% RiY [P20], not corroborating the 0.06% charge saving the report claims. The false-positive guards note that fund range and option data changes over time, which partially mitigates the Standard Life UFPLS and fund-count discrepancies, but the policy research was conducted contemporaneously with the advice and is the primary evidence relied upon — it directly contradicts the report's presentation of these dimensions. The vulnerability overlay does not appear to create additional concerns specific to the replacement analysis beyond those already identified. The combination of: (a) a one-sided comparison on Standard Life UFPLS availability contradicted by evidence; (b) Standard Life fund range presented as inferior when evidence shows it is superior; (c) the new solution ranking below the existing solution at all growth rates without clear disclosure; and (d) a missing exit penalty disclosure for Scottish Widows, collectively constitute material concerns that the replacement rationale is not fully supported and contains evidence-contradicted claims.

  [G9.2] Benefits and costs of switching, including where benefits outweigh higher costs
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts: (1) No exit penalties on any ceding plan. (2) For the Scottish Widows plan (£211.32), withdrawal under small pots rules: 25% tax-free, 75% taxed at marginal rate, estimated net receipt £170 from a £200 fund. (3) For the Aviva existing plan (£3,744.36), annual charge difference of -0.06% / -£2.25 in favour of new solution. (4) For the Zurich plan (£103,439.24), annual charge difference of -0.49% / -£506.85 in favour of new solution. (5) The report states 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' for certain plans. (6) Benefits claimed include: lower costs, removal of lifestyling risk, greater investment choice, adviser charging facilitation, ongoing monitoring, multi-asset fund selection, enhanced retirement flexibility. (7) Critical yield of 4.8% required at mid-growth, with £4,672 required outperformance in year one. (8) No initial advice fee disclosed in the switching cost analysis.
    File:     [P3] (Pension switch report) shows the Zurich pension switch comparison: at mid growth (2.95%), existing projected value £127,000 vs new solution £115,000; reduction in yield 0.3% existing vs 0.6% new; critical yield 0.3%; effect of switching -£12,000 or -9.4%. [P6] shows the combined three-plan switch (Aviva + Zurich + Standard Life): existing RIY 0.3%, new solution RIY 0.6%; critical yield 3.2% at mid-growth; effect of switching -£2,000 or -1.6% at mid. [P12] shows a scenario where new solution RIY is 2.1%-2.3% vs existing 0.3%, with a critical yield of 4.8% at mid-growth and effect of switching -£12,000 at mid. [P7] shows new solution investment details (CT Universal MAP 50%, HSBC Global Strategy 50%) and immediate fund value impact: existing £110,185.60, new solution day one £106,880.60, shortfall £3,305. [P9] (Zurich policy info) confirms no transfer value/early retirement charge and no fund switch charge. [P13] (Policy research) shows no MVR on any plan. [P2] shows an alternative comparison retaining Zurich and Standard Life with new solution projected £4,260 at 0.6% RIY. [P1] states critical yield 4.8% per year to age 75 at mid-growth (2.94%) and required outperformance of £4,672 in first year. [P11] (KIID) shows 0.18% ongoing charge and 0% entry/exit charges. No passage corroborates the -0.06% / -£2.25 charge differential for the Aviva plan, the -0.49% / -£506.85 charge differential for the Zurich plan, the £170 net receipt estimate, the 25%/75% tax split figure (passage shows these are report assertions only), or the initial advice fee (absent from all passages). The pension switch report (category G) shows the new solution has a HIGHER reduction in yield (0.6%) than the existing arrangements (0.3%) in all scenarios, meaning the new solution is more expensive in RIY terms, not cheaper.
    Discrepancies:
      - 1. The report claims 'Lower Costs' as a benefit and states the Aviva plan is '0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' (-0.06% / -£2.25 differential). Neither the -0.06% figure nor the -£2.25 amount is corroborated by any passage. The pension switch report (P6, P3) consistently shows the new solution has a HIGHER RIY (0.6%) than existing arrangements (0.3%), contradicting the lower-cost claim at both plan and aggregate level.
      - 2. The report claims the Zurich plan switch saves -0.49% / -£506.85 annually. Neither figure is corroborated by any passage. P3 shows the Zurich-only switch results in a higher RIY for the new solution (0.6% vs 0.3% existing) and a projected loss of £12,000 at mid-growth, directly contradicting a cost-saving claim.
      - 3. The report states the Scottish Widows small pot withdrawal will yield an estimated £170 net from a £200 fund (25% tax-free / 75% taxed). The £170, £200, 25% and 75% figures are not corroborated by any evidence passage.
      - 4. P7 shows an immediate capital shortfall of £3,305 on switch day (existing £110,185.60 vs new solution day one £106,880.60). The report does not appear to address this immediate reduction in fund value as a cost of switching.
      - 5. The report states critical yield is 4.8% at mid-growth, corroborated by P1 and P12. However, P12 context shows this is a scenario with a 2.1%-2.3% RIY for the new solution — substantially higher than the 0.6% RIY shown in the main three-plan comparison (P6), suggesting the 4.8% critical yield may relate to a scenario with additional advice charges included, but the basis is not clearly reconciled between the report and the switch report.
      - 6. The initial advice fee is absent from all switching cost analyses in both the report and the supporting evidence, meaning the full cost of switching (including initial advice charge) is not disclosed in the cost comparison.
      - 7. The pension switch report (P6) shows that switching all three plans results in a lower projected maturity value than retaining them at all growth rates (e.g., mid-growth: £125,000 new vs £127,000 existing), yet the report claims lower costs as a primary benefit without adequately addressing this outcome.
      - 8. The report's internal inconsistency (noted in extraction) that the Aviva existing charge is 0.50% but the new solution is 0.44%, yet the text states 'the plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' — this is contradicted by the charge arithmetic (0.50% - 0.44% = 0.06% saving on the new solution, not 0.06% more expensive), creating an ambiguous or erroneous statement in the report.
    The central cost-benefit claim — that the recommended Aviva Platform solution is cheaper than the ceding arrangements — is materially contradicted by the pension switch report (category G evidence, P3, P6). That report consistently shows the new solution carries a higher reduction in yield (0.6%) than the existing arrangements (0.3%), meaning the new solution is more expensive on a charges basis, not cheaper. The report's assertion of a -0.06% saving on the Aviva plan and a -0.49% saving on the Zurich plan are not corroborated by any passage and are inconsistent with the switch report projections. The switch report also quantifies the effect of switching as a monetary decrease in projected maturity value at every growth rate tested (e.g., -£2,000 at mid-growth for the three-plan switch; -£12,000 for Zurich alone). The report acknowledges a critical yield requirement of 4.8% and a £4,672 first-year outperformance need (corroborated by P1), which itself signals that the switch is not cost-advantageous without superior investment performance. The report justifies the switch on non-cost grounds (lifestyling removal, investment choice, adviser charging facilitation, monitoring), and P9 confirms no Zurich exit penalty, P13 confirms no MVR — those elements are supported. However, the claim that costs are lower is a specific benefit assertion that the switch report directly contradicts, and this is not a minor dimension but the headline benefit cited. The immediate capital reduction of £3,305 on switch day (P7) is an additional undisclosed cost. The initial advice fee is absent from the cost comparison throughout. The vulnerability overlay does not appear to materially alter this finding; no vulnerability-specific concern arises on the face of the evidence in relation to the replacement analysis. The contradiction between the report's 'lower costs' claim and the switch report's consistent demonstration of higher RIY for the new solution, combined with unsubstantiated charge differential figures and an undisclosed initial advice fee, constitutes a high-severity concern. The Zurich plan at £103,439 is by far the largest ceding arrangement and the switch report shows it suffers a projected loss of £12,000 at mid-growth — the plan-specific cost/benefit analysis in the suitability report does not reconcile with this evidence.

  [G9.3] Details of lost features and guarantees
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report states that all five existing arrangements have no safeguarded benefits (stated verbatim: 'This pension arrangement does not contain any safeguarded benefits'). For the People's Pension plan, the report identifies that employer contributions would be lost on transfer ('If you were to transfer away, you would miss out on the valuable benefit of your employer's contributions'), and accordingly recommends retaining that plan. The report's replacement analysis records employerContributionsLost as true for one plan (People's Pension) and false for the remaining four. The path /replacementAnalysis/0/featuresOrGuaranteesLost is marked as NotPresentInDocument by the extraction, meaning the report does not enumerate any features or guarantees lost for the plans that are actually being switched/encashed.
    File:     Provider documentation confirms the following for each plan: Scottish Widows (ZU4225575, being encashed): plan schedule confirms no Guaranteed Annuity Rates, no Guaranteed Minimum Pension, no guarantees, discounts or bonuses, plan is paid up with no current contributions (Employer N/A, Employee N/A). Aviva (SP50163762, being switched): policy info confirms no guaranteed rates including annuity rates, no Guaranteed Minimum Pension, loyalty units not applicable, no protected tax-free lump sum. Zurich (being switched): provider letter confirms 'Guarantees, bonuses, guaranteed minimum pension, safeguarding, life styling, block/bulk transfers, market value reduction, protected or enhanced tax free cash do not apply to this plan.' Standard Life (being retained per some references but included in switch analysis): policy info confirms no Guaranteed Annuity Rates, no Guaranteed Minimum Pension, no Guaranteed Fund at retirement, no Reference Scheme Test, 'no Safeguarded benefits attached to this member's plan', though it notes a Differential Fund Discount (Leaver Discount 0.4800%) that may not be available from other providers on transfer. People's Pension (retained): policy documentation shows it is a live workplace scheme; the plan schedule for Scottish Widows confirms that plan has no current employer contributions (Employer N/A). The Policy Research document (category G) confirms no GAR, no GMP, no guaranteed growth rates across all five plans. The Aviva policy info confirms a Lifestage Investment Approach (auto-switching) that would be lost on transfer, though this is not addressed in the report's lost-features analysis.
    Discrepancies:
      - 1. The report's /replacementAnalysis/0/featuresOrGuaranteesLost path is NotPresentInDocument — the report does not enumerate specific features or guarantees lost for the plans that are actually switched or encashed (Aviva SP50163762, Zurich, Standard Life, Scottish Widows ZU4225575). The extraction confirms this is report silence, not extraction failure.
      - 2. Standard Life policy documentation explicitly notes a Differential Fund Discount / Leaver Discount of 0.4800% that 'may not be available from other pension providers upon transfer.' The report does not address this potentially valuable feature being lost on transfer.
      - 3. Aviva SP50163762 has a Lifestage Investment Approach (auto-switching) confirmed in the Aviva policy info. The report does not address whether this feature is lost and whether it is relevant to the client.
      - 4. The report states employer contributions would be lost for People's Pension (correctly leading to a retain recommendation), but the Scottish Widows plan schedule confirms employer contributions are already N/A for that plan — consistent with the report's basis for the retain recommendation. No contradiction here, but the employer contribution loss analysis applies only to the retained plan, not to any switched plan.
      - 5. The figures computed show a discrepancy between the sum of individual plan values (£116,998.47) and the recommended transfer value (£110,185), an unexplained shortfall of £6,813.47, which may indicate not all plan values are being transferred — but this is flagged as a separate CHK-001 matter rather than a lost-features issue per se.
    The comparison can be made. Provider documentation is present for all five plans and corroborates the report's 'no safeguarded benefits' assertions for Scottish Widows, Aviva, Zurich and Standard Life. The Zurich letter is the most explicit, confirming guarantees, GMP, MVR, protected TFC and other safeguarded features do not apply. The Scottish Widows schedule, Aviva policy info and Standard Life letter each independently confirm absence of GARs, GMPs and safeguarded benefits. The Policy Research document (category G) further corroborates across all plans. So far as the 'no safeguarded benefits' claim is concerned, it is well-evidenced. However, two genuine gaps remain after the guards. First, the Standard Life documentation explicitly warns that a Differential Fund Discount (Leaver Discount 0.4800%) 'may not be available from other pension providers upon transfer.' This is a potentially valuable feature that the report does not acknowledge or address in its lost-features analysis. Whether Standard Life is being transferred or retained is itself ambiguous given the internal inconsistency noted in the extraction, but the charge advantage is real and documented. Second, Aviva's Lifestage Investment Approach is confirmed in the policy documentation and is not addressed in the report's replacement analysis. These are omissions of material features from the lost-features disclosure, not mere minor dimensions. The employer contribution point is properly handled — the People's Pension is retained precisely because of the employer contribution risk, and the Scottish Widows plan is confirmed as having no active employer contributions, so no employer contribution loss arises for the encashed plan. On vulnerability: no specific vulnerability factors in the evidence pack bear directly on the lost-features analysis beyond the general need for clear disclosure, which is already an issue here. Severity is assessed as Moderate: the 'no safeguarded benefits' position is corroborated, so the highest-severity trigger (safeguarded benefits given up with no analysis) does not apply, but a documented charge advantage (Standard Life leaver discount) that may be lost on transfer is not addressed in the report, and the Aviva Lifestage feature is unaddressed — both are material omissions for plans being switched.

  [G9.4] Alternatives to the switch considered
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The canonical model records three alternatives considered: (1) Switch investment funds within People's Pension — rejected because no multi-asset funds are available and the fund range is limited, and because the funds do not meet the client's risk profile of 5. (2) Leave pension invested and allow to grow (Do Nothing) — considered but no explicit rejection reasons recorded; the model notes this is inferred from the report presenting both encashment and retention as options for Scottish Widows before recommending encashment. (3) Switch investment funds within Zurich — rejected because Zurich does not offer risk level 5 funds (requiring a blend of a risk 7 and risk 2 fund), the blended approach reduces diversification, blended funds have not performed as well as Aviva options, and the client's main priority is consolidation with all drawdown options. No separate 'alternatives considered' section is recorded for the Aviva (SP50163762) or Standard Life (D2301334000) switches.
    File:     Passage P1 confirms for People's Pension: 'the funds do not meet your risk profile; Currently, Scottish Widows does not offer a single multi-asset fund...' (note: this text relates to Scottish Widows, not People's Pension, but P1 also contains the People's Pension advice table showing 'Retain'). For the Aviva existing plan (SP50163762), P1 shows a recommendation to switch with reasons (lower costs, lifestyling concern, greater investment choice, enhanced retirement flexibility, adviser charging facilitation, ongoing monitoring) but no explicit consideration of an in-situ fund switch or do-nothing alternative. For Standard Life (D2301334000), P2 similarly sets out reasons for switching (lower costs at 0.08%, lifestyling, greater investment choice, enhanced retirement flexibility, adviser charging facilitation, ongoing monitoring) without an explicit alternative-considered section. For Zurich (P10026-719-001/DL), P4 explicitly addresses the in-situ switch alternative: blended funds at risk 7 and risk 2, no multi-asset funds at risk 5, diversification reduction, marginal underperformance, and consolidation objective. Passage P3 includes a comparison table covering all five plans with retirement options, fund numbers, adviser servicing, and other considerations. The Pension Switch Report (P5, P9) covers Aviva, Zurich and Standard Life with charge comparisons and alternative comparisons section (per P5 contents: 'Alternative comparisons — Aviva Life & Pensions UK Limited - Aviva plan only; Standard Life - SL pension only; Zurich - Zurich pension only') but the actual alternative comparison content for each is truncated in the passages provided.
    Discrepancies:
      - The canonical model records an 'alternatives considered' entry for People's Pension (in-situ fund switch) but People's Pension is ultimately recommended to be Retained, not switched — the alternative analysis for People's Pension is therefore not a replacement-context alternatives assessment but rather the reason consolidation into People's Pension was rejected as the receiving scheme. This is a mislabelling/framing difference.
      - No explicit 'alternatives to switching' section is recorded in the canonical model for the Aviva (SP50163762) existing plan switch, and the file passages (P1) do not show an in-situ fund switch or do-nothing alternative being addressed for that plan.
      - No explicit 'alternatives to switching' section is recorded in the canonical model for the Standard Life (D2301334000) switch, and P2 does not show an in-situ fund switch or do-nothing alternative being addressed for that plan.
      - The Do Nothing / Leave to Grow alternative is recorded only as 'Inferred' for Scottish Widows (not a ceding plan being switched — it is being encashed) and is not recorded for the four plans actually being switched to Aviva Platform.
      - The Pension Switch Report (P5/P9) lists an 'Alternative comparisons' section for each of the three ceding plans (Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life), which could constitute alternatives analysis, but the content of those sections is not available in the retrieved passages to confirm what alternatives were assessed or whether rejection reasoning is adequate.
      - The canonical model records no rejection reasons for the Do Nothing alternative, meaning the alternative is noted but not substantively addressed.
      - Standard Life is noted as having lifestyling funds as a reason to switch, but no in-situ fund switch away from lifestyling within Standard Life is addressed as an alternative in the canonical model or visible file passages.
    The report addresses alternatives most thoroughly for Zurich (P4 provides detailed, plan-specific reasoning covering fund availability, blend limitations, diversification, performance and consolidation objective) and provides some reasoning for People's Pension (though this is framed as rejection of People's Pension as the consolidation vehicle, not alternatives to switching People's Pension — which is in fact retained). For the two remaining ceding plans — Aviva (SP50163762) and Standard Life (D2301334000) — the suitability report passages (P1, P2) set out reasons favouring the switch but do not explicitly address alternatives such as an in-situ fund switch or a do-nothing option. The Pension Switch Report (P5/P9) references an 'Alternative comparisons' section covering each ceding plan, which is a structural positive, but the actual content of those subsections is not available in the retrieved passages, so it cannot be confirmed whether adequate rejection reasoning is present for Aviva and Standard Life. The Standard Life plan's lifestyling issue could theoretically be resolved by an in-situ fund switch, but this is not addressed. The Aviva existing plan also has no adviser servicing (P10 confirms 'This plan does not allow adviser charging'), which is a relevant plan-specific reason but is raised as a benefit of the new plan rather than as a rejection of the alternative of remaining invested. The Do Nothing alternative is only inferred for Scottish Widows (an encashment, not a transfer) and is absent as an explicit consideration for the switched plans. The guard on set-coverage is relevant: some case-wide reasoning (consolidation objective, risk alignment, drawdown flexibility) is applied across plans, which may legitimately cover multiple plans. However, Aviva and Standard Life differ materially from Zurich in that the in-situ alternatives reasoning is more developed for Zurich. Given the missing content of the alternatives sections in the switch report and the absence of explicit in-situ alternative consideration for Aviva and Standard Life in the suitability report, a Potential Concern at Moderate severity is appropriate. No vulnerability factors are apparent in the evidence that would materially alter this assessment.
