  [G9.5] Switching costs exceed the benefits
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts: (1) No exit penalties on any ceding plan ('No charges for transfer and/or switching'). (2) No initial advice fee is disclosed. (3) For the Aviva existing plan: the new arrangement is 0.06% cheaper, saving £2.25 p.a. (4) For the Scottish Widows small pot (£200 fund): withdrawn under small pots rules — 25% tax-free cash, 75% taxed at marginal rate, estimated net receipt £170. (5) For the Standard Life plan: similarly 0.06% cheaper, £2.25 saving. (6) For the Zurich plan: 0.49% cheaper, £506.85 p.a. saving; annual charge difference 0.26%, £268.94; out-of-market risk noted. (7) Critical yields stated as 1.9% (low growth), 4.8% (mid growth), 7.8% (high growth) for the Zurich switch. Benefits cited include lower costs, removal of lifestyling risk, greater investment choice, adviser charging facilitation, ongoing monitoring, multi-asset fund selection, and enhanced retirement flexibility.
    File:     The Defaqto pension switch report (passages P1–P8) provides the following: (1) Critical yields for Zurich switch: 1.9% (low), 4.8% (mid), 7.8% (high) — corroborated by P7. (2) Effect of switching Zurich: -£10,400 (9.6% decrease) at low growth; -£12,000 (9.4% decrease) at mid growth; -£15,000 (10.0% decrease) at high growth — per P7. (3) The new solution RIY for Zurich is 2.1%–2.3% vs existing 0.3%, indicating materially higher charges in the switch tool. (4) Immediate fund value impact: existing £110,185.60 vs new solution day one £106,880.60 — a shortfall of £3,305 (P2, P7). (5) The switch report shows the Aviva existing plan has a 0.6% RIY and new solution also 0.6% RIY with a critical yield of 6.0% and effect of switching of -£30 or 0.6% decrease (P5). (6) Standard Life switch shows small effect of switching at -£10 or 0.3% decrease and critical yield of 5.9% at high growth (P6, P8). (7) No corroboration found in passages for the report's 0.06% saving, £2.25 saving, 0.49% saving, £506.85 saving, £268.94 annual difference, £170 net small pots receipt, or the 25%/75% tax split figures. (8) The switch report shows a 0.6% RIY on the new solution vs 0.3% on the existing Zurich pension — the new solution has a higher RIY, not lower, contradicting the report's cost-saving narrative for the largest plan.
    Discrepancies:
      - 1. The report claims the new arrangement is 0.06% cheaper than the existing Aviva plan; no figure of 0.06% cost saving appears in any passage. The Defaqto switch report (P5) shows identical RIY of 0.6% for both existing and new solution on the Aviva plan, with a critical yield of 6.0% — no saving evidenced.
      - 2. The report claims annual charge saving of £2.25 for the Aviva plan switch; this figure does not appear in any passage.
      - 3. The report claims the new arrangement is 0.06% cheaper than the Standard Life plan; no corroboration in passages. P6/P8 show near-identical RIY (0.6% vs 0.6%) with -£10 effect of switching.
      - 4. The report claims £2.25 annual saving for Standard Life plan; not corroborated in any passage.
      - 5. The report claims 0.49% annual charge saving and £506.85 saving for the Zurich switch. The Defaqto switch report (P7) shows the new solution RIY is 2.1%–2.3% vs existing 0.3%, indicating the new solution is materially MORE expensive in fund charges, directly contradicting the report's claimed saving.
      - 6. The report claims an annual charge difference of 0.26% (£268.94) for the Zurich plan; neither figure corroborated in passages. The switch tool figures imply the opposite direction.
      - 7. The switch report (P7) shows projected maturity values are lower under the new solution at all growth rates for Zurich (e.g. -£12,000 at mid growth), directly contradicting the report's claim that lower costs justify the switch.
      - 8. The immediate impact on fund value is a reduction of £3,305 (P2/P7: existing £110,185.60 vs new day-one £106,880.60) — this switching cost is not quantified or addressed in the report's cost-benefit analysis.
      - 9. The report states estimated net small pots receipt of £170 from a £200 fund; no corroboration in passages (nearest figure £30 in P5, which relates to a different comparison).
      - 10. The report states 25% tax-free / 75% taxed at marginal rate for small pots treatment; not corroborated in any passage.
      - 11. The report's critical yield of 4.8% is framed as based on 'mid-growth rate of 2.94%' including advice fees; while 4.8% and 2.94% are corroborated in P7 and P1, the report describes this as requiring only 4.8% growth which is presented as achievable, but the switch tool at mid growth (2.95%) shows a £12,000 decrease — the framing in the report appears misleading relative to the switch tool evidence.
      - 12. The initial advice fee amount is absent from the report; this is a cost of switching that should be disclosed.
    This is a Potential Concern of high severity. The most material issue is the Zurich plan, which is the largest ceding plan by value (approximately £103,439). The report asserts the new arrangement is 0.49% cheaper (£506.85 saving p.a.) and that this justifies the switch. However, the Defaqto pension switch report (P7) directly contradicts this: the new solution has a RIY of 2.1%–2.3% compared to the existing Zurich pension's 0.3%, meaning the new solution is materially more expensive in terms of charges drag. At every growth scenario the projected maturity value under the new solution is lower than retaining the Zurich pension (e.g. -£12,000 at mid growth, -£15,000 at high growth). The critical yield at mid growth is 4.8%, meaning the new solution must grow at 4.8% p.a. merely to match the existing pension — against a mid-growth assumption of 2.95%. The report's cost-saving narrative for the Zurich switch is therefore directly contradicted by the switch tool evidence. The immediate fund value shortfall of £3,305 on day one of the switch is evidenced in P2/P7 but not addressed in the report's switching cost analysis. For the Aviva existing plan the switch tool shows no cost saving (identical RIY) with a critical yield of 6.0% — well above mid-growth assumptions — contradicting the report's claimed 0.06% saving. For the Standard Life plan the position is similar. The annual saving figures (£2.25, £506.85, £268.94) and the small pots receipt figure (£170) are all unsubstantiated by any passage. The initial advice fee is not disclosed, leaving total switching costs incomplete. The benefits cited (lifestyling removal, investment choice, adviser servicing) are non-financial and while such benefits can justify cost in principle (per the guard), the guard requires the report to argue this explicitly in the context of the actual cost comparison. Here the report misrepresents the cost comparison itself, so the non-financial benefit argument does not rescue the finding. No vulnerability overlay material to this specific check is identified in the evidence. Overall, the report's switching cost/benefit analysis is materially contradicted by the independent switch tool evidence across multiple plans, most significantly for the largest plan (Zurich).

  [G9.6] Lost guarantees or benefits disadvantage the client
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report states that all five existing arrangements have no exit penalties, with the description 'No charges for transfer and/or switching' applied uniformly across all five plans. The extracted model has no entry for safeguarded benefits being given up (/existingArrangements[]/safeguardedBenefits/isBeingGivenUp is absent), meaning the extraction did not capture any assertion from the report on whether safeguarded benefits exist or are being surrendered.
    File:     Scottish Widows (SW Further Info 4): confirms 'there are no guarantees, discounts or bonuses attached to this member's plan' and that Market Value Adjustment/Reduction are both not applicable. The Policy Research file (Test Case 1) confirms no Market Value Reduction for any of the five plans. The Fact Find (B) shows fields for GMP, Enhanced Tax Free Cash, Guaranteed Annuity Rate, EFI/Loyalty Bonus/Terminal Bonus, Guaranteed Growth Rates all blank/unpopulated for the plans shown. The Pension Switch Report (G) confirms no immediate switching fees beyond the day-one shortfall of £3,305 arising from adviser charges, and notes no penalties at point of switching. Provider documentation for Scottish Widows explicitly rules out guarantees. However, no equivalent provider-level confirmation of no guarantees is present in the pack for Aviva (existing), Zurich, Standard Life, or People's Pension.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts no exit penalties for all five plans, which is corroborated for Scottish Widows by provider documentation and for all five by the policy research table (no MVR). However, the report is silent on safeguarded benefits (e.g. guaranteed annuity rates, GMP, protected tax-free cash) for all five plans — the extraction notes this path as absent from the document, meaning the report does not address safeguarded benefits at all.
      - Provider-level confirmation of no safeguarded benefits exists only for Scottish Widows [P13]. No equivalent provider document confirming absence of guaranteed annuity rates, GMP, protected benefits or other valuable features is present in the pack for Aviva (existing personal pension), Zurich Managed Pension, Standard Life, or People's Pension.
      - The Fact Find [P14] leaves GMP, Enhanced Tax Free Cash, Guaranteed Annuity Rate, EFI/Loyalty Bonus/Terminal Bonus, and Guaranteed Growth Rates blank for the plans shown, which is ambiguous — it could mean none exist or that the fields were not completed.
      - The Pension Switch Report shows a day-one shortfall of £3,305 [P4/P5] and projects lower maturity values under the new solution at all growth rates for the combined switch [P4], yet the report does not appear to address whether any of this reduction is attributable to loss of guaranteed benefits rather than charges alone.
    The guard for G9.6 states it only applies where G9.3 identified a benefit actually being given up. However, the threshold question — whether safeguarded benefits exist — cannot be answered from the pack for four of the five ceding plans. The Scottish Widows plan is the only one with explicit provider confirmation that no guarantees, discounts or bonuses are attached [P13]. For the remaining four plans (Aviva existing, Zurich, Standard Life, People's Pension), the pack contains no provider-level documentation ruling out safeguarded benefits. The Fact Find leaves the relevant fields blank [P14], which is not equivalent to a positive confirmation of absence. The Policy Research file confirms no MVR for all five plans [P12], but MVR is not the same as guaranteed annuity rates, GMP or protected tax-free cash. The Zurich plan in particular holds the largest value (£103,439.24) and was the primary driver of the switch economics; the absence of any provider document confirming no safeguarded benefits for Zurich is the most material gap. The suitability report itself is silent on safeguarded benefits entirely (the extraction flags the path as NotPresentInDocument). Under COBS 9A.2.18R and COBS 9.2.2R, the adviser must consider and document valuable features that may be lost. The silence in the report, combined with missing provider evidence for four plans, means this requirement is not demonstrably met. This is assessed as a Potential Concern at High severity because the largest ceding plan by value (Zurich, £103,439) has no provider documentation confirming absence of safeguarded benefits, and the report does not address the point at all. Vulnerability overlay: no vulnerability characteristics identified in the evidence appear to alter the conclusion on this specific technical point, though the client's stated desire to simplify his pensions because he finds them hard to understand [P12] underscores the importance of ensuring the adviser has properly assessed what valuable features might be lost before consolidating.

  [G9.7] Other alternatives provide better outcomes for the client
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report considers five options: (1) leave existing pensions untouched, (2) leave in place and switch funds in situ, (3) switch into another existing arrangement, (4) switch into the employer's pension (People's Pension), and (5) set up a new plan (Aviva). Option 4 is discounted because People's Pension is the live workplace scheme with ongoing employer contributions that would be lost on transfer, the funds do not meet the client's risk profile (rating 5), the plan is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution, and there is a £4.50 annual admin charge with no multi-asset funds. Option 2 (in situ fund switch within Zurich) is discounted because Zurich has no risk-level-5 multi-asset funds, a blended approach reduces diversification, blended funds have marginally underperformed Aviva options, and the client's main priority is consolidation, control and full drawdown flexibility. Option 2 for People's Pension is discounted because there are no multi-asset funds and the funds do not meet the risk profile. Option 5 (new Aviva plan) is recommended. The Scottish Widows plan is recommended for encashment under small pots rules. People's Pension is retained. Aviva existing, Zurich and Standard Life plans are switched into the new Aviva Platform Personal Pension.
    File:     The pension switch report (Defaqto) provides plan-by-plan comparison tables for each ceding arrangement (Aviva plan, Zurich, Standard Life) against the new solution, covering projected maturity values at low/mid/high growth rates, reduction in yield and effect of switching. For the Zurich plan (largest by value at £103,439), mid-growth existing projected value is £127,000 vs new solution £125,000 (effect of switching: £2,000 or 1.6% decrease); reduction in yield moves from 0.3% to 0.6%. Alternative comparisons are provided for each plan individually. The overall comparison (all switched plans combined) shows existing £129,000 vs new solution £124,000 (mid-growth). The report shows no benefits lost on switching for any plan. The Zurich policy info confirms drawdown is not available on the Zurich plan, supporting the flexibility rationale for switching. The suitability report table shows People's Pension allows FAD and UFPLS and is the current workplace scheme with employer contributions noted. The research table in the suitability report compares retirement options, fund numbers, servicing adviser availability and other considerations across all five existing arrangements. The pension switch report includes an alternative comparisons section examining each ceding plan individually and an overall position. The stakeholder pension benchmark comparison is also included. The suitability report explicitly addresses why Option 4 (employer scheme) is discounted and why an in situ fund switch is not appropriate for Zurich or People's Pension.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states the People's Pension plan is '0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' (used as a reason to discount Option 4). However, the report also identifies an internal inconsistency: the Aviva existing arrangement charge is 0.50% and the new Aviva Platform charge is 0.44%, a difference of 0.06% in the other direction — the new solution is cheaper by 0.06%, not more expensive. The text as quoted ('the plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution') appears to refer to People's Pension being more expensive than the Aviva solution, but this is not independently evidenced in the file by a People's Pension charge comparison table.
      - The pension switch report's alternative comparisons section covers Aviva plan only, Standard Life only, and Zurich only — it does not separately model the alternative of switching everything except Zurich (i.e. retaining Zurich in situ while switching the others), which would be relevant given Zurich's dominance of the portfolio by value.
      - The overall mid-growth projected maturity value with the new solution is £124,000 versus existing £129,000 — the new solution produces a lower projected outcome at mid-growth. The report justifies this on qualitative grounds (consolidation, drawdown flexibility, cost savings) but the quantitative comparison shows a materially worse projected outcome under the recommended switch.
      - The report claims the Zurich blended funds 'have not performed as well as the options available through Aviva, albeit marginally' but the pension switch report's Defaqto data for the Zurich fund shows N/A for fund statistics and diamond ratings, providing limited independent corroboration of this performance claim. The suitability report's performance chart does include a comparison (A=Aviva existing, B=Aviva risk 5 portfolio, D=Zurich) but Zurich is labelled D and is absent from the cumulative performance table rows shown.
      - The rejection reasons for Options 1–3 in the /research/optionsConsidered[] entries carry no documented rejection reasons in the canonical model (rejectionReasons arrays are empty), even though narrative reasons appear elsewhere in the report for some of these.
      - The report asserts Scottish Widows encashment under small pots rules but no explicit alternatives analysis for the Scottish Widows plan specifically (beyond noting encashment vs retention as inferred) is evidenced in the pension switch report's alternative comparisons section.
    The report addresses alternatives at both a generic level (five options) and at a plan-specific level for Zurich and People's Pension. The key substantive alternatives — in situ fund switch, consolidation into employer scheme, do nothing — are all considered and discounted with reasons. The consolidation objective (OBJ3) is a legitimate client-driven objective per the guard, and simplification/control are valid reasons.
    
    The most significant issue is the quantitative outcome: the pension switch report shows that switching produces a lower projected maturity value than retaining existing arrangements (£124,000 vs £129,000 at mid-growth across the combined scenario). The report justifies higher costs by reference to qualitative benefits (drawdown flexibility on Zurich, consolidation, adviser charging facilitation, ongoing monitoring). The Zurich policy info at [P9] confirms drawdown is not available on the Zurich plan, which corroborates the flexibility rationale as a genuine benefit lost by staying. This is a meaningful qualitative benefit that partially addresses the projected value shortfall, and the pension switch report explicitly notes 'No benefits were listed for the existing products' regarding guaranteed or safeguarded benefits — confirmed for Zurich at [P9] ('Guarantees, bonuses, guaranteed minimum pension, safeguarding... do not apply to this plan') and for Scottish Widows at [P24] (all guarantees shown as No).
    
    However, the claim that People's Pension is '0.06% more expensive' than the recommended solution as a reason to discount Option 4 is not independently evidenced by a charge comparison document for People's Pension specifically in the file. The pension switch report covers Aviva, Zurich and Standard Life but does not include a People's Pension charge comparison. This leaves one element of the Option 4 discounting unsubstantiated by the retrieved evidence.
    
    The performance claim about Zurich blended funds underperforming is only marginally supported — the Defaqto report shows N/A for Zurich fund statistics, and while the suitability report's performance chart includes a Zurich line (D), its cumulative performance data is not reproduced in the retrieved passages. This is a low-level concern given the 'albeit marginally' qualifier used.
    
    The overall finding is a Potential Concern at Low-to-Moderate severity: the alternatives analysis is substantively present and the qualitative rationale (especially drawdown unavailability on Zurich) is well-supported, but the charge comparison used to discount Option 4 (People's Pension 0.06% more expensive) lacks direct evidential support in the file, and the projected value shortfall from switching (£5,000 or ~3.9% at mid-growth) is not explicitly addressed in the report as a trade-off the client is accepting. No vulnerability factors are identified in the evidence that would materially alter this assessment.

  [G9.8] Evidence supporting claims that higher costs are justified by additional benefits
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts benefits of switching across multiple ceding plans: (1) Scottish Widows small pot withdrawal under small pots rules, tax-efficient, estimated £170 net receipt, not detracting from retirement plan; (2) Aviva existing plan: lower costs (0.06% cheaper), lifestyling fund risk, greater investment choice, adviser charging facilitation, ongoing monitoring; (3) Standard Life: lower costs (0.08% cheaper), lifestyling, greater investment choice, enhanced retirement flexibility, adviser charging facilitation, ongoing monitoring; (4) Zurich: greater investment choice, multi-asset fund selection (Zurich cannot offer risk level 5 single fund, requires blended approach with interest deposits), enhanced retirement flexibility, adviser charging facilitation, ongoing monitoring — but higher annual charges (+£268.94 or +0.26% p.a.), justified by non-cost benefits. The report states 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' for Aviva, which contradicts the charge table showing the new solution is cheaper (0.50% existing vs 0.44% new = saving). For Zurich, the report openly acknowledges higher costs and claims the non-cost benefits justify the switch. The report states the new plan needs to outperform by £4,672 in the first year (4.8% critical yield at mid-growth 2.94%).
    File:     The pension switch report (category G) confirms: (1) Aviva plan switched to Aviva Platform — existing 0.50% charge vs new solution charge of 0.6% RIY (per switch report comparison table), with the effect of switching showing a decrease. (2) Zurich pension switch — new solution has 0.6% RIY vs existing 0.3% RIY; at mid-growth the new solution projects £125,000 vs existing £128,000 (Zurich only alternative), and the effect of switching is negative across all growth rates (e.g. £12,000 or 9.4% decrease at mid-growth when switching all three). The switch report ranks the new solution 5th out of 10, below retaining Zurich alone (1st, 0.2% RIY, £128,000), retaining existing solution (2nd), Aviva plan alone (3rd), and Standard Life alone (4th). The critical yield for the Zurich-only switch is 4.8% at mid-growth against a 2.95% assumed rate. The Zurich policy information confirms no exit/transfer charges and no fund switch charges. The suitability report charge table shows Aviva 0.50% existing vs -0.06% impact (i.e. new solution is cheaper for Aviva). Standard Life is shown with 0.08% saving. Zurich shows existing 0.93% vs impact +0.26% (more expensive). The report's statement that the Aviva plan 'is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' is internally contradictory — the table shows the new solution is cheaper. The switch report confirms new solution investment details as CT Universal MAP Balanced 50% and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced 50%. The suitability report mentions the Zurich fund blend (Managed AP + Interest Deposits) as justification but the switch report appendix for Zurich shows only 'fund' with Zurich provider and N/A ISIN — no named fund details corroborating the specific blend claim. Policy research (category E) confirms Zurich has no exit charges and fund switch charge of £0.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states for the Aviva plan 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' — this is contradicted by the charge table on page 11 which shows Aviva existing 0.50% vs new solution impact of -0.06%, meaning the new solution is cheaper, not the existing plan. The wording should say the existing plan is 0.06% MORE expensive, but the sentence as written says the plan (existing) is 0.06% more expensive than recommended — this is actually consistent with the table but the phrasing is ambiguous and the extraction flags it as contradictory.
      - The switch report ranks the new solution 5th out of 10 alternatives at mid-growth (£125,000), below retaining the existing solution (£127,000, 2nd), Zurich alone (£128,000, 1st), Aviva alone (£126,000, 3rd), and Standard Life alone (£126,000, 4th). The report does not address why a materially better-projected option (retaining Zurich alone or the existing solution) was not recommended.
      - For the Zurich switch, the report claims the benefit of 'multi-asset fund selection' because Zurich cannot offer a risk level 5 fund, requiring a blend of Managed AP (risk 7) and Interest Deposits (risk 2). The switch report appendix for Zurich shows only a generic 'fund' entry with N/A ISIN — no corroboration of the specific fund blend described in the suitability report.
      - The Zurich switch involves a higher annual cost (+£268.94 / +0.26% p.a.) and the switch report shows a negative effect of switching at all growth rates (e.g. -£12,000 or -9.4% at mid-growth for the combined switch). The critical yield of 4.8% substantially exceeds the assumed mid-growth rate of 2.95%. The report asserts the benefits justify the additional cost but the switch report's own projections show material expected underperformance.
      - The report states an initial advice charge of £3,305 which reduces the day-one fund value from £110,185.60 to £106,880.60 (confirmed in the switch report). This immediate capital reduction is disclosed in the switch report but the suitability report's justification of benefits does not explicitly address how the initial charge impact is recouped, beyond the general critical yield discussion.
      - The report's claim that the new solution offers 'lower costs' for Zurich is contradicted by the charge analysis showing a +0.26% (+£268.94 p.a.) increase.
      - The switch report ranks the new solution below four alternative options including retaining all existing pensions, yet the suitability report does not explain why these higher-projected alternatives were rejected.
      - The report does not address whether the Zurich lifestyling concern (funds moving outside agreed risk range) could have been resolved by an internal fund switch rather than a full transfer — the suitability report dismisses this briefly ('we would not recommend an internal fund switch') but does not provide detailed evidence that the client's consolidation preference was formally documented as overriding the cost disadvantage.
    The central concern for this requirement is whether claims that higher costs are justified by additional benefits are supported by the evidence — specifically for the Zurich transfer where costs increase materially. For the Aviva and Standard Life plans, the new solution is cheaper, so the 'higher costs justified by benefits' test does not bite per the guard. However, for the Zurich plan (the largest by value at ~£103,439), the switch involves higher annual charges (+0.26% / +£268.94 p.a.) and the switch report's own projections show materially adverse outcomes: at mid-growth (2.95%), switching Zurich only results in a projected shortfall of £3,000 (2.1% decrease), and the combined switch produces a £12,000 shortfall (9.4% decrease) vs retaining the existing solution. The critical yield of 4.8% substantially exceeds the assumed 2.95% growth rate, meaning the switch is projected to leave the client worse off on a like-for-like basis. The suitability report justifies this on non-cost grounds: (a) inability to access a proper risk-level-5 fund within Zurich (requiring a blend of risk-7 and risk-2 funds); (b) consolidation objective; (c) enhanced flexibility and adviser servicing. The multi-asset fund argument is supported in principle by the suitability report's description but there is no corroboration in the switch report appendix of the specific Zurich fund blend (showing only N/A ISIN). More significantly, the switch report ranks retaining Zurich alone as the highest projected outcome (£128,000, 0.2% RIY) above every other option including the new solution. The suitability report does not substantively address why the client's interest in consolidation and risk alignment could not be better served by retaining Zurich (the best performing option) and switching only the smaller plans. The adviser dismisses internal fund switching briefly but does not present evidence that Zurich's fund range was formally assessed against the client's specific risk requirement in a way that evidences no suitable alternative existed. The vulnerability overlay does not appear to present additional material concerns based on available evidence — no specific vulnerability characteristics are identified in the retrieved passages that would heighten the concern about the Zurich switch cost justification beyond the standard assessment. The charge direction issue for Aviva (the report's ambiguous wording) is minor and explained by the extraction ambiguity note — the table clearly shows the new solution is cheaper for Aviva. The main concern is the Zurich transfer: a switch increasing costs by £268.94 p.a. with a projected £3,000-£12,000 shortfall at mid-growth, where the switch report itself places the new solution below multiple alternatives including retaining Zurich, without adequate evidence in the file that the non-cost benefits were weighed rigorously against this material projected disadvantage.

  [G9.9] Evidence elsewhere in the file that supports or contradicts the replacement rationale
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report makes five replacement/retention decisions: (1) People's Pension retained because it is the current workplace scheme with ongoing employer contributions that would be forfeited on transfer; (2) Scottish Widows encashed because it does not offer a single multi-asset fund aligned with risk rating 5, would require combining two funds (one entirely cash/money market), does not allow adviser management, and limits withdrawal flexibility; (3) Aviva existing pension switched to Aviva Platform for lower costs (0.06% saving), escape from lifestyling fund that will drift outside agreed risk range, greater investment choice, adviser servicing facilitation, and ongoing monitoring; (4) Zurich switched because no risk level 5 multi-asset funds exist within it, blend of risk-7 and risk-2 funds required, single-asset approach reduces diversification, blended funds have underperformed Aviva alternatives, plus lower costs and flexibility; (5) Standard Life switched for lower costs, greater investment choice, escape from lifestyling, adviser servicing, and ongoing monitoring. The report also claims the Zurich switch involves higher annual charges of £268.94 more per year yet is still justified by risk alignment and consolidation benefits, while the Aviva switch is described as delivering a 0.06% cost saving.
    File:     [P14]/[P10] Scottish Widows policy info confirms: no guarantees, discounts or bonuses, Money4Life Plans do not support ongoing adviser charging or transferring of Servicing Rights, Market Value Adjustment/Reduction not applicable. File note [P16] records: 'Discounted as no servicing and reduced flexibility with withdrawing, so no more research was done' for Scottish Widows; confirms Aviva's only mixed-asset fund is risk-rated 4 (not 5); confirms Zurich has no risk-level 5 funds, requiring blend of 7 and 2; confirms Standard Life funds in the 20-60% equity range are more expensive than Aviva. [P17] records current risk levels: Aviva risk-2, Zurich risk-7, Standard Life risk-4. [P8] Aviva policy info confirms lifestage/auto-switching investment approach is in place, no guaranteed rates, no Guaranteed Minimum Pension, plan value £3,744.36. [P3] Zurich policy info explicitly states: 'Guarantees, bonuses, guaranteed minimum pension, safeguarding, life styling, block/bulk transfers, market value reduction, protected or enhanced tax free cash do not apply to this plan.' [P5]/[P6]/[P20] Pension switch report (Defaqto) shows a comparison of existing vs new solution with projected maturity values, stakeholder pension comparison, and alternative comparisons for each plan individually. [P6] shows the new solution ranks 5th out of 9 options by projected maturity value (£125,000 vs £128,000 for best option, the Zurich pension itself), with the existing solution ranking 2nd at £127,000. [P22] shows the Pension Switch Charges table: Aviva existing 0.50%, impact -0.06% (i.e. new is cheaper by 0.06%); Zurich existing 0.93%, impact -0.49% (new is cheaper); Standard Life existing 0.18% fund + 0.10% platform = 0.28% but impact shown as +0.26% (new is MORE expensive by 0.26%). The report states the Zurich switch involves £268.94 more per year — but the charges table shows Zurich existing at 0.93% and the new solution at 0.44%, implying a saving not an increase. The canonical model's own arithmetic confirms EA4 existing annual charge at 0.93% on Zurich (~£961.98) vs new solution 0.44% (~£484.81), a saving of ~£477. The report's claim of £268.94 higher cost for the Zurich switch is contradicted by the charges table. The report also states the Aviva switch saves 0.06% which is consistent with [P22]. People's Pension: file note [P17] notes 'No alternative as Defaqto risk rates all of them as a 9' and report confirms retention due to ongoing employer contributions.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report claims the Zurich switch results in higher annual charges of £268.94 more per year, yet the Pension Switch Charges table [P22] shows the Zurich existing charge at 0.93% and the new solution impact as -0.49% (a saving), which at Zurich's fund value of ~£103,439 implies a saving of approximately £477/year rather than an additional cost of £268.94.
      - The canonical model simultaneously records a 'SwitchJustification' for the Zurich plan citing 'lower costs' (page 8 quote) and a separate entry acknowledging 'higher annual charges (£268.94 more per year)' — these two rationale entries for the same plan are internally contradictory within the report itself.
      - The charges arithmetic extracted from the report shows EA5 impact (attributed to Zurich) as £268.94 at 0.26%, which the canonical model applies to Zurich (£103,438 fund value), but the charges table in [P22] shows the Zurich impact as -0.49% (saving), not +0.26% (cost). The +0.26% / £268.94 figure appears to relate to the Standard Life plan (which has a 0.18%+0.10%=0.28% existing charge), not Zurich.
      - The Pension Switch Charges table [P22] shows Standard Life existing charge at 0.18% with an impact of +0.26%, meaning the new solution is more expensive for Standard Life by approximately 0.26% yet the report presents this switch as delivering 'lower costs' without acknowledging the cost increase for Standard Life specifically.
      - The Defaqto report [P6] shows the new solution ranks 5th by projected maturity value at £125,000, below the existing solution (2nd, £127,000) and even below the Zurich pension alone (1st, £128,000), which could be considered contradictory to the overall cost/benefit narrative, though consolidation and non-cost rationale are also cited.
      - The report's rationale for the Aviva switch describes 'lower costs' with 'the plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' — this wording is ambiguous/contradictory (it says the plan is more expensive, but the context means the existing plan, not the new one), though the charges table confirms the new solution is 0.06% cheaper for Aviva which supports the intended meaning.
      - Scottish Widows encashment rationale cites funds limited to combining two funds (one entirely money market/cash). The file note [P16] records '30% liquid fund and 70% of Portfolio three' as the alternative, and [P14] confirms no adviser servicing possible, supporting the rationale, but no explicit fund range document confirms the report's assertion that no single multi-asset fund at risk level 5 exists — this is corroborated only by file note rather than provider fund literature.
    The replacement rationale for most plans is broadly supported by the file evidence. The People's Pension retention on employer contribution grounds is unambiguously supported. The Scottish Widows encashment rationale (no adviser servicing, no suitable single-asset fund, limited flexibility) is corroborated by [P14] confirming Money4Life plans do not support ongoing adviser charging, and by the file note [P16]. The Aviva switch rationale (lifestyling confirmed by [P8], no guarantees per [P8], 0.06% cost saving per [P22]) is well supported. The Zurich switch rationale regarding lack of risk-5 multi-asset funds and blend of risk-7/risk-2 is corroborated by [P16] and [P3]. However, a material discrepancy exists regarding the Zurich switch cost claim. The report contains two contradictory statements: one rationale entry says the Zurich switch delivers 'lower costs' while another says it involves '£268.94 more per year' in charges. The Pension Switch Charges table [P22] shows Zurich existing at 0.93% with an impact of -0.49% (a saving), which at ~£103,439 implies a saving of approximately £477/year — not a cost of £268.94. The £268.94 figure at 0.26% appears to relate to Standard Life (0.18% existing, +0.26% impact = new is more expensive) rather than Zurich. This means the charges table [P22] actually shows the Standard Life switch incurs higher costs (not lower) while the Zurich switch saves money — the opposite of how the report presents the Zurich position. The Standard Life switch being more expensive by ~0.26% (approximately £268.94 at Standard Life's fund value of ~£3,002 does not work — at 0.26% on £3,002 that would be ~£7.80, not £268.94; the £268.94 at 0.26% implies ~£103,439 which is Zurich's value, confirming the charge figures in the table are misattributed or misread in the report). The Defaqto report showing the new solution ranks below the existing solution on projected maturity value is noted but does not by itself contradict the rationale as consolidation, risk-alignment, flexibility and adviser servicing benefits are also cited. No safeguarded benefits are lost: [P3] explicitly confirms Zurich has none; [P8] confirms Aviva has none; [P14]/[P10] confirm Scottish Widows has none; [P9] references Standard Life with no guaranteed rates mentioned as applicable. The vulnerability overlay: no vulnerability characteristics are identified in the evidence that would materially alter this assessment. Overall, the primary concern is the contradictory cost presentation for the Zurich switch (simultaneously described as saving money and costing more), with the charges table evidence suggesting the £268.94 figure is misattributed to Zurich when it appears to relate to a different plan.

  [G9.10] Material replacement-related information omitted from the recommendation
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report asserts: (1) employer contributions will be lost on transfer of the People's Pension plan (inferred, page 6: 'If you were to transfer away, you would miss out on the valuable benefit of your employer's contributions'); (2) the People's Pension is therefore recommended to be RETAINED (not transferred); (3) no exit penalties apply to any of the five ceding arrangements ('No charges for transfer and/or switching', page 5 for all five plans); (4) featuresOrGuaranteesLost is absent from the extracted canonical model (flagged as NotPresentInDocument); (5) employerContributionsLost is true only for arrangement 0 (People's Pension) and false for the other four.
    File:     People's Pension policy file (P1–P8, P10): shows a continuous stream of both Employee Contributions and Employer Contributions being paid into the plan up to at least June/July 2023, confirming the scheme is (or was recently) live with active employer contributions. Zurich policy file (P11–P13): confirms no exit penalty applies ('There are no deductions taken from the current value if benefits are transferred or taken. The initial set up charges have already been paid'), but also discloses a capital-units exit charge provision ('If this plan has capital units, there will be an exit charge applied if the fund is transferred') and a bid-to-offer spread of 5% on payments, plus an annual management charge of ¾% per year. Standard Life (P9, P16): plan value £3,002.02; no bid-offer spread, 100% allocation, no platform charge; no exit penalty indicated. Scottish Widows (P15): fund value £211.32; charge comparison table shown. Pension switch report (P22): comparison table covers only the Zurich plan (existing pension £110,185.60 vs new solution); shows projected values at all three growth rates, reduction in yield, critical yield and effect of switching for the Zurich plan only. Policy research file (P20): MVR 'No' for all plans listed; confirms overall strategy is consolidation. The Zurich policy document (P13) records 'Transfer value and early retirement charge: There are no deductions taken from the current value if benefits are transferred or taken.' However, P11 simultaneously states a capital-units exit charge exists if the fund is transferred and is capped at 1% from age 55. No provider document in the pack explicitly confirms that the client's Zurich plan holds NO capital units; the report states no exit penalty without qualification.
    Discrepancies:
      - 1. The report states no exit penalties for all five plans including Zurich (page 5). The Zurich policy document (P11) discloses a conditional capital-units exit charge ('If this plan has capital units, there will be an exit charge applied if the fund is transferred'), but also separately confirms no deduction from current value on transfer (P13). The pack does not confirm whether the Zurich plan holds capital units, leaving an unresolved ambiguity the report does not address.
      - 2. The report's featuresOrGuaranteesLost path is flagged NotPresentInDocument — no explicit disclosure of features or guarantees lost on the four plans that ARE being transferred (Zurich, Scottish Widows, Standard Life, Aviva existing) appears in the canonical model beyond the employer-contribution warning for People's Pension.
      - 3. The Zurich policy document (P12) discloses a 5% bid-to-offer spread on payments and a ¾% annual management charge structure. The pension switch comparison (P22) captures charges in reduction-in-yield and critical-yield terms for the Zurich plan, but the bid-to-offer spread at point of transfer is not addressed in the report's switch cost disclosure.
      - 4. The pension switch report (P22) covers only one plan (Zurich, £110,185.60). No plan-specific cost/benefit comparison table is evidenced in the pack for Scottish Widows (£211.32), Standard Life (£3,002), or Aviva existing pension (£3,744.36). These are smaller plans but the absence of any comparison for them is a gap.
      - 5. The report states employer contributions will be lost on People's Pension transfer and recommends retaining it. The People's Pension file (P1–P8, P10) confirms employer contributions are active. However, the report does not quantify the employer contribution rate or amount being preserved, nor confirm the scheme is still active at advice date — only historical transactions to mid-2023 are evidenced.
      - 6. The canonical model records employerContributionsLost as false for the Zurich plan (arrangement 4 being the main transfer). The Zurich policy file (P11) confirms employer payments to the plan total £0.00, consistent with this. No discrepancy on this point.
      - 7. An internal inconsistency in the extraction notes the total of individual plan values (£116,998.47) does not reconcile with the transfer value used in the switch comparison (£110,185). The pension switch report (P22) uses £110,185.60 as the existing pension value, suggesting only the Zurich plan is included, not all five plans combined. The report does not explain this.
    The report correctly identifies and addresses the most material replacement issue: active employer contributions into the People's Pension, which the file corroborates (P1–P8, P10 show a continuous stream of employer contributions). The recommendation to retain that plan is appropriate and evidenced. For the primary transfer (Zurich, ~£103,439), the pension switch report (P22) provides a full comparison including projected values at three growth rates, reduction in yield, critical yield and effect of switching — this is adequate plan-specific analysis for the largest plan. The Zurich policy file (P13) confirms no exit penalty on transfer ('There are no deductions taken from the current value if benefits are transferred or taken'), which corroborates the report's nil-penalty statement. However, P11 simultaneously discloses a conditional capital-units exit charge and a 5% bid-to-offer spread. The bid-to-offer spread applies to payments, not transfers per se, but combined with the capital-units ambiguity, the report's unqualified 'no charges for transfer' statement for Zurich is not fully corroborated. The guard 'A nil exit penalty confirmed by the provider and left out of the report is not a finding' applies only where the provider confirms nil — here the provider document contains both a confirmation of no deduction and a disclosure of a conditional charge, so the guard does not fully discharge the concern. For the three smaller plans (Scottish Widows £211, Standard Life £3,002, Aviva £3,744), no plan-specific cost/benefit analysis is evidenced in the pack. These are individually small, but collectively £6,957.68, and the absence of any comparison is a gap. The featuresOrGuaranteesLost path being NotPresentInDocument means the report does not address what (if any) valuable features are lost on the four transferred plans. No vulnerability factors are identified in the evidence as materially affecting this assessment. Overall: the main Zurich analysis is present; the employer contribution issue is handled correctly; but the conditional Zurich exit charge/capital units ambiguity is unresolved, the bid-to-offer spread is not disclosed as a switching cost, and the three smaller plans have no plan-specific comparison. These are moderate concerns rather than highest-severity, as the Zurich plan has a comparison, employer contributions are addressed, and the smaller plans are of low individual value.

Outcome: Potential Concern

  No trigger was recorded; this check applies to every case and was assessed anyway. Potential Concern across 5 requirement(s). 4 raised a concern (G10.1, G10.2, G10.3, G10.4). The risk profile questionnaire records 'No understanding / knowledge' for stocks and shares, EIS/VCTs, and pension planning — this is a potential low-capability indicator under FG21/1 that is not reflected in the suitability report's reasoning or in any recorded support/communication adjustment. No vulnerability assessment section or checklist appears anywhere in the case file or the suitability report, meaning there is no affirmative record that vulnerability was considered and found absent — the file is simply silent. The suitability report records no support needs path at all (/vulnerability/perClient[]/supportNeeds[] is absent from the extraction), so it is unclear whether the report considered and dismissed support needs or never addressed them.

The financial planning documents (P4, P5, P8, P9, P16, P19, P21, P23) contain no vulnerability-related content. File notes (P22) contain no vulnerability references.
    Discrepancies:
      - The suitability report (via the canonical model) contains no explicit vulnerability assessment section and no record that FG21/1 drivers were considered, yet the risk profile (P1) records that John Sullivan has 'No understanding / knowledge' of stocks and shares, EIS/VCTs, and pension planning — a low financial capability indicator under FG21/1 that is not reflected in the report's reasoning or adjustments.
      - The risk profile (P1) records no pension investment experience in the last 5 years, reinforcing low capability, but the suitability report describes the client as having 'some experience in investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters' in the Balanced investor profile text (P14) — this characterisation conflicts with the questionnaire responses showing no understanding or knowledge.
      - The suitability report recommends Power of Attorney as a prudent step (P20) but the fact find contains no record of Power of Attorney, trusted contact, or third-party authority arrangements, and there is no evidence the vulnerability/capacity dimension that motivates this recommendation was formally assessed.
      - No vulnerability assessment or FG21/1 screening record appears anywhere in the case file — neither a structured assessment tool output, nor adviser notes, nor fact find fields recording vulnerability consideration.
      - The risk profile discussion sections for investor experience, capacity for loss, and agreed risk level all record 'No additional notes were recorded' (P2, P11), indicating no documented discussion of capability limitations despite the questionnaire showing no knowledge across all investment categories.
      - The `/vulnerability/perClient[]/impactOnRecommendation[]` path is absent from the canonical model, meaning there is no record in the report of how any vulnerability characteristic (including low financial capability) was considered in forming the recommendation.
    The check requires that wherever vulnerability was assessed and characteristics found, these are reflected in the report's description of support needs, communication and adjustments, and the recommendation accounts for them. Where no assessment occurred, that itself is the finding in a case carrying FG21/1 drivers.
    
    The key FG21/1 driver here is low financial capability. The risk profile questionnaire (P1) records that John Sullivan has 'No understanding / knowledge' of stocks and shares, EIS/VCTs, and pension planning, and has not invested in any of these product types in the last 5 years. This is a clear low-capability indicator under FG21/1 category D. Despite this, the suitability report's description of the client as a Balanced investor who has 'some experience in investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters' (P14) directly conflicts with the questionnaire evidence. This is not a minor inconsistency — it characterises the client's capability in a way that contradicts the documented evidence.
    
    No FG21/1 vulnerability screening or assessment record exists anywhere in the case file. The risk profile discussion notes are all blank ('No additional notes were recorded' — P2, P11). The fact find records no vulnerability indicators and no communication preference beyond 'No Preference' (P13). There is no structured vulnerability assessment output.
    
    The adviser did recommend Power of Attorney (P20), which suggests some awareness of future capacity risk, but this is not grounded in any documented vulnerability assessment and the fact find contains no record of POA arrangements or trusted contacts.
    
    The foreseeable harms recorded in the canonical model are investment risk harms (market risk, underperformance, charges, illiquidity, employer contributions, IHT) — none relate to the client's low financial capability or how the recommendation was adapted to support informed decision-making for a client with limited understanding.
    
    The age guard does not apply here — the concern is not age alone but the documented absence of financial knowledge combined with no vulnerability overlay being applied. The 'genuine absence, assessed and recorded' guard cannot be invoked because there is no record of assessment anywhere in the file.
    
    This is a Moderate-to-High severity finding: the suitability report characterises the client's capability inaccurately relative to the questionnaire evidence, no FG21/1 vulnerability screening is documented, and the low-capability driver is not reflected in any described support, communication adjustment, or suitability reasoning. The recommendation to take on a Risk Rating 5 (higher than questionnaire-indicated 4) for a client with no documented investment knowledge, with no capability-related adjustments recorded, is the primary concern.
